On the free energy of vector spin glasses with non-convex interactions
Hong-Bin Chen, Jean-Christophe Mourrat

Abstract
. The limit free energy of spin-glass models with convex interactions can be represented as a variational problem involving an explicit functional. Models with non-convex interactions are much less well-understood, and simple variational formulas involving the same functional are known to be invalid in general. We show here that a slightly weaker property of the limit free energy does extend to non-convex models. Indeed, under the assumption that the limit free energy exists, we show that this limit can always be represented as a critical value of the said functional. Up to a small perturbation of the parameters defining the model, we also show that any subsequential limit of the law of the overlap matrix is a critical point of this functional. We believe that these results capture the fundamental conclusions of the non-rigorous replica method.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Informal summary of the main results

We fix an integer
D\geqslant 1
, and let
(H_{N}(\sigma))_{\sigma\in(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{D}}
be a centered Gaussian field such that, for every
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{D})
and
\tau=(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{D})\in(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{D}
, we have

(1.1)

\mathbb{E}\left[H_{N}(\sigma)H_{N}(\tau)\right]=N\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\tau^{\intercal}}{N}\right),

where
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{D\times D};\mathbb{R})
is a smooth function admitting an absolutely convergent power-series expansion with
\xi(0)=0
, and where
\sigma\tau^{\intercal}
denotes the matrix of scalar products

(1.2)

\sigma\tau^{\intercal}=(\sigma_{d}\cdot\tau_{d^{\prime}})_{1\leqslant d,d^{\prime}\leqslant D}.

The notation in (
1.2
) is natural if we think of
\sigma
and
\tau
as
D
-by-
N
matrices, and we often identify
(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{D}
with
\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
. The class of functions
\xi
such that there exists a Gaussian random field satisfying (
1.1
), together with an explicit construction of the Gaussian process
H_{N}
, are given in Subsection
1.5
below. We are mostly interested in models for which the function
\xi
is non-convex. A representative example in this class is the bipartite model, which is obtained by choosing
D=2
and
\xi((A_{d,d^{\prime}})_{1\leqslant d,d^{\prime}\leqslant 2})=A_{1,1}A_{2,2}
. This model can be realized explicitly by setting
H_{N}(\sigma)
to be

\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}J_{i,j}\sigma_{1,i}\sigma_{2,j},

where
(J_{i,j})_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant N}
are independent standard Gaussian random variables.

We also fix a probability measure
P_{1}
with compact support, and for each integer
N\geqslant 1
, we denote by
P_{N}=P_{1}^{\otimes N}
the
N
-fold tensor product of
P_{1}
. We think of
P_{N}
as a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}\simeq(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{D}
.
We are interested in understanding, for each fixed
\beta\geqslant 0
, the large-
N
limit of the free energy

\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\left(\beta H_{N}(\sigma)\right)\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma).

For
D=1
,
\xi(r)=r^{2}
, and
P_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{-1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\delta_{1}
, this corresponds to the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model
[
94
]
. In this context, a candidate for the limit free energy was derived in
[
63
,
84
,
85
,
86
,
87
]
using sophisticated non-rigorous arguments within the framework of the replica method. In this approach, a number of formal manipulations are performed in order to be in a position to appeal to the Laplace principle for integrals of exponential functions, say of the form

(1.3)

\frac{1}{N}\log\int\exp(N\mathscr{J}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x.

The actual expression that shows up within the replica method also involves the number of replicas as a parameter. As this number, a positive integer, is sent to zero, unexpected things happen. In particular, the idea that the limit of (
1.3
) ought to be the supremum of
\mathscr{J}
is no longer valid; for the SK model and all other scalar models with Ising spins, the limit becomes the infimum of
\mathscr{J}
instead. For more general models, even this “inverted variational principle” is no longer necessarily valid. To the best of our understanding, in general, the main takeaway of the replica method is that the limit free energy can be represented as a critical value of this function
\mathscr{J}
. By this, we mean that the limit can be represented in the form of
\mathscr{J}(x)
for some
x
such that
\nabla\mathscr{J}(x)=0
. Crucially, one also expects that the critical point itself encodes the asymptotic law of the overlap between two independent replicas sampled according to the corresponding Gibbs measure.

The main results of this paper are rigorous versions of statements of this form, for general models and for a suitable choice of
\mathscr{J}
. Precisely, under the assumption that the free energy converges to some limit, we show that this limit must be a critical value of some explicit functional. Whether or not the free energy converges, we also show that, up to a small perturbation of the energy function
H_{N}
, any subsequential limit of the law of the overlap matrix is described by a critical point of the same functional.

A related route towards obtaining a fully unambiguous characterization of the limit free energy was explored in
[
68
,
71
]
; see also
[
37
]
. The idea there is to consider an enriched version of the free energy that depends on more parameters, and aim to show that this enriched free energy is the solution to an explicit Hamilton–Jacobi equation.
We will also work with this enriched version of the free energy throughout this paper.

One possible approach to solving Hamilton–Jacobi equations is through the method of characteristics. In our context, the characteristics are straight lines, and formal calculations suggest that the gradient of the free energy ought to be constant along those lines. Using the equation then yields a prescription for the value of the solution itself along each of these lines. As long as the characteristic lines do not cross each other, one can show that defining a function according to this prescription yields a valid solution to the equation (see for instance
[
37
, Section 3.5]
for more precision). In this perspective, our main result can be rephrased as saying that in fact, the limit free energy can
always
be represented as the value prescribed by one of these characteristic lines. The only remaining source of ambiguity is that for large times, or in other words at low temperature, there may be multiple characteristic lines reaching a point, and we only assert that one of these characteristic lines prescribes the correct value.

1.2.
Precise statement of the main results

Let
S^{D}
be the space of
D\times D
real symmetric matrices, and
S^{D}_{+}
be the subset of positive semi-definite matrices.
Let

(1.4)

\mathcal{Q}:=\left\{q:[0,1)\to S^{D}_{+}\ :\ q\text{ is right-continuous with left limits, and is increasing}\right\},

where “
q
is increasing” means that, for every
u,v\in\left[0,1\right)
,

u\leqslant v\hskip 10.00002pt\implies\hskip 10.00002ptq(u)\leqslant q(v),

and the latter inequality is interpreted as
q(v)-q(u)\in S^{D}_{+}
. For every
r\in[1,\infty]
, we set
\mathcal{Q}_{r}:=\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{r}([0,1];S^{D})
. The enriched free energy will be a function defined over
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{1}
.
Here and throughout, we set
\mathbb{R}_{+}=[0,\infty)
.
When
q\in\mathcal{Q}
is constant, i.e.
q=h
with
h\in S^{D}_{+}
, this enriched free energy is defined as

(1.5)

\overline{F}_{N}(t,h)\\
:=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\left(\sqrt{2t}H_{N}(\sigma)-tN\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)+\sqrt{2h}z\cdot\sigma-h\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\right)\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma),

where
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{D})
is a random element of
(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{D}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
with independent standard Gaussian entries, independent of
(H_{N}(\sigma))_{\sigma\in(\mathbb{R}^{N})^{D}}
, and
\mathbb{E}
denotes the expectation with respect to all randomness. In (
1.5
) and throughout the paper, whenever
a
and
b
are two matrices of the same size, we denote by
a\cdot b=\tr(ab^{\intercal})
the entrywise scalar product, and by
|a|=(a\cdot a)^{1/2}
the associated norm. The compensating term
tN\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)
is equal to half the variance of the Gaussian random variable that immediately precedes it in the expression (
1.5
); and as a consequence, an application of Jensen’s inequality yields that
\overline{F}_{N}
is non-negative. This compensating term is helpful for the analysis, and we expect that it can be removed a posteriori using similar but simpler arguments as those presented here (in the sense that the final results are in terms of the critical points of a functional that has been enriched with a finite number of additional parameters). Here we will content ourselves with the observation that this term is constant for the bipartite model with
\pm 1
spins, or more generally for any model with
\pm 1
spins for which the function
\xi
only depends on the diagonal of its argument, so its presence causes no harm in these cases. (We also explain how this compensating term can be removed in the case when
\xi
is convex in Section
8
.) The definition of
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
for non-constant
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
is more involved, as one needs to replace the “replica-symmetric” random magnetic field
\sqrt{2h}z
appearing in (
1.5
) by a more complicated one that is coupled with a Poisson–Dirichlet cascade; we refer to (
3.7
) and Proposition
3.1
for a precise definition. Even with general
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, the structure of this term remains that of a “one-body” potential, in the sense that it does not feature any interaction between spins indexed by different indices in
\{1,\ldots,N\}
. In particular, for every
N\geqslant 1
, we have that

(1.6)

\psi(q):=\overline{F}_{1}(0,q)=\overline{F}_{N}(0,q).

For every
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
,
p\in L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
, and
t\geqslant 0
, we define

(1.7)

\mathcal{J}_{t,q}(q^{\prime},p):=\psi(q^{\prime})+\left\langle p,q-q^{\prime}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}+t\int_{0}^{1}\xi(p).

In this expression, we use the shorthand notation
\int_{0}^{1}\xi(p)=\int_{0}^{1}\xi(p(u))\,\mathrm{d}u
, and the inner product between
p
and
q-q^{\prime}
is that of the space
L^{2}([0,1],S^{D})
, that is,

\left\langle p,q-q^{\prime}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\int_{0}^{1}p(u)\cdot(q(u)-q^{\prime}(u))\,\mathrm{d}u.

The mapping in (
1.7
) has a close relationship with the Hamilton–Jacobi equation

(1.8)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle{\partial_{t}f-\int_{0}^{1}\xi(\partial_{q}f)=0}&\quad\text{ on }\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2},\\
\displaystyle{f(0,\cdot)=\psi}&\quad\text{ on }\mathcal{Q}_{2}.\end{array}\right.

A first indication of this fact is that, for each fixed
q^{\prime}
and
p
, the mapping
(t,q)\mapsto\mathcal{J}_{t,q}(q^{\prime},p)
is an affine solution to the equation (
1.8
) (with a different initial condition). More importantly, under convexity conditions on
\xi
and
\psi
respectively, the Hopf–Lax and Hopf formulas allow us to write the solution to (
1.8
) evaluated at
(t,q)
as a saddle-point problem for the functional
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
, as was shown in
[
29
]
(see also
[
37
, Section 3.4]
for the case of equations in finite dimension). For these reasons, we call the mapping
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
the
Hamilton–Jacobi functional
. While neither
\xi
nor
\psi
need to be convex nor concave in general, the conjecture of
[
68
,
71
]
(see also
[
37
, Question 6.11]
) is that
\overline{F}_{N}
converges to the solution to (
1.8
). The function
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
will play the role of the function
\mathscr{J}
discussed informally in the previous subsection. We say that the pair
(q^{\prime},p)\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}\times L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
is a
critical point
of the mapping
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
if

(1.9)

q=q^{\prime}-t\nabla\xi(p)

and

(1.10)

p=\partial_{q}\psi(q^{\prime}).

In more explicit notation, these identities state that, for almost every
u\in[0,1)
, we have

q(u)=q^{\prime}(u)-t\nabla\xi(p(u))\hskip 20.00003pt\text{ and }\hskip 20.00003ptp(u)=\partial_{q}\psi(q^{\prime},u),

and
\partial_{q}\psi(q,\cdot)
denotes the Gateaux derivative of
\psi
at
q
, see (
2.2
) for a precise definition.
The condition (
1.9
) is the first-variation relation for the variable
p
in the mapping
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
, while the condition (
1.10
) is the same for the variable
q^{\prime}
(ignoring the constraint
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
). Notice that inserting (
1.10
) into (
1.9
) yields that

(1.11)

q=q^{\prime}-t\nabla\xi(\partial_{q}\psi(q^{\prime})).

At least formally, the characteristic line associated with the equation (
1.8
) and emanating from the point
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
is the trajectory

t^{\prime}\mapsto\big(t^{\prime},q^{\prime}-t^{\prime}\nabla\xi(\partial_{q}\psi(q^{\prime}))\big)

(cf.
[
37
, (3.94)]
).
The relation (
1.11
) can therefore be rephrased as saying that the characteristic line emanating from
q^{\prime}
passes through the point
(t,q)
.

In order to clarify the relationship between the Hamilton–Jacobi functional and the Parisi formula, we first present a characterization of the limit free energy that is valid under the assumption that
\xi
is convex over
S^{D}_{+}
. The limit free energy was first identified for a class of scalar models including the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model in
[
49
,
102
]
. The argument was then extended to general scalar models in
[
76
,
79
,
77
]
, using an alternative approach based on the ultrametricity property of the Gibbs measure. This approach was then extended to models as in (
1.1
) in
[
13
,
80
,
81
,
82
]
, under the assumption that the function
\xi
is convex over
\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
. Here we show that the more general assumption that
\xi
is convex over
S^{D}_{+}
is sufficient for this result to be valid. To generate examples of functions that satisfy the weaker condition but not the stronger one, we note that if
\xi_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}\to\mathbb{R}
is convex over
\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
, then any function of the form

\xi:A=(A_{d,d^{\prime}})_{d,d^{\prime}\leqslant 1}\mapsto\xi_{0}(A)+\sum_{d=1}^{D}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\beta_{d,k}^{2}A_{d,d}^{k},

with
(\beta_{d,k})_{d\leqslant D,k\geqslant 1}
decaying sufficiently rapidly to ensure the absolute summability of the series, is convex over
S^{D}_{+}
, but it is not necessarily convex over
\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
.

Theorem 1.1
.

If
\xi
is a convex function over
S^{D}_{+}
, then for every
t\geqslant 0
and
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, we have

(1.12)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)=\sup_{q^{\prime}\in q+\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\inf_{p\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\mathcal{J}_{t,q}(q^{\prime},p)=f(t,q),

where
f
is the solution to (
1.8
).

The fact that the Parisi formula (
q=0
) can be recast in terms of the Hamilton–Jacobi functional in the case
D=1
is from
[
69
]
, and was extended to arbitrary
q
in
[
72
]
. The fact that the solution to the Hamilton–Jacobi equation (
1.8
) with convex
\xi_{|S^{D}_{+}}
admits a variational representation is from
[
29
]
, and
the inequality

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\geqslant f(t,q)

is shown in
[
68
,
71
]
for arbitrary
\xi
. The converse bound will follow from the results obtained below, which are more precise than necessary in this case.

To clarify further the connection between Theorem
1.1
and the classical expression of the Parisi formula, when
\xi
is convex over
S^{D}_{+}
, we define its convex dual
\xi^{*}
by setting, for every
a\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
,

(1.13)

\xi^{*}(a):=\sup_{b\in S^{D}_{+}}\left\{a\cdot b-\xi(b)\right\}.

As will be shown here, one can rewrite the saddle-point problem in (
1.12
) as

(1.14)

\sup_{q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\left\{\psi(q+q^{\prime})-t\int_{0}^{1}\xi^{*}\left(\frac{q^{\prime}}{t}\right)\right\},

which perhaps most closely resembles the classical finite-dimensional Hopf–Lax formula adapted to the equation in (
1.8
).
For scalar models and
q=0
, it was shown in
[
69
]
that one can recover the classical expression of the Parisi formula from the formula in (
1.12
) via a change of variables.
This change of variables essentially consists in replacing the variable
q^{\prime}
with the variable
p
defined so that
q^{\prime}=t\nabla\xi(p)
, so that (
1.9
) holds; see also
[
37
, Section 6.5]
for more precision.

For possibly non-convex
\xi
, the first identity in (
1.12
) is false in general
[
68
]
. Another plausible candidate saddle-point formula is

(1.15)

\sup_{p\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\inf_{q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\mathcal{J}_{t,q}(q^{\prime},p),

which is the Hopf formula for the solution to (
1.8
) when
\psi
is convex
[
29
]
. A formula of this form arises in the context of certain problems of statistical inference in high dimensions, see
[
10
,
11
,
12
,
30
,
26
,
28
,
54
,
58
,
59
,
60
,
61
,
64
,
66
,
67
,
90
,
91
]
, as well as
[
37
, Chapter 4]
for a presentation following the Hamilton–Jacobi approach. However, it is shown in
[
68
, Section 6]
that the variational formula in (
1.15
) cannot be the limit free energy in general, because the function
\psi
is not convex in general. A similar phenomenon seems to occur for certain problems of statistical inference on sparse graphs, see
[
38
,
36
,
55
]
. More broadly, there is no arrangement of inf’s and sup’s in front of the Hamilton–Jacobi functional that could be equal to the limit free energy in general
[
68
]
. In analogy with the change of variables described below (
1.14
), one might alternatively want to use (
1.9
) and ask whether the limit free energy could be written in the form of the supremum or the infimum over
p\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
of the functional

(1.16)

p\mapsto\psi(q+t\nabla\xi(p))-t\int_{0}^{1}(p\cdot\nabla\xi(p)-\xi(p)),

but these are also invalid candidates in general
[
68
]
.

Our first main result states that, under the assumption that the limit free energy exists, it must be a critical value of the Hamilton–Jacobi functional.

Theorem 1.2

(Critical point representation)
.

Assume that
\overline{F}_{N}
converges to some
f
. Then for every
t\geqslant 0
and
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, there exists
(q^{\prime},p)\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}^{2}
that is a critical point of
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
and is such that

(1.17)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)=\mathcal{J}_{t,q}(q^{\prime},p).

We understand that the assumption of convergence of
\overline{F}_{N}
to
f
is pointwise, that is, for every
(t,q)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, we assume that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)=f(t,q)
. As will be explained in Propositions
3.1
and
3.3
, the function
\overline{F}_{N}
is Lipschitz in its arguments, with a Lipschitz constant that is uniformly bounded in
N
, so the existence of subsequential limits is a consequence of the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem (and pointwise convergence on a dense subset of
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{1}
implies convergence everywhere). The assumption of convergence could be weakened to a local statement around the point
(t,q)
for which we want (
1.17
) to hold, but we prefer to refrain from doing so for clarity. As shown in Proposition
7.3
, one can also represent the limit free energy at
(t,q)
as a critical value of the function in (
1.16
).

In fact, for most choices of
(t,q)
, we can identify an explicit choice for the critical point
(q^{\prime},p)
appearing in Theorem
1.2
, which is expressed in terms of the limit free energy
f
. Readers who are familiar with the method of characteristics (or see
[
37
, Section 3.5]
) may guess our choice of
(q^{\prime},p)
: since the derivative of the solution ought to be constant along the characteristic line, we aim to choose
q^{\prime}
so that
\partial_{q}f(t,q)=\partial_{q}\psi(q^{\prime})
, and thus
p=\partial_{q}\psi(q^{\prime})=\partial_{q}f(t,q)
.

To make this statement precise, we define
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}
to be the set of all
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
such that
q(0)=0
and there exists a constant
c>0
satisfying, for every
u\leqslant v\in[0,1]
,

(1.18)

q(v)-q(u)\geqslant c(v-u)\,\mathrm{Id}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\mathsf{Ellipt}(q(v)-q(u))\leqslant c^{-1},

where
\mathrm{Id}
denotes the identity matrix, and
\mathsf{Ellipt}(a)
denotes the ratio between the largest and the smallest eigenvalues of the matrix
a\in S^{D}_{+}
.
Roughly speaking, the space
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}
is the set of all
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
that are “uniformly increasing” with a bounded ellipticity ratio. Although the space
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
has empty interior as a subspace of
L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
, the space
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}
will functionally play the role of the interior of
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. This space is for instance convenient when exploring the notion of Gateaux differentiability of a function defined on
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
; we refer to Definition
2.2
for the precise notion of Gateaux differentiability we use. We will show below that if
\overline{F}_{N}
converges to some function
f
, then the limit
f
must be Gateaux differentiable almost everywhere in
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. Our usage of the phrase “almost everywhere” requires some explanation here, since the space
\mathcal{Q}_{2}\subseteq L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
is infinite-dimensional. What we mean is that the set of points at which
f
is not Gateaux differentiable is a Gaussian null set (see Definition
2.4
). In particular, if
\overline{F}_{N}
converges to some function
f
, then
f
must be differentiable on a subset of
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})
that is dense in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
.

Theorem 1.3

(Critical point identification)
.

Assume that
\overline{F}_{N}
converges to some limit
f
. For every
t\geqslant 0
and
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}([0,1];S^{D})
, if
f(t,\cdot)
is Gateaux differentiable at
q
, then letting
p=\partial_{q}f(t,q)
and
q^{\prime}=q+t\nabla\xi(p)
, we have that
(q^{\prime},p)\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}^{2}
is a critical point of
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
and is such that (
1.17
) holds.

Up to a small perturbation of the free energy, we can also identify the limit of the law of the overlap matrix in terms of this critical point. We denote by
\langle\cdot\rangle
the Gibbs measure associated with the enriched free energy with parameters
(t,q)
, see (
3.8
) for a precise definition. The canonical random variable under this measure is denoted by
\sigma
, and we denote by
\sigma^{\prime}
an independent copy of
\sigma
under
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle
, also called a replica. The overlap matrix is the random variable
N^{-1}\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}
, taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
.

We now introduce the perturbation to the Hamiltonian that we need to add. We let
(\widehat{H}_{N}(\sigma))_{\sigma\in(\mathbb{R}^{D})^{N}}
be the centered Gaussian field such that, for every
\sigma,\tau\in(\mathbb{R}^{D})^{N}
,

(1.19)

\mathbb{E}\left[\widehat{H}_{N}(\sigma)\widehat{H}_{N}(\tau)\right]=N\left|\frac{\sigma\tau^{\intercal}}{N}\right|^{2}.

This Gaussian field can be constructed explicitly by setting

\widehat{H}_{N}(\sigma):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{d=1}^{D}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}W_{i,j}\sigma_{d,i}\sigma_{d,j},

where
(W_{i,j})
are independent standard Gaussians. We impose this new Gaussian field to be independent of all other sources of randomness. For every
\widehat{t}\geqslant 0
, we define
\widehat{F}_{N}(t,\widehat{t},q)
to be the enriched free energy as discussed earlier and to which we add the following quantity in the exponential:

(1.20)

\sqrt{2\widehat{t}}\,\widehat{H}_{N}(\sigma)-\widehat{t}N\left|\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right|^{2};

see also (
7.13
) for more precision. The corresponding functional
\mathcal{J}
then becomes

(1.21)

\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{t,\widehat{t},q}(q^{\prime},p):=\psi(q^{\prime})+\left\langle p,q-q^{\prime}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}+t\int_{0}^{1}\xi(p)+\widehat{t}\int_{0}^{1}|p|^{2}.

Theorem 1.4

(Identification of the law of the overlap)
.

Assume that
\widehat{F}_{N}
converges to some limit
\widehat{f}
along a subsequence. Suppose also that, for some
t\geqslant 0
,
\widehat{t}>0
, and
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}([0,1];S^{D})
, we have that
\widehat{f}(t,\widehat{t},\cdot)
is Gateaux differentiable at
q
, and that
\widehat{f}(t,\cdot,q)
is differentiable at
\widehat{t}
. Then letting
p=\partial_{q}\widehat{f}(t,\widehat{t},q)
and
q^{\prime}=q+t\nabla\xi(p)+2\widehat{t}p
, we have that
(q^{\prime},p)\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}^{2}
is a critical point of
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{t,\widehat{t},q}
. Moreover, as
N
tends to infinity along the said subsequence, the overlap matrix converges in law under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle
to the random variable
p(U)
, where
U
is a uniform random variable over
[0,1]
.

Notice that in Theorem
1.4
, we do not require that the free energy converges as
N
tends to infinity, as convergence along a subsequence suffices; and we recall that the Lipschitz continuity properties of
\widehat{F}_{N}
guarantee that we can always extract convergent subsequences. If we do make the assumption that
\widehat{F}_{N}
converges along the full sequence, then an application of Theorem
1.3
yields a representation of the free energy itself as

\lim_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)=\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{t,\widehat{t},q}(q^{\prime},p),

for the same critical point
(q^{\prime},p)
of
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{t,\widehat{t},q}
. To be clear, we mention that the statement that
(q^{\prime},p)
is a critical point of
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{t,\widehat{t},q}
means that

q=q^{\prime}-t\nabla\xi(p)-2\widehat{t}p\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptp=\partial_{q}\psi(q^{\prime}).

As for Theorem
1.3
, for each
t\geqslant 0
, almost every choice of
(\widehat{t},q)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})
is a point that satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
1.4
. In particular, the set of such points is dense in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
.

In fact, we will prove a stronger version of Theorem
1.4
in which we identify the limit law of the array of overlaps involving arbitrarily many independent copies of
\sigma
under
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle
.

A natural question is whether results such as Theorem
1.2
characterize the limit free energy
f
uniquely. The next proposition answers this question positively at high temperature.

Proposition 1.5

(Uniqueness of critical point at high temperature)
.

There exists
t_{c}>0
such that for every
t\in[0,t_{c})
and
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, the function
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
has a unique critical point in
\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{2}
.

For
t>0
sufficiently small and
q=0
, Theorem
1.2
is shown in
[
34
]
. Showing Theorem
1.2
at high temperature for non-zero paths
q
is not a straightforward adaptation of the argument in
[
34
]
because the system is no longer replica-symmetric in this case, no matter how small
t
is.

The Hamilton–Jacobi functional may have multiple critical points in general. Interestingly, this fact does not completely rule out the possibility that Theorem
1.2
might fully characterize the limit free energy. Indeed, one can exclude some spurious critical points by exploiting the continuity properties of the free energy, for instance by using the following simple observation.

Proposition 1.6

(Relevant critical points must be stable)
.

For each integer
n\geqslant 1
, let
(t_{n},q_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, and let
(q^{\prime}_{n},p_{n})\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{2}
be a critical point of
\mathcal{J}_{t_{n},q_{n}}
such that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t_{n},q_{n})=\mathcal{J}_{t_{n},q_{n}}(q^{\prime}_{n},p_{n}).

Suppose that
(t_{n},q_{n})
converges towards
(t,q)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. Then the sequence
(q^{\prime}_{n},p_{n})
is precompact in
\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{2}
. Moreover, any subsequential limit
(q^{\prime},p)\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{2}
is a critical point of
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
, and is such that

(1.22)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)=\mathcal{J}_{t,q}(q^{\prime},p).

Proposition
1.6
implies that among all the candidate critical points of
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
, we can exclude those that cannot be represented as limits of critical points at nearby locations. While we will not prove this here, we expect that some spurious critical points can indeed be excluded using this property.

In order to avoid possible confusion, we mention that the notion of stability of critical points implied by Proposition
1.6
is different from another possible notion of stability sometimes evoked by physicists and that has its clearest meaning for convex models. Indeed, since Theorem
1.1
gives a variational formula for the limit free energy in this case, we can deduce constraints on the Hessian of the Hamilton–Jacobi functional at the relevant critical point, at least at a formal level. From the arguments of
[
68
, Section 6]
, we do not expect that one can strengthen Theorem
1.2
and prescribe the “orientation” of the Hessian of the Hamilton–Jacobi functional at a critical point
(q^{\prime},p)
in general. Our understanding is that physicists sometimes proceed by inspecting the orientation of the Hessian of the unique critical point of the high-temperature phase, and by then postulating that the same orientation must be valid at low temperature. While this might work in some cases, we would be surprised if this procedure was valid in general.

We now comment on our assumption of convergence of the free energy in Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
. Even for certain scalar models (with
\xi
that is not convex on
\mathbb{R}
), the only technique we are aware of for showing the convergence of the free energy to some limit consists in obtaining a full characterization of the limit; in other words, it consists in showing that Theorem
1.1
holds. In view of this, we find it more promising to direct further effort towards a full identification of the limit for general models, rather than towards the discovery of an argument that would only guarantee convergence.

1.3.
Related works

We have already mentioned a number of works that are related to the present paper. In particular, we recall that the limit free energy of the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model was determined in
[
49
,
102
,
103
,
104
]
. The proof for general scalar models (
D=1
) was obtained in
[
76
,
77
]
by showing that the Ghirlanda–Guerra identities
[
2
,
45
,
47
]
suffice to ensure the ultrametricity of the Gibbs measure. The version of Theorem
1.1
with
\xi
assumed to be convex over
\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
was obtained in
[
13
,
80
,
81
,
82
]
. The main ingredient of the proof there is that by enforcing ultrametricity of the overlaps associated with a large family of linear combinations of the spins of different types, one can deduce that the overlaps corresponding to these different types synchronize with one another. These properties of ultrametricity and synchronization will also be used crucially in the present work. Related results for spherical models were obtained in
[
31
,
101
]
for scalar models, and in
[
16
,
17
,
57
,
83
]
for models with multiple types of spins.

Connections between mean-field models of statistical mechanics and Hamilton–Jacobi equations have already been noted in
[
23
,
75
]
; we also refer to
[
18
]
for a recent survey on related topics. In the context of spin glasses, these connections were first explored in
[
1
,
14
,
15
,
48
]
, under a replica-symmetric or one-step replica symmetry breaking assumption. The fact that the Parisi formula can be seen as the Hopf–Lax formula of an infinite-dimensional Hamilton–Jacobi equation is from
[
70
,
73
]
; see also
[
37
]
for a pedagogical presentation. The rigorous justification of the fact that this Hopf–Lax formula is indeed the solution to the Hamilton–Jacobi equation is from
[
29
]
. An inequality between the limit free energy and the solution to the Hamilton–Jacobi equation is obtained in
[
68
,
71
]
for general models.

For certain spherical models with multiple types and non-convex
\xi
called “pure models”, the limit free energy was identified explicitly in
[
97
,
99
,
98
,
100
]
, assuming that this limit free energy is well-defined; see also
[
9
]
for the case of the bipartite spherical Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model. A more geometric analysis of the energy landscape is in
[
20
,
53
,
56
,
62
]
; see also
[
6
,
7
,
41
,
95
]
for scalar models.

In the low-temperature regime, the free energy is a good approximation for the maximum of the energy function
\sigma\mapsto H_{N}(\sigma)
over the support of
P_{N}
(see for instance
[
37
, Exercise 6.3]
). We think that it would be interesting to work out what consequences our results entail for the asymptotic behavior of this maximum, in the limit of large system size. For some convex models, a Parisi-like variational formula capturing this was obtained in
[
8
,
32
,
35
]
. Relatedly, there has been much recent progress on the problem of determining whether there exists a polynomial-time algorithm that identifies a configuration
\sigma
in the support of
P_{N}
such that
H_{N}(\sigma)
is close to its maximal value. The answer turns out to depend on the specifics of the model, and relates to the overlap gap property
[
43
]
; see also
[
42
,
44
]
for surveys. In general, one can identify a specific value
\mathsf{ALG}
such that there exists a polynomial-time algorithm that can identify a configuration
\sigma
with
H_{N}(\sigma)\simeq N\mathsf{ALG}
with high probability, but such that any algorithm within a broad class will demonstrably fail to improve upon this limit value
\mathsf{ALG}
, as shown first for scalar models in
[
40
,
50
,
65
,
93
,
96
]
. Remarkably, they have been successfully extended to general, possibly non-convex spherical models with multiple types in
[
51
,
52
]
, even though the asymptotic behavior of the maximum of
H_{N}
is currently not understood at this level of generality.

1.4.
Outline of the paper

In Section
2
, we explore the differentiability properties of Lipschitz functions on
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. In particular, we show that any such function is Gateaux-differentiable outside of a Gaussian null set. In Section
3
, we define the free energy
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
for piecewise-constant paths
q
using discrete Poisson–Dirichlet cascades, and then extend it to any path
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
by continuity. We also show that the sequence
(\overline{F}_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is precompact, and that
\overline{F}_{N}
is locally semi-concave uniformly over
N
. It is convenient to also give a direct definition of the free energy and its associated Gibbs measure for arbitrary paths
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
. For this purpose, we derive a number of preliminary results involving continuous Poisson-Dirichlet cascades in Section
4
, and then give this direct definition of the free energy in Section
5
. In that section, we also study the differentiability properties of
\overline{F}_{N}
and of any subsequential limit with respect to the path
q
. We turn to cavity calculations in Section
6
. Using the ultrametricity and synchronization results from
[
76
]
and
[
80
,
81
,
82
]
, we extend the Aizenman–Simms–Starr scheme
[
3
]
and obtain precise representations of
\overline{F}_{N+1}-\overline{F}_{N}
and of
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}
. Information about
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}
gives us access to the asymptotic law of the overlap matrix. In Section
7
, we combine these results to show Theorem
1.3
and then Theorem
1.2
, as well as some refinements involving overlap arrays. We also show that any subsequential limit
f
of the free energy
\overline{F}_{N}
must be such that, at every point
(t,q)
of Gateaux differentiability of
f
, we have

(1.23)

\partial_{t}f(t,q)=\int_{0}^{1}\xi(\partial_{q}f(t,q,u))\,\mathrm{d}u.

In particular, this shows that the function
f
must satisfy the Hamilton–Jacobi equation (
1.8
) everywhere outside of a Gaussian null set. Finally, Section
8
derives further consequences of our results in the special case when
\xi
is convex over
S^{D}_{+}
. In particular, we prove Theorem
1.1
there.

1.5.
Explicit representation of the Gaussian field
H_{N}

Before closing this introduction, we clarify the class of functions
\xi
such that (
1.1
) can hold for some Gaussian process
H_{N}
, and give an explicit construction of
H_{N}
in this case. By
[
71
, Proposition 6.6]
, the function
\xi
must take the form

(1.24)

\xi(a)=\sum_{p=1}^{+\infty}\mathsf{C}^{(p)}\cdot a^{\otimes p},

where
\mathsf{C}^{(p)}\in S^{{D}^{p}}_{+}
for each
p\geqslant 1
. In (
1.24
), the notation
a^{\otimes p}
stands for the
p
-fold tensor product of the matrix
a\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
, which we interpret as a
D^{p}
-by-
D^{p}
matrix. We recall that we assume that the series in (
1.24
) is absolutely convergent for every
a\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
. The summation in (
1.24
) starts at
p=1
since we assume that
\xi(0)=0
. There is no loss of generality in this assumption, since the general case can be obtained by simply adding a fixed Gaussian random variable to
H_{N}
, and this changes neither the averaged free energy nor the Gibbs measure.

To give an explicit construction of the Hamiltonian
H_{N}
, it suffices to consider the case when only one of the terms in the series in (
1.24
) is non-zero, since the general case can then be obtained by adding independent Gaussian fields corresponding to each term in the series. We therefore fix
p\geqslant 1
, and let

(J^{(\mathbf{d})})_{\mathbf{d}\in\{1,\ldots D\}^{p}}
be a centered Gaussian vector with covariance matrix
\mathsf{C}^{(p)}
. We let
((J^{(\mathbf{d})}_{\mathbf{i}})_{\mathbf{d}\in\{1,\ldots,D\}^{p}})_{\mathbf{i}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}^{p}}
denote
N^{p}
independent copies of the
D^{p}
-dimensional random vector
(J^{(\mathbf{d})})_{\mathbf{d}\in\{1,\ldots D\}^{p}}
. In particular, we have for every
\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,D\}^{p}
and
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}^{p}
that

\mathbb{E}\left[J_{\mathbf{i}}^{(\mathbf{d})}J_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}^{(\mathbf{d}^{\prime})}\right]=\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathbf{i}=\mathbf{i}^{\prime}\}}\,\mathsf{C}^{(p)}_{\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}.

We then set, for every
\sigma=(\sigma_{d,i})_{1\leqslant d\leqslant D,1\leqslant i\leqslant N}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
,

H_{N}(\sigma):=N^{-\frac{p-1}{2}}\sum_{d_{1},\ldots,d_{p}=1}^{D}\ \sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}=1}^{N}J_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}}^{(d_{1},\ldots,d_{p})}\sigma_{d_{1},i_{1}}\cdots\,\sigma_{d_{p},i_{p}}.

This Gaussian process satisfies (
1.1
) for the function
\xi
given by

\xi(a)=\mathsf{C}^{(p)}\cdot a^{\otimes p}.

1.6.
Assumption on the support of
P_{1}

All the results stated above are valid under the assumption that the reference measure
P_{N}=P_{1}^{\otimes N}
is such that
P_{1}
has compact support in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
. For convenience, we assume from now on that

(1.25)

the support of
P_{1}
is contained in the closed unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{D}
.

Up to a rescaling of the variables, this entails no loss in generality.

2.
Differentiability of Lipschitz functions

The main goal of this section is to show that a Lipschitz function on the space of paths
\mathcal{Q}
is differentiable “almost everywhere”. We do not know whether a statement in this spirit is valid if the notion of differentiability is meant in the sense of Fréchet. We will show that it is indeed valid if the notion of differentiability is somewhat weakened, and with “almost everywhere” being understood in the sense that the set of points of non-differentiability is a Gaussian null set.

Throughout this section, we let
E
be a separable Banach space, and let
G
be a subset of
E
. In our application, we will choose
E
to be
L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
and
G=\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. We denote by
|\cdot|_{E}
the norm on the space
E
, by
E^{*}
the (continuous) dual of
E
, and by
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{E}
the canonical pairing of
(E^{*},E)
. We start by giving precise definitions of the notions of differentiability we use.

Definition 2.1

(Fréchet differentiability)
.

A function
g:G\to\mathbb{R}
is
Fréchet differentiable
at
{q}\in G
if there is a unique
y^{*}\in E^{*}
such that

(2.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q^{\prime}}\in G\setminus\{{q}\}\\
|{q^{\prime}}-{q}|_{E}\leqslant r\end{subarray}}\frac{\left|g({q^{\prime}})-g({q})-\left\langle y^{*},{q^{\prime}}-{q}\right\rangle_{E}\right|}{|{q^{\prime}}-{q}|_{E}}=0.

In this case, we call
y^{*}
the
Fréchet derivative
of
g
at
q
.

The condition in (
2.1
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle g({q^{\prime}})-g({q})=\left\langle y^{*},{q^{\prime}}-{q}\right\rangle_{E}+o\left(\left|{q^{\prime}}-{q}\right|_{E}\right)

for
{q^{\prime}}\in G
tending to
{q}
. The notion of Fréchet differentiability is often defined only when
G
is an open set, in which case if there exists
y^{*}\in E^{*}
satisfying (
2.1
), then it is necessarily unique. But we do not require that
G
be open here.

For every
q\in G
, we define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Adm}(G,q):=\left\{e\in E\ \big|\ \exists r>0\,:\,\forall t\in[0,r],\ q+te\in G\right\}

to be the set of admissible directions at
q
along which a small line segment is contained in
G
.

Definition 2.2

(Gateaux differentiability)
.

A function
g:G\to\mathbb{R}
is
Gateaux differentiable
at
q\in G
if the following two conditions hold:

•

for every
e\in\mathrm{Adm}(G,q)
, the following limit exists:

(2.2)

g^{\prime}(q,e):=\lim_{r\searrow 0}\frac{g(q+re)-g(q)}{r};

•

there is a unique
y^{*}\in E^{*}
such that for every
e\in\mathrm{Adm}(G,q)
, we have
g^{\prime}(q,e)=\left\langle y^{*},e\right\rangle_{E}
.

In this case, we call
y^{*}
the
Gateaux derivative
of
g
at
q
.

We stress that our definition of Gateaux differentiability may differ from the most classical one. For one thing, the notion is most commonly used only when
G
is an open set. Most importantly, the notion of Gateaux differentiability is often understood in the sense that only the first of the two properties we listed is valid, while here we require in particular that the mapping
e\mapsto g^{\prime}(q,e)
be linear and continuous. We refer to
[
74
]
for a comparison of the different notions. When
G
is an open set, our definition coincides with those of
[
74
, Definition 4]
,
[
88
, (d) page 528]
, and
[
21
, Definition 4.1]
(we impose
r>0
in (
2.2
) while the limit is two-sided in
[
88
,
21
]
, but these are equivalent since we also have that
g^{\prime}(q,e)=-g^{\prime}(q,-e)
). An example of a Lipschitz function that is nowhere Fréchet differentiable is the
\ell^{1}
norm on the space of absolutely summable sequences; this function is Gateaux differentiable at every nowhere-vanishing sequence.

In the problem we are ultimately interested in, the function defined on
G
admits a Lipschitz extension on the full space
E
. Under the assumption that the norm
|\cdot|_{E}
is Fréchet differentiable away from the origin, it was shown in
[
89
]
that a Lipschitz function on
E
must be Fréchet differentiable on a dense set. However, what we really want is to identify a dense set of points of differentiability
in
G
. Since in our application, the set
G
has empty interior, the existence of a dense set of points of differentiability in
E
does not guarantee the existence of any such point in
G
.

We will therefore rely instead on the fact, proved in
[
5
,
88
]
, that a Lipschitz function on
E
must be Gateaux differentiable outside of a Gaussian null set. As will be explained next, a Gaussian null set is sufficiently “thin” that in our application, we can deduce in particular the existence
in
G
of a dense set of points of Gateaux differentiability.

We now give a precise definition of the notion of a Gaussian null set, and present the relevant results from
[
88
]
. We equip
E
with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra generated by its norm topology.

Definition 2.3
.

A probability measure
\mu
on
E
is said to be a
non-degenerate Gaussian measure
with mean
a\in E
if for every non-zero
e^{*}\in E^{*}
, the measure
\mu\circ(e^{*})^{-1}
is a Gaussian measure with mean
e^{*}(a)
and non-zero variance.

Although we will not make use of this fact, we mention in passing that when
E
is a separable Banach space as we assume here, the mean of a Gaussian measure can always be represented as an element of
E
, by
[
22
, Theorem 3.2.3]
. This is easier to see when
E
a separable Hilbert space (e.g.
[
22
, Theorem 2.3.1]
), or more generally when
E
is reflexive. In our application the space
E
will be a separable Hilbert space.

Definition 2.4
.

A Borel subset
B
of
E
is said to be a
Gaussian null set
if for every non-degenerate Gaussian measure
\mu
on
E
, we have
\mu(B)=0
.

The following lemma is a simple modification of
[
88
, Lemma 3]
; we provide a complete proof for the reader’s convenience.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
(w_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
E
that has dense linear span and satisfies
\lim_{n\to\infty}|w_{n}|_{E}=0
, and let
K
be the closed convex hull of
\{0\}\cup\{w_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
. For every
x\in E
, the set
x+K
is not a Gaussian null set.

Proof.

We denote by
\ell^{2}
the Hilbert space of square-summable sequences indexed by
\mathbb{N}
, and decompose the proof into two steps.

Step 1.
Let
\mu
be a non-degenerate Gaussian measure on
\ell^{2}
with mean
a\in\ell^{2}
. In this step, we show that
\mu
attributes a positive mass to every open set; or equivalently, that the support of
\mu
is the full space
\ell^{2}
. By
[
22
, Theorem 2.3.1]
, there exists a continuous self-adjoint operator
A:\ell^{2}\to\ell^{2}
such that for every
u\in\ell^{2}
, the measure
\mu\circ u^{-1}
is Gaussian with mean
u\cdot a
and variance
u\cdot Au
. In the notation
\mu\circ u^{-1}
, we interpret
u
as the element of the dual of
\ell^{2}
canonically associated with
u
, namely the mapping
x\mapsto u\cdot x
. Since
\mu
is non-degenerate, the kernel of
A
must be trivial. Since
A
is self-adjoint, this implies that the image of
A
is dense in
\ell^{2}
. We also observe that for every
h\in\ell^{2}
, the translation of
\mu
by
Ah
is equivalent to
\mu
, since its Radon–Nikodym derivative with respect to
\mu
is the mapping

x\mapsto\exp\left((x-a)\cdot h-\frac{1}{2}h\cdot Ah\right).

Now suppose that there exists an open set
U\subseteq\ell^{2}
such that
\mu(U)=0
. We deduce that the translation of
U
by any
Ah
,
h\in\ell^{2}
also has zero measure. But since the image of
A
is dense in
\ell^{2}
, we can find a sequence
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(U+Ah_{n})=\ell^{2}.

We have thus reached the absurd conclusion that
\mu(\ell^{2})=0
; so there exists no open set
U\subseteq\ell^{2}
such that
\mu(U)=0
.

Step 2.

We define the linear map
L:\ell^{2}\to E
such that, for every
y=(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ell^{2}
,

\displaystyle Ly:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-n}y_{n}w_{n}.

Let
U
be the unit ball of
\ell^{2}
. Since the set
\{0\}\cup\{w_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{-w_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is compact in
E
, its closed convex hull
G
is also compact (see for instance
[
4
, Theorem 5.35]
).
Since
|y_{n}|\leqslant 1
for every
y\in U
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
LU\subseteq G
. Compactness of
G
implies that
G
is bounded and thus
L
is continuous. We define
T:\ell^{2}\to E
such that, for every
y\in\ell^{2}
,

T(y):=x+\frac{1}{2}Ly+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-n-1}w_{n}=x+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-n}\left(\frac{y_{n}+1}{2}\right)w_{n}.

Since
\frac{y_{n}+1}{2}\in[0,1]
for every
y\in U
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
T(U)\subseteq x+K
. We let
\mu
be a non-degenerate Gaussian measure on
\ell^{2}
. By the result of the previous step, we have that
\mu
attributes a positive mass to
U
. We denote
\nu:=\mu\circ T^{-1}
. Since
T
is affine and continuous, the measure
\nu
is a Gaussian measure on
E
(with a mean in
E
); and since
T(U)\subseteq x+K
, it attributes a positive mass to
x+K
. To see that it is non-degenerate, let
e^{*}\in E^{*}
be non-zero. For every
y\in\ell^{2}
, we have

\left\langle e^{*},T(y)\right\rangle_{E}=\left\langle z,y\right\rangle_{\ell^{2}}+c,

where

z=\left(2^{-n-1}\left\langle e^{*},w_{n}\right\rangle_{E}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptc=\left\langle e^{*},x+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}2^{-n-1}w_{n}\right\rangle_{E}.

Recall that the linear span of
(w_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is dense in
E
. If
z
was zero, then by linearity and continuity of
e^{*}
, we would deduce that
e^{*}
is zero as well, which is excluded. Since
\nu\circ(e^{*})^{-1}
is a translation of the measure
\mu\circ z^{-1}
, and
\mu
is non-degenerate, we conclude that
\nu
is non-degenerate as well. To sum up, we have found a non-degenerate Gaussian measure
\nu
such that
\nu(x+K)>0
, so the set
x+K
is not a Gaussian null set.
∎

The next theorem is a special case of
[
88
, Theorem 6]
. This result is itself based on
[
5
, Theorem II.2.1]
, which shows a similar result using another notion of thin sets now called “Aronszajn null sets”, and the main contribution of
[
88
]
is to show that these sets are Gaussian null sets. We also refer to
[
21
, Theorem 6.42 and Proposition 6.27]
for a recent monograph presentation of this result.

Theorem 2.6

(Differentiability of Lipschitz functions
[
5
,
88
]
)
.

If a function
g:E\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous, then it is Gateaux differentiable outside of a Gaussian null subset of
E
.

We now specialize this result to the sets
E
and
G
of interest to us, namely
E=L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
and
G=\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. We recall that the
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
is the intersection of the set
\mathcal{Q}
defined in (
1.4
) with
L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
. For every
p\in[1,\infty]
, we use the shorthand notation
L^{p}:=L^{p}([0,1];S^{D})
. We also recall that the set
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}
is defined in the sentence containing (
1.18
). We observe that the set
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
is dense in
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. Indeed, denoting by
S^{D}_{++}
the set of positive definite matrices, we have that

\left\{u\mapsto cu\,\mathrm{Id}+\sum_{k=0}^{K}q_{k}\mathbf{1}_{\left[\frac{k}{K+1},\frac{k+1}{K+1}\right)}\mid c>0,\ q_{0},\ldots,q_{K}\in S^{D}_{++}\right\},

is dense in
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
and is a subset of
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
.

Proposition 2.7
.

If
g:\mathcal{Q}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous, then there exists a Gaussian null set
\mathcal{N}
of
{L^{2}}
such that
g
is Gateaux differentiable on
\mathcal{Q}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{N}
. Moreover,
(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})\setminus\mathcal{N}
is dense in
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
.

Similarly, if
g:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous (in its two variables), then there exists a Gaussian null set
\mathcal{N}
of
\mathbb{R}\times{L^{2}}
such that
g
is Gateaux differentiable jointly in its two variables on
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2})\setminus\mathcal{N}
. Moreover,
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}))\setminus\mathcal{N}
is dense in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
.

Proof.

We only prove the first part of the proposition, since the second part is obtained similarly. We decompose the proof into two steps.

Step 1
. We construct a Lipschitz extension
\overline{g}:L^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
to the Lipschitz function
g:\mathcal{Q}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
.

We denote by
P:L^{2}\to\mathcal{Q}_{2}
the orthogonal projection to
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. By definition, for every
q\in L^{2}
, the point
P(q)
is the unique minimizer in
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
of the mapping

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{Q}_{2}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
q^{\prime}&\mapsto&|q-q^{\prime}|_{L^{2}}^{2}.\end{array}\right.

We refer to
[
37
, Exercise 2.16 and solution]
for a justification that the mapping
P
is well-defined, based on the fact that
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
is a closed and convex subset of
L^{2}
. It is also shown there using the convexity of
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
that, for every
q\in L^{2}
and
p\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, we have

(2.3)

\left\langle q-P(q),p-P(q)\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\leqslant 0.

We show that, for every
q,q^{\prime}\in L^{2}
,

(2.4)

|P(q^{\prime})-P(q)|_{L^{2}}\leqslant|q^{\prime}-q|_{L^{2}}.

For every
q,q^{\prime}\in L^{2}
, we use the identity (
2.3
) with
p
substituted with
P(q^{\prime})
, and similarly with
q
and
q^{\prime}
interchanged, to get that

\left\langle q-P(q),P(q^{\prime})-P(q)\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\leqslant 0\leqslant\left\langle q^{\prime}-P(q^{\prime}),P(q^{\prime})-P(q)\right\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Rearranging and using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, this yields that

|P(q^{\prime})-P(q)|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leqslant\left\langle q^{\prime}-q,P(q^{\prime})-P(q)\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\leqslant|q^{\prime}-q|_{L^{2}}\,|P(q^{\prime})-P(q)|_{L^{2}}.

This shows (
2.4
). To construct the extension of
g
to
L^{2}
, we set, for every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
,

\overline{g}(q):=g(P(q)).

Since
P
is the identity on
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, the function
\overline{g}
is indeed an extension of the function
g
. In view of (
2.4
) and of the fact that
g
is Lipschitz, the function
\overline{g}
is also Lipschitz, as desired.

Step 2.

We let
\overline{g}:L^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be a Lipschitz extension of
g
as given by the previous step. By Theorem
2.6
, there exists a Gaussian null set
\mathcal{N}\subseteq{L^{2}}
such that
\overline{g}
is Gateaux differentiable on
{L^{2}}\setminus\mathcal{N}
.
Letting
x\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{N}
, we verify that
g
is Gateaux differentiable at
x
. Let
y^{*}\in(L^{2})^{*}\simeq L^{2}
denote the Gateaux derivative of
\overline{g}
at
x
. The limit
g^{\prime}(x,e)
in (
2.2
) exists for every
e\in\mathrm{Adm}(\mathcal{Q}_{2},x)
, and it is equal to
\left\langle y^{*},e\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
. One can check that the orthogonal of
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
in
L^{2}
is trivial, so the closed linear span of
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
is
L^{2}
. Moreover, we clearly have
\mathcal{Q}_{2}\subseteq\mathrm{Adm}(\mathcal{Q}_{2},x)
, so there can be only one
y^{*}
such that
g^{\prime}(x,e)=\left\langle y^{*},e\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
for every
e\in\mathrm{Adm}(\mathcal{Q}_{2},x)
. This shows that
g
is Gateaux differentiable at
x\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{N}
.

We next show that
(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})\setminus\mathcal{N}
must be dense in
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
.
Notice that the set

W:=\{q\in\mathcal{Q}:\>q(0)=0\ \text{and}\ \forall u\leqslant v\in[0,1),\ q(v)-q(u)\leqslant(v-u)\mathrm{Id}\}

has dense linear span in
{L^{2}}
.
Let
(w_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
W
whose linear span is dense in
{L^{2}}
. We can rescale
w_{n}
to ensure that
|w_{n}|_{L^{2}}\leqslant 1
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}|w_{n}|_{L^{2}}=0
. Let
K
be as given in Lemma
2.5
. We fix any
x\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
and any
\varepsilon>0
. By Lemma
2.5
, we have that
x+\varepsilon K
is not a Gaussian null set, which implies that
(x+\varepsilon K)\not\subseteq\mathcal{N}
. Hence, there is
y\in(x+\varepsilon K)\setminus\mathcal{N}
such that
g
is Gateaux differentiable at
y
.

To complete the argument, we show that
x+\varepsilon K\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
. Let
\kappa\in K
and let
c
be the constant in (
1.18
) for
x
. We write
x^{\prime}:=x+\varepsilon\kappa
and we clearly have
x^{\prime}(0)=0
. Fix any
0\leqslant u\leqslant v\leqslant 1
. We have
x^{\prime}(v)-x^{\prime}(u)\geqslant x(v)-x(u)\geqslant c(v-u)\mathrm{Id}
. Let
\lambda_{\max}
and
\lambda_{\min}
be the largest and the smallest eigenvalue of
x(v)-x(u)
, respectively. Note that
\lambda_{\min}\geqslant c(v-u)
and that the eigenvalues of
\kappa(v)-\kappa(u)
lie in
[0,v-u]
. So, we get

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ellipt}(x^{\prime}(v)-x^{\prime}(u))\leqslant\frac{\lambda_{\max}+\varepsilon(v-u)}{\lambda_{\min}+0}=\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}+\frac{\varepsilon(v-u)}{\lambda_{\min}}\leqslant c^{-1}+\frac{\varepsilon}{c}.

So, we conclude that
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}
, and we clearly also have that
x^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}
. Therefore, we have verified
x+\varepsilon K\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
.

Hence,
y\in(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})\setminus\mathcal{N}
.
Since
|y-x|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, we conclude that
(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})\setminus\mathcal{N}
is dense in
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
and thus also in
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
.
∎

3.
Definition and regularity properties of the free energy

In this section, we define the free energy
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
for arbitrary choices of the path
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
. When the path
q
is constant equal to
h\in S^{D}_{+}
, we have already displayed the definition of the free energy in (
1.5
). Here we will first extend this definition to piecewise-constant
q\in\mathcal{Q}
. One can then verify that the free energy is
L^{1}
-Lipschitz continuous in
q
, and this therefore defines the free energy
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
for arbitrary choices of
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
by density.
Another consequence of this Lipschitz continuity property is that the sequence of functions
(\overline{F}_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is precompact. We also show that the free energy is locally semi-concave. This property will be used later to assert the convergence of the derivatives of
\overline{F}_{N}
at any point of differentiability of the limit.

We start by defining the free energy
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
for piecewise-constant
q\in\mathcal{Q}
. When
q
is constant equal to
h\in S^{D}_{+}
, we had introduced in (
1.5
) the external random magnetic field
\sqrt{2h}z
acting on the spin configuration
\sigma
, where
z
takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
and has independent standard Gaussian entries. We will here replace this random magnetic field by a richer object that, in the language of physics, has a finite number of replica-symmetry breaks. Precisely, for each choice of integer
K\in\mathbb{N}
(the number of replica-symmetry breaks) and parameters
(q_{k})_{0\leqslant k\leqslant K}\subseteq S^{D}_{+}
and
(\zeta_{k})_{1\leqslant k\leqslant K}\subseteq[0,1]
satisfying the constraints

(3.1)

0=q_{-1}\leqslant q_{0}<q_{1}<\cdots<q_{K}

and

(3.2)

0=\zeta_{0}<\zeta_{1}<\cdots<\zeta_{K}<\zeta_{K+1}=1,

we aim to make sense of
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
where
q
is given by

(3.3)

q=\sum_{k=0}^{K}q_{k}\mathbf{1}_{\left[\zeta_{k},\zeta_{k+1}\right)}.

In (
3.1
) and throughout the paper, whenever
a,b\in S^{D}
, we write
a<b
to mean that
b-a\in S^{D}_{+}
and
a\neq b
. The construction involves the notion of Poisson–Dirichlet cascades, which we now introduce briefly. We let
\mathcal{A}
denote the infinite-ary tree of depth
K
, which we encode as

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}:=\mathbb{N}^{0}\cup\mathbb{N}^{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathbb{N}^{K},

with the understanding that
\mathbb{N}^{0}=\{\emptyset\}
. We understand that
\emptyset
is the root of the tree
\mathcal{A}
, and that for each
k\in\{0,\ldots,K-1\}
and
\alpha=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
, the children of
\alpha
are all the nodes

\alpha n:=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k},n)\in\mathbb{N}^{k+1},

where
n
ranges in
\mathbb{N}
. For every
\alpha=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})\in\mathcal{A}
, we denote its depth by
|\alpha|:=k
, and for every
\ell\leqslant k
, we write

\alpha_{|\ell}:=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{\ell})

to denote the ancestor of
\alpha
at depth
\ell
. For any two leaves
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{K}
, we write

(3.5)

\alpha\wedge\beta:=\max\left\{k\leqslant K\mid\alpha_{|k}=\beta_{|k}\right\}

to denote the depth of the most recent common ancestor to
\alpha
and
\beta
. The
Poisson–Dirichlet cascade
(also called Ruelle probability cascade after
[
92
]
) with parameters
(\zeta_{k})_{1\leqslant k\leqslant K}
is a random probability measure on the leaves
\mathbb{N}^{K}
, whose weights we denote by
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
; in particular,
v_{\alpha}\geqslant 0
and
\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}v_{\alpha}=1
. Briefly, this probability measure is constructed as follows (more details can be found in
[
77
, Section 2.3]
or
[
37
, Section 5.6]
). With each non-leaf vertex
\alpha
at depth
k\leqslant K-1
, we attach an independent Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with intensity measure
x^{-1-\zeta_{k+1}}\,\mathrm{d}x
. This Poisson point process can be ordered decreasingly, say
u_{\alpha 1}\geqslant u_{\alpha 2}\geqslant\cdots
, and we attribute the weight
u_{\alpha n}
to the child vertex
\alpha n
. This attributes a weight to each vertex except the root. Finally, for each leaf vertex
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}
, we compute the product of all the weights attached to the nodes from the root to the leaf vertex
\alpha
, excluding the root, and we denote the result by
w_{\alpha}
, that is,
w_{\alpha}:=\prod_{k=1}^{K}u_{\alpha_{|k}}
. The family
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
is then obtained by normalizing the family
(w_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
into a probability measure, that is,
v_{\alpha}:=w_{\alpha}/\sum_{\beta}w_{\beta}
. We construct this object independently of
(H_{N}(\sigma))_{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}}
.

In order to define the random magnetic field, we also give ourselves
(z_{\beta})_{\beta\in\mathcal{A}}
a collection of independent standard
\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
-valued Gaussian vectors, independent of the Gaussian field
(H_{N}(\sigma))
and the cascade
(v_{\alpha})
.
For every
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}
, we set

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}^{q}(\alpha):=\sum_{k=0}^{K}(q_{k}-q_{k-1})^{1/2}z_{\alpha_{|k}}.

The random variable
\mathsf{w}^{q}(\alpha)
takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
.
For every
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{K}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathsf{w}^{q}(\alpha)\mathsf{w}^{q}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\intercal}=Nq_{\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}}.

Recalling the definition of the piecewise-constant path
q
in (
3.3
), we define

(3.7)

\overline{F}_{N}(t,q):=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\int\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\exp\left(H_{N}^{\mathrm{disc}}(t,q,\sigma,\alpha)\right)\,v_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma),

where

H_{N}^{\mathrm{disc}}(t,q,\sigma,\alpha):=\sqrt{2t}H_{N}(\sigma)-tN\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)+\sqrt{2}\mathsf{w}^{q}\cdot\sigma-\sigma\cdot q_{K}\sigma.

We denote by
\langle\cdot\rangle
the associated Gibbs measure, and by
(\sigma,\alpha)
the canonical random variable. That is, for every bounded measurable
f:\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{K}\to\mathbb{R}
,

(3.8)

\left\langle f(\sigma,\alpha)\right\rangle:=\frac{\int\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}f(\sigma,\alpha)\exp\big(H_{N}^{\mathrm{disc}}(t,q,\sigma,\alpha)\big)v_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)}{\int\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\exp\big(H_{N}^{\mathrm{disc}}(t,q,\sigma,\alpha)\big)v_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)}.

We denote by
((\sigma^{\ell},\alpha^{\ell}))_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
independent copies of
(\sigma,\alpha)
under
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle
, also called replicas. We may also write
(\sigma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
,
(\sigma^{\prime\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime})
for independent copies of
(\sigma,\alpha)
in calculations in which only two or three such copies suffice. As is classical and will be explained in more details below (or see
[
37
, (6.60)]
), one can show that for every
k\in\{0,\ldots,K\}
,

(3.9)

\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{1}_{\{\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}=k\}}\right\rangle=\zeta_{k+1}-\zeta_{k}.

Using this, one can show the following Lipschitz continuity property of
\overline{F}_{N}
as a function of the path
q
.

Proposition 3.1

(Lipschitz continuity of
\overline{F}_{N}
)
.

For every
t,t^{\prime}\geqslant 0
and every piecewise-constant
{q},{q^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have

(3.10)

\displaystyle\left|\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})-\overline{F}_{N}(t^{\prime},{q^{\prime}})\right|\leqslant\left|{q}-{q^{\prime}}\right|_{L^{1}}+|t-t^{\prime}|\,\sup_{|a|\leqslant 1}|\xi(a)|.

As a consequence, the free energy
\overline{F}_{N}
admits a unique extension to
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{1}
satisfying (
3.10
) for every
t,t^{\prime}\geqslant 0
and
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
.

In (
3.10
) and throughout, for every
p\in[1,\infty]
, we use the notation

|q-q^{\prime}|_{L^{p}}:=\left(\int_{0}^{1}|q-q^{\prime}|^{p}\right)^{1/p},

with the usual understanding as an essential supremum when
p=\infty
. We also often use the shorthand
L^{p}:=L^{p}([0,1];S^{D})
, and we recall that
\mathcal{Q}_{p}:=\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{p}
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

The Lipschitz continuity in
q
is in
[
71
, Proposition 3.1]
; see also
[
37
, Proposition 6.3]
for a proof when
D=1
using similar notation as here. Besides the verification of (
3.9
), the main step of the proof is to show that if we allow for repetitions in the sequence
(q_{k})_{0\leqslant k\leqslant K}
, replacing the strict inequalities “
<
” in (
3.1
) by “
\leqslant
”, and follow along with the exact same construction of
\overline{F}_{N}
, then we end up with the same quantity as if we had removed the repetitions in the sequence
(q_{k})_{0\leqslant k\leqslant K}
(and thereby reduced the value of
K
and removed some “unused”
\zeta_{k}
’s in the representation (
3.3
)) to begin with.

For the Lipschitz continuity in
t
, we use the Gaussian integration by parts in
[
37
, Theorem 4.6]
, to write, for every
t\geqslant 0
and piecewise-constant
q\in\mathcal{Q}
,

(3.11)

\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2t}}H_{N}(\sigma)-N\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)\right\rangle=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right)\right\rangle.

By the assumption (
1.25
) on the support of
P_{1}
and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we have for every
\sigma
in the support of
P_{N}
that

(3.12)

|\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}|^{2}=\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}=|\sigma^{\intercal}\sigma|^{2}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\left(\sum_{d=1}^{D}\sigma_{d,i}\sigma_{d,j}\right)^{2}\leqslant N^{2}.

In particular, we have that

\left|\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\right|\leqslant\sup_{|a|\leqslant 1}|\xi(a)|,

so the proof is complete.
∎

We also recall the following observation justifying the identity in (
1.6
).

Proposition 3.2

(Initial condition)
.

For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, we have

(3.13)

\overline{F}_{N}(0,q)=\overline{F}_{1}(0,q).

Proof.

The proof of this result is based on the fact that we chose
P_{N}
to be a product measure,
P_{N}=P_{1}^{\otimes N}
.
By the Lipschitz property in Proposition
3.1
, it suffices to show the identity (
3.13
) for piecewise-constant
q
. The proof is then classical, for
D=1
it can be found in the first part of
[
37
, Lemma 6.4]
using similar notation as here, and the argument applies verbatim to arbitrary
D
.
∎

Given two metric spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, we say that a sequence
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of functions
g_{n}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
converges
locally uniformly
to some
g:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, if
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
g
uniformly on every metric ball of
\mathcal{X}
.
By combining Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
, we can verify that the sequence
\overline{F}_{N}
is precompact.

Proposition 3.3

(Precompactness of
\left(\overline{F}_{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
)
.

For every
p\in(1,+\infty]
, the sequence
(\overline{F}_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is precompact for the topology of local uniform convergence in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{p}
.

The proof of this proposition relies on the following lemma, which states that for every
r<p\in[1,+\infty]
, the embedding
\mathcal{Q}_{r}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{p}
is compact.

Lemma 3.4

(Compact
\mathcal{Q}_{p}
embeddings)
.

Let
r\in(1,+\infty]
, and let
\left(q^{(n)}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of paths in
\mathcal{Q}
such that

(3.14)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|q^{(n)}\right|_{L^{r}}<+\infty.

There exist a subsequence
\left(q^{(n_{k})}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
from
\left(q^{(n)}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and a path
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{r}
such that for every
p\in[1,+\infty)
with
p<r
, the subsequence
\left(q^{(n_{k})}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges almost everywhere and in
L^{p}
to
q
.

Proof.

Let
M<+\infty
denote the supremum in (
3.14
). Since the path
q^{(n)}
is increasing, we use Jensen’s inequality to write, for every
u<1
,

\left|q^{(n)}(u)\right|\leqslant\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}\left|q^{(n)}(t)\right|\,\mathrm{d}t\leqslant\frac{1}{1-u}\left|q^{(n)}\right|_{L^{1}}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-u}\left|q^{(n)}\right|_{L^{r}}\leqslant\frac{M}{1-u}.

By a diagonal extraction argument, we can therefore extract a subsequence from
(q^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that converges pointwise at every rational number in
[0,1)
; for every
u\in[0,1)\cap\mathbb{Q}
, we denote by
q^{(\infty)}(u)
this limit. For every
u\in[0,1)
, we set

q(u):=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in\mathbb{Q}\\
t\searrow u\end{subarray}}q^{(\infty)}(u).

Since
q^{(\infty)}
is increasing, this limit is well-defined. By construction, the path
q
belongs to
\mathcal{Q}
, and whenever
u<1
is a point of continuity of
q
, we must have that
q^{(n)}(u)
converges to
q(u)
along the subsequence. In particular, Fatou’s lemma ensures that
{q\in\mathcal{Q}_{r}}
. Moreover, by the dominated convergence theorem, the convergence of
q^{(n)}
to
q
along the subsequence is valid in
L^{p}([0,u];S^{D})
for every
u<1
and
p<+\infty
. So it suffices to verify that, for each
p<r
,

(3.15)

\lim_{u\nearrow 1}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{u}^{1}\left|q^{(n)}(t)\right|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t=0.

By Jensen’s inequality, we have

\left(\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}\left|q^{(n)}(t)\right|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/p}\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}\left|q^{(n)}(t)\right|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/r}\leqslant\frac{M}{(1-u)^{1/r}}.

The right-hand side is understood as
M
when
r=+\infty
. The inequality above implies (
3.15
) and therefore completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

We fix
p\in(1,+\infty]
. In view of Propositions
3.1
and
3.2
, it suffices to show that the intersection of any
L^{p}
ball with the set
\mathcal{Q}
is compact in
L^{1}
, by the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem. This follows from Lemma
3.4
.
∎

Proposition
3.3
implies that if there exists a subsequence along which
\overline{F}_{N}
converges pointwise to some limit, then the convergence in fact holds locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{p}
for every
p>1
.

Our next result records a monotonicity property of the mapping
q\mapsto\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
. One way to think of this result is to say that the derivative
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q,\cdot)
always belongs to
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. Since we would like to use common language also concerning possible limits of
f
, which are not necessarily differentiable everywhere, we prefer to phrase it as a monotonicity property per se, using the notion of dual cones. We denote by
\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{*}
the cone dual to
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, which is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{*}:=\left\{\kappa\in{L^{2}}\ \mid\ \forall q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2},\ \left\langle\kappa,q\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\geqslant 0\right\}.

The following result can be found in
[
29
, Lemma 3.4 (2)]
. We also give a proof here for the reader’s convenience.

Lemma 3.5
.

We have

(3.16)

\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{*}=\left\{\kappa\in{L^{2}}\ \mid\ \forall t\in[0,1),\ \int_{t}^{1}\kappa(s)\mathrm{d}s\in S^{D}_{+}\right\}.

Proof.

We start by recalling, e.g. from
[
66
, Lemma 2.2]
, that for every
a\in S^{D}
, we have the equivalence

(3.17)

a\in S^{D}_{+}\hskip 10.00002pt\iff\hskip 10.00002pt\forall b\in S^{D}_{+},\ a\cdot b\geqslant 0.

(In other words, the cone
S^{D}_{+}
is self-dual.)
We denote by
K
the set on the right side of (
3.16
). Let
\kappa\in K
and
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
be a continuously differentiable path, whose derivative we denote by
\dot{q}
. Integrating by parts, we see that

(3.18)

\displaystyle\left\langle\kappa,q\right\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\kappa(u)\cdot q(u)\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=q(0)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\kappa(u)\,\mathrm{d}u+\int_{0}^{1}\kappa(u)\cdot\int_{0}^{u}\dot{q}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=q(0)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\kappa(u)\,\mathrm{d}u+\int_{0}^{1}\dot{q}(t)\cdot\int_{t}^{1}\kappa(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}t.

Using that
q
is increasing and (
3.17
), we see that
\dot{q}
must take values in
S^{D}_{+}
. Since we also have
q(0)\in S^{D}_{+}
, we conclude that
\left\langle\kappa,q\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\geqslant 0
. Arguing by approximation, this result can be extended to every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. We have thus shown that
K\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{*}
.

Conversely, let
\kappa\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{*}
. For every continuous path
\dot{q}\in C([0,1];S^{D}_{+})
, the path defined for every
u\in[0,1]
by

q(u):=\int_{0}^{u}\dot{q}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

belongs to
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, and the notation
\dot{q}
is consistent. We conclude from the calculation in (
3.18
) that

\int_{0}^{1}\dot{q}(t)\cdot\int_{t}^{1}\kappa(u)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}t\geqslant 0.

Using that this property holds for every choice of
\dot{q}\in C([0,1];S^{D}_{+})
, that the mapping
t\mapsto\int_{t}^{1}\kappa(u)\,\mathrm{d}u
is continuous, and the characterization in (
3.17
), we conclude that
\kappa\in K
, thereby finishing the proof.
∎

For every subset
G
of
{L^{2}}
and function
g:G\to\mathbb{R}
, we say that
g
is
\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{*}
-increasing
if for every
q,q^{\prime}\in G
, we have

(3.19)

\displaystyle{q}-{q^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{Q}^{*}_{2}\hskip 10.00002pt\implies\hskip 10.00002ptg({q})\geqslant g({q^{\prime}}).

The following result is a rephrasing of
[
71
, Proposition 3.8]
.

Proposition 3.6
.

For every
t\geqslant 0
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the mapping
\overline{F}_{N}(t,\cdot)
is
\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{*}
-increasing.

It will be very useful for our purposes to notice that the free energy
\overline{F}_{N}
is locally semi-concave as a function of
q
. We first obtain a local semi-concavity property for regularly-spaced piecewise-constant paths, and then also derive a version that concerns sufficiently regular continuous paths. We recall that, for every
a\in S^{D}_{+}
, we denote by
\mathsf{Ellipt}(a)
the ratio between the largest and the smallest eigenvalues of
a
.

Proposition 3.7

(Semi-concavity of
\overline{F}_{N}
for piecewise-constant paths)
.

There exists a constant
C<+\infty
depending only on
D
and
\xi
such that the following holds. Let
(\zeta_{k})_{1\leqslant k\leqslant K}
be as in (
3.2
), let
t,t^{\prime}\geqslant 0
,
0=q_{-1}\leqslant q_{0}<\cdots<q_{K}\in S^{D}_{+}
, and
0=q^{\prime}_{-1}\leqslant q^{\prime}_{0}<\cdots<q^{\prime}_{K}\in S^{D}_{+}
, and set

(3.20)

q:=\sum_{k=0}^{K}q_{k}\mathbf{1}_{\left[\zeta_{k},\zeta_{k+1}\right)}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptq^{\prime}:=\sum_{k=0}^{K}q_{k}^{\prime}\mathbf{1}_{\left[\zeta_{k},\zeta_{k+1}\right)}.

Letting
c\in(0,1]
be such that
t,t^{\prime}\geqslant c
and

(3.21)

\forall k\in\{0,\ldots,K\},\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{c}{K+1}\mathrm{Id}\leqslant q_{k}-q_{k-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\mathsf{Ellipt}(q_{k}-q_{k-1})\leqslant c^{-1},

and similarly with
q
replaced by
q^{\prime}
in (
3.21
), we have, for every
\lambda\in[0,1]
,

(3.22)

(1-\lambda)\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)+\lambda\overline{F}_{N}(t^{\prime},q^{\prime})-\overline{F}_{N}\big((1-\lambda)(t,q)+\lambda(t^{\prime},q^{\prime})\big)\\
\leqslant C\lambda(1-\lambda)c^{-2}\left((t-t^{\prime})^{2}+(K+1)\sum_{k=0}^{K}\left|\left(q_{k}-q_{k-1}\right)-\left(q^{\prime}_{k}-q^{\prime}_{k-1}\right)\right|^{2}\right).

Proof.

We start with a preliminary step.

Step 0
.
Recall that we denote by
S^{D}_{++}
the set of
D
-by-
D
positive definite matrices. For every
h\in S^{D}_{++}
and
a\in S^{D}
, we set

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h}}(a):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-1}\left(\sqrt{h+\varepsilon a}-\sqrt{h}\right),

as well as

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{2}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-1}\left(\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h+\varepsilon a}}(a)-\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)\right).

We denote by
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(h)
the smallest eigenvalue of
h
.
In this step, we show that

(3.23)

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)\right|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}|a|\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(h)^{-\frac{1}{2}}

and

(3.24)

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{D}^{2}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)\right|\leqslant\frac{1}{4}|a|^{2}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(h)^{-\frac{3}{2}}.

For two matrices
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
, we write
b\circledast b^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}(bb^{\prime}+b^{\prime}b)
for the symmetrized matrix multiplication.
Writing
h+\varepsilon a=\left(\sqrt{h}+\varepsilon\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)+o(\varepsilon)\right)^{2}
and expanding the square, we see that

(3.25)

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{h}\circledast\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)=a

Let
\lambda=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{D})
and
o
be an orthogonal matrix such that
h=o\lambda o^{\intercal}
. We have
\sqrt{h}=o\sqrt{\lambda}o^{\intercal}
, where
\sqrt{\lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\dots,\sqrt{\lambda_{D}})
.
Writing
D^{\prime}:=o^{\intercal}\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)o
and
a^{\prime}:=o^{\intercal}ao
, we get from (
3.25
) that for every
d,d^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots D\}
,

D^{\prime}_{d,d^{\prime}}\left(\sqrt{\lambda_{d}}+\sqrt{\lambda_{d^{\prime}}}\right)=a^{\prime}_{d,d^{\prime}}.

This implies that

|\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)|=|D^{\prime}|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}|a^{\prime}|\ {\max_{d\leqslant D}\lambda_{d}^{-1/2}}=\frac{1}{2}|a|\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(h)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

as announced in (
3.23
).

Turning to (
3.24
), we start with (
3.25
) applied to
h+\varepsilon a
:

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{h+\varepsilon a}\circledast\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h+\varepsilon a}}(a)=a.

By the definitions of the derivatives, this means that, as
\varepsilon
tends to zero,

\displaystyle 2\left(\sqrt{h}+\varepsilon\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)+o(\varepsilon)\right)\circledast\left(\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)+\varepsilon\mathsf{D}^{2}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)+o(\varepsilon)\right)=a

Using (
3.25
) to cancel out terms and sending
\varepsilon
to zero, we get

\displaystyle\sqrt{h}\circledast\mathsf{D}^{2}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)=-\left(\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)\right)^{2}.

Using the same diagonalization argument as for (
3.23
) yields that

(3.26)

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{D}^{2}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)\right|\leqslant\left|\left(\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{h}}(a)\right)^{2}\right|\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(h)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Observing also that, for every matrix
b\in S^{D}
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{D}
,

\left|b^{2}\right|^{2}=\sum_{d=1}^{D}\lambda_{d}^{4}\leqslant\left(\max_{d\leqslant D}\lambda_{d}^{2}\right)\,\sum_{d=1}^{D}\lambda_{d}^{2}\leqslant\left(\sum_{d=1}^{D}\lambda_{d}^{2}\right)^{2}=|b|^{4},

we obtain (
3.24
) from (
3.23
) and (
3.26
).

Now that this preliminary step is complete, we turn to the proof of Proposition
3.7
per se, which will be decomposed into two steps. For notational clarity we will only show the semi-concavity property in (
3.22
) in the special case of
t=t^{\prime}
, but it will be clear from the proof that the argument also covers the general case. To lighten notation, we therefore fix
t\geqslant 0
from now on.

Step 1
.
For every
s\geqslant 0
and
y=(y_{0},\ldots,y_{K})\in(S^{D}_{+})^{K+1}
, we define

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{N}^{s,y}(\sigma,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}sH_{N}(\sigma)-s^{2}N\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)+\sqrt{2}\sum_{k=0}^{K}y_{k}{z}_{\alpha_{|k}}\cdot\sigma-\sum_{k=0}^{K}y_{k}^{2}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal},

\displaystyle G_{N}(s,y)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\int\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\exp\left(\widetilde{H}_{N}^{s,y}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\,v_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma).

Notice that, with notation as in (
3.1
)-(
3.3
), we have

(3.27)

\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})=G_{N}(\sqrt{t},(\sqrt{{q}_{k}-{q}_{k-1}})_{0\leqslant k\leqslant K}).

As already mentioned, we will keep
t
fixed in this proof for notational clarity, but the required modifications for the general case should be clear. We therefore fix
s=\sqrt{t}
from now on and suppress it from the notation, simply writing
G_{N}(y)
in place of
G_{N}(\sqrt{t},y)
.

In this step, we show that the Hessian of
G_{N}
is uniformly bounded from above. With the notation
y=(y_{0},\ldots,y_{K})\in(S^{D}_{+})^{K+1}
, we denote by
\partial_{y_{k}}G_{N}(y)
the derivative with respect to the
k
-th coordinate of
y
. This derivative is an element of
S^{D}
