. In this proof, it is at times convenient to think of each coordinate
y_{k}
of
y
simply as a
\frac{D(D+1)}{2}
-dimensional vector, so that we can view
\partial_{y_{k}}\partial_{y_{l}}G_{N}(y)
as a
\frac{D(D+1)}{2}
-by-
\frac{D(D+1)}{2}
matrix, and we can for instance write the Taylor expansion, as
y^{\prime}=(y^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,y_{K}^{\prime})\in(S^{D}_{+})^{K+1}
tends to
y\in(S^{D}_{+})^{K+1}
,

G_{N}(y^{\prime})=G_{N}(y)+\sum_{k=0}^{K}(y_{k}^{\prime}-y_{k})\cdot\partial_{y_{k}}G_{N}(y)\\
+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k,l=0}^{K}(y^{\prime}_{k}-y_{k})\cdot\partial_{y_{k}}\partial_{y_{l}}G_{N}(y)(y^{\prime}_{l}-y_{l})+o(|y-y^{\prime}|^{2}).

Notice that our definitions of scalar products are consistent regardless of whether we regard
y_{k}
as a matrix or as a
\frac{D(D+1)}{2}
-dimensional vector. We also abbreviate the expansion above as

G_{N}(y^{\prime})=G_{N}(y)+(y^{\prime}-y)\cdot\nabla G_{N}(y)+\frac{1}{2}(y^{\prime}-y)\cdot\nabla^{2}G_{N}(y)(y^{\prime}-y)+o(|y-y^{\prime}|^{2}).

With this notation in place, the goal of this step is to show that, for every
k\in\{0,\ldots,K\}
, we have

(3.28)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{y_{k}}G_{N}(y)\right|\leqslant 2|y_{k}|,

and that for every
a\in(S^{D})^{K+1}
,

(3.29)

\displaystyle a\cdot\nabla^{2}G_{N}(y)a\leqslant 2|a|^{2}.

We start with the first-order derivative of
G_{N}
. For every
k\in\{0,\dots,K\}
and
a\in S^{D}_{+}
, using the Gaussian integration by parts in
[
37
, Theorem 4.6]
, we can compute

\displaystyle a\cdot\partial_{y_{k}}G_{N}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}G_{N}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k-1},y_{k}+\varepsilon a,y_{k+1},\dots,y_{K})\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\sqrt{2}az_{\alpha_{|k}}\cdot\sigma-2ay_{k}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\right\rangle=\frac{2}{N}\mathbb{E}\left\langle ay_{k}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}\mathbf{1}_{\{\alpha_{|k}=\alpha^{\prime}_{|k}\}}\right\rangle.

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and (
3.12
), we have

(3.30)

\displaystyle|\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}|^{2}=\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}=\sigma^{\intercal}\sigma\cdot\sigma^{\prime\intercal}\sigma^{\prime}\leqslant|\sigma^{\intercal}\sigma|\,|\sigma^{\prime\intercal}\sigma^{\prime}|\leqslant N^{2}.

Combining the last two displays yields that

\left|a\cdot\partial_{y_{k}}G_{N}(y)\right|\leqslant 2|a|\,|y_{k}|,

which is (
3.28
).

Turning to the proof of (
3.29
), we first observe that, for every
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)a^{2}+\lambda b^{2}=((1-\lambda)a+\lambda b)^{2}+\lambda(1-\lambda)(a-b)^{2}.

Using this and Hölder’s inequality, we obtain that, for every
y,y^{\prime}\in(S^{D})^{K+1}
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)G_{N}(y)+\lambda G_{N}(y^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log(\cdots)^{1-\lambda}(\cdots)^{\lambda}

\displaystyle\leqslant-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\int\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\exp\left((1-\lambda)\widetilde{H}_{N}^{s,y}(\sigma,\alpha)+\lambda\widetilde{H}_{N}^{s,y^{\prime}}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\,v_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\int\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\exp\left(\widetilde{H}_{N}^{s,(1-\lambda)y+\lambda y^{\prime}}(\sigma,\alpha)+r\right)\,v_{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)

where

\displaystyle r:=-\lambda(1-\lambda)\sum_{k=0}^{K}\left(y_{k}-y_{k}^{\prime}\right)^{2}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\geqslant-\lambda(1-\lambda)N\left|y-y^{\prime}\right|^{2},

using also (
3.12
) in the last inequality.
We thus deduce that

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)G_{N}\left(y\right)+\lambda G_{N}\left(y^{\prime}\right)\leqslant G_{N}\left((1-\lambda)y+\lambda y^{\prime}\right)+\lambda(1-\lambda)\left|y-y^{\prime}\right|^{2}.

Substituting
y+\varepsilon a
for
y^{\prime}
in the above display, setting
\lambda
to be
\frac{1}{2}
, and then sending
\varepsilon
to zero, we obtain (
3.29
).

Step 2
.
In this step, we change variables and use the results of the previous step to bound the second derivatives in the
q_{k}
’s of
\overline{F}_{N}
.
For every
x\in(S^{D}_{+})^{K+1}
, we write
\sqrt{x}:=\left(\sqrt{x_{k}}\right)_{1\leqslant k\leqslant K}
and set
\widetilde{G}_{N}(x):=G_{N}\left(\sqrt{x}\right)
. For every
x\in(S^{D}_{++})^{K+1}
and
a\in(S^{D})^{K+1}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\widetilde{G}_{N}(x+\varepsilon a)=\sum_{k=0}^{K}D_{\sqrt{x_{k}+\varepsilon a_{k}}}(a_{k})\cdot\partial_{y_{k}}G_{N}(\sqrt{x+\varepsilon a}),

and thus

\displaystyle a\cdot\nabla^{2}\widetilde{G}_{N}(x)a

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon^{2}}\widetilde{G}_{N}(x+\varepsilon a)\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l=0}^{K}\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{x_{k}}}(a_{k})\cdot\partial_{y_{k}}\partial_{y_{l}}G_{N}\left(\sqrt{x}\right)\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{x_{l}}}(a_{l})+\sum_{k=0}^{K}\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{x_{k}}}^{2}(a_{k})\cdot\partial_{y_{k}}G_{N}\left(\sqrt{x}\right).

Applying the estimates (
3.28
) and (
3.29
), and then (
3.23
) and (
3.24
), we get

\displaystyle a\cdot\nabla^{2}\widetilde{G}_{N}(x)a

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\sum_{k=0}^{K}\left|\mathsf{D}_{\sqrt{x_{k}}}(a_{k})\right|^{2}+2\sum_{k=0}^{K}\left|\mathsf{D}^{2}_{\sqrt{x_{k}}}(a_{k})\right|\left|\sqrt{x_{k}}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{K}|a_{k}|^{2}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(x_{k})^{-1}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{K}|a_{k}|^{2}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(x_{k})^{-\frac{3}{2}}|\sqrt{x_{k}}|.

Notice that

|\sqrt{x_{k}}|^{2}\leqslant D\,\mathsf{Ellipt}(x_{k})\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(x_{k}).

In particular, for every
x\in(S^{D}_{+})^{K+1}
satisfying

(3.31)

\mbox{for every $k\in\{0,\ldots,K\}$, }\ \frac{c}{K+1}\mathrm{Id}\leqslant x_{k}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathsf{Ellipt}(x_{k})\leqslant c^{-1},

we have that, for some constant
C<+\infty
depending only on
D
,

a\cdot\nabla^{2}\widetilde{G}_{N}(x)a\leqslant C(K+1)c^{-2}|a|^{2}.

For every
x,x^{\prime}\in(S^{D}_{+})^{K+1}
with
x
satisfying (
3.31
) and
x^{\prime}
satisfying these same bounds as
x
displayed in (
3.31
), we therefore have that, for every
\lambda\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)\widetilde{G}_{N}(x)+\lambda\widetilde{G}_{N}\left(x^{\prime}\right)\leqslant\widetilde{G}_{N}\left((1-\lambda)x+\lambda x^{\prime}\right)+C\lambda(1-\lambda)c^{-2}(K+1)|x-x^{\prime}|^{2}.

We now recall from (
3.27
) that for
0=q_{-1}<q_{0}<\cdots<q_{K}
, and for
q
as in (
3.20
), we have that
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)=\widetilde{G}_{N}\big((q_{k}-q_{k-1})_{0\leqslant k\leqslant K}\big)
. For
q,q^{\prime}
as in the assumptions of the theorem, a change of variables in the previous display therefore yields the desired result.
∎

We now extend this semi-concavity result to the case of continuous paths. For every
c>0
, we write

(3.32)

\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow,c}:=\big\{{q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}\ \mid\ q(0)=0\ \text{ and }\ \forall u\leqslant v\in[0,1),\quad q(v)-q(u)\geqslant c(v-u)\mathrm{Id}\quad\\
\text{and }\quad\mathsf{Ellipt}(q(v)-q(u))\leqslant c^{-1}\big\}.

We observe that
\bigcup_{c>0}\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow,c}=\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}
, where we recall that
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}
was introduced in the sentence containing (
1.18
). For every path
q\in\mathcal{Q}
, we denote by
\dot{q}
its distributional derivative. Whenever this distributional derivative can be represented as an element of
L^{2}
, we write that
\dot{q}\in L^{2}
.

Proposition 3.8

(semi-concavity of
\overline{F}_{N}
for continuous paths)
.

There exists a constant
C<+\infty
such that, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
c>0
,
t,t^{\prime}\geqslant c
,
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow,c}
with
\dot{q}-\dot{q}^{\prime}\in L^{2}
, and
\lambda\in[0,1]
,

(3.33)

(1-\lambda)\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})+\lambda\overline{F}_{N}(t^{\prime},q^{\prime})-\overline{F}_{N}\big((1-\lambda)(t,q)+\lambda(t^{\prime},q^{\prime})\big)\\
\leqslant C\lambda(1-\lambda)c^{-2}\left((t-t^{\prime})^{2}+\left|\dot{q}-\dot{q}^{\prime}\right|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right).

Proof.

By a density argument and Proposition
3.1
, it suffices to show the result for smooth paths
q,q^{\prime}
. We therefore choose two paths
q,q^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow,c}\cap C^{\infty}([0,1];S^{D})
, and for each
K\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,K\}
, we set

q_{k}:=q\left(\frac{k}{K+1}\right),

as well as the piecewise-constant path
q^{K}\in\mathcal{Q}
defined by

q^{K}:=\sum_{k=0}^{K}q_{k}\mathbf{1}_{\left[\frac{k}{K+1},\frac{k+1}{K+1}\right)}.

We do the same construction for the path
q^{\prime}
, obtaining a family of parameters
(q^{\prime}_{k})_{0\leqslant k\leqslant K}
and a path
q^{\prime K}
. As always, we set
q_{-1}=q^{\prime}_{-1}=0
. An application of Proposition
3.7
yields

(3.34)

(1-\lambda)\overline{F}_{N}\left(t,q^{K}\right)+\lambda\overline{F}_{N}\left(t^{\prime},q^{\prime K}\right)-\overline{F}_{N}\left((1-\lambda)\left(t,q^{K}\right)+\lambda\left(t^{\prime},q^{\prime K}\right)\right)\\
\leqslant C\lambda(1-\lambda)c^{-2}\left((t-t^{\prime})^{2}+(K+1)\sum_{k=0}^{K}\left|\left(q_{k}-q_{k-1}\right)-\left(q^{\prime}_{k}-q^{\prime}_{k-1}\right)\right|^{2}\right).

By Proposition
3.1
, the left side of this inequality converges to the left side of (
3.33
). Under our smoothness assumption on
q
, the difference

\dot{q}\left(\frac{k}{K+1}\right)-\frac{q_{k}-q_{k-1}}{K+1}=\dot{q}\left(\frac{k}{K+1}\right)-\frac{1}{K+1}\left(q\left(\frac{k}{K+1}\right)-q\left(\frac{k-1}{K+1}\right)\right)

converges to zero as
K
tends to infinity, uniformly over
k\in\{0,\ldots,K\}
. Since the same goes for the path
q^{\prime}
, we can replace the second term in the large parentheses on the right side of (
3.34
) by

\frac{1}{K+1}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\left|\dot{q}\left(\frac{k}{K+1}\right)-\dot{q}^{\prime}\left(\frac{k}{K+1}\right)\right|^{2},

up to an error that tends to zero as
K
tends to infinity. This is a Riemann-sum approximation of the integral
|\dot{q}-\dot{q}^{\prime}|_{L^{2}}^{2}
, so under our smoothness assumption on
q
and
q^{\prime}
, it converges to this integral as
K
tends to infinity. Recalling (
3.34
), we thus obtain the desired result.
∎

In short, we think of Proposition
3.8
as stating that the function
q\mapsto\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
is locally semi-concave with respect to the quadratic form
q\mapsto|\dot{q}|_{L^{2}}^{2}
. One of the useful consequences of this fact for our purposes is that if
\overline{F}_{N}
converges to some limit
f
, and if
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}
is a point of differentiability of
f(t,\cdot)
, then the
q
-derivative of
\overline{F}_{N}
at
(t,q)
must converge to the
q
-derivative of
f
at
(t,q)
. We postpone a precise statement to this effect to Section
5
.

4.
Continuous cascades and overlap approximations

Recall that in Section
3
, we defined the free energy
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
for piecewise-constant paths
q
, and then extended this function to all
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
by continuity. In this section, we lay the groundwork that will allow us to give a direct definition of
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
for arbitrary paths
q
. We also show some useful properties of the objects involved, and how to identify them with the discrete objects introduced in Section
3
when the path
q
is piecewise constant. One of the advantages of the direct construction is that it allows us to also directly manipulate the associated Gibbs measure for continuous paths
q
.

The basic object in this construction is a Poisson–Dirichlet cascade whose overlap is uniformly distributed over the interval
[0,1]
.
For the reader’s convenience, we briefly review its construction here. Recall from the previous section that for each choice of the parameters
(\zeta_{k})_{1\leqslant k\leqslant K}
as in (
3.2
), we can build the associated Poisson–Dirichlet cascade
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
, which encodes the weights of a random probability measure on
\mathbb{N}^{K}
. For each realization of
(v_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
, we construct independent random variables
(\alpha^{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
taking values in
\mathbb{N}^{K}
that are sampled according to these weights. Choosing also real numbers
(q_{k})_{0\leqslant k\leqslant K}
satisfying (
3.1
), we then build the “overlap” array
(q_{\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}})_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\geqslant 1}
, where we recall the notation
\wedge
from (
3.5
). We have thus defined a mapping taking any piecewise-constant path
q\in L^{\infty}([0,1];\mathbb{R})
of the form in (
3.3
) as input, and returning a random array indexed by
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
(and in truth it is rather the law of this random array that is unambiguously well-defined). If we take any sequence of piecewise-constant paths
q^{(n)}
that converges in
L^{\infty}([0,1];\mathbb{R})
to the identity map, then the sequence of resulting overlap arrays converges in law in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions, by
[
37
, Corollary 5.32]
. The law of the limit does not depend on the approximating sequence, and we denote it by
R=(R_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}})_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\geqslant 1}
. By the Dovbysh–Sudakov theorem in
[
77
, Theorem 1.7]
, this limit array can be represented as a “true” overlap array. That is, there exists a separable Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
, whose scalar product we denote by
\wedge
, and a random probability measure
\mathfrak{R}
on the unit sphere of
\mathfrak{H}
, such that if
(\alpha^{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
denote independent random variables sampled according to
\mathfrak{R}
, then the overlap array
(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}})_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\geqslant 1}
has the same law as the limit array
R
constructed above. In this statement, the law of
(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}})_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\geqslant 1}
is understood after we average both over the sampling of the random variables
(\alpha^{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
and over the randomness inherent to the random probability measure
\mathfrak{R}
itself.

We denote by
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
the expectation with respect to the sampling of the independent variables
(\alpha^{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
, defined for each fixed realization of
\mathfrak{R}
, and we denote by
(\bm{\Omega},\mathbb{P})
the probability space, with associated expectation
\mathbb{E}
, corresponding to the sampling of
\mathfrak{R}
itself. It will at times be convenient to denote explicitly that
\mathfrak{R}
is a random variable, and we use the notation
\varpi\mapsto\mathfrak{R}_{\varpi}
when we want to clarify that
\mathfrak{R}
is a mapping from
\bm{\Omega}
to the space of probability measures on
\mathfrak{H}
.

By construction, the overlap
\alpha^{1}\wedge\alpha^{2}
is distributed uniformly over
[0,1]
under
\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{R}
. (Strictly speaking, we should rather write
\mathbb{P}\,\mathfrak{R}^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
or
\mathbb{P}\,\mathfrak{R}^{\otimes 2}
in place of
\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{R}
, but we allow ourselves this abuse of notation.) In particular, we have
\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{R}
-almost surely that
\alpha^{1}\wedge\alpha^{2}\in[0,1]
. Equivalently, denoting by
\mathfrak{U}
the (random) support of the measure
\mathfrak{R}
, we have with
\mathbb{P}
-probability one that for every
\alpha^{1},\alpha^{2}\in\mathfrak{U}
,

\alpha^{1}\wedge\alpha^{2}\geqslant 0.

As a direct consequence of the construction of
\mathfrak{R}
from discrete tree structures, we have with
\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{R}
-probability one that

(4.1)

\alpha^{1}\wedge\alpha^{3}\geqslant\min\left(\alpha^{1}\wedge\alpha^{2},\alpha^{2}\wedge\alpha^{3}\right).

Equivalently, this states that
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely, the relation (
4.1
) holds for every
\alpha^{1},\alpha^{2},\alpha^{3}
in
\mathfrak{U}
. Since
\mathfrak{U}
is a subset of the unit sphere of
\mathfrak{H}
, this property states that the set
\mathfrak{U}
is ultrametric. Explicitly, this means that
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely, we have for every
\alpha^{1},\alpha^{2},\alpha^{3}\in\mathfrak{U}
that

\left|\alpha^{1}-\alpha^{3}\right|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leqslant\max\left(\left|\alpha^{1}-\alpha^{2}\right|_{\mathfrak{H}},\left|\alpha^{2}-\alpha^{3}\right|_{\mathfrak{H}}\right),

where
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{H}}
denotes the norm in
\mathfrak{H}
.
The set
\mathfrak{U}
is the continuous analogue of the leaves
\mathbb{N}^{K}
of the tree
\mathcal{A}
that appeared in the construction of the discrete cascades.

To summarize, there exists a measurable subset
\bm{\Omega}^{*}
of
\bm{\Omega}
with
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\Omega}^{*})=1
such that for every
\varpi\in\bm{\Omega}^{*}
, we have that

(4.2)

\begin{cases}\mbox{$\mathfrak{U}_{\varpi}$ is a subset of the unit sphere in $\mathfrak{H}$},\\
\forall\alpha^{1},\alpha^{2}\in\mathfrak{U}_{\varpi},\quad\alpha^{1}\wedge\alpha^{2}\geqslant 0,\\
\forall\alpha^{1},\alpha^{2},\alpha^{3}\in\mathfrak{U}_{\varpi},\quad\alpha^{1}\wedge\alpha^{3}\geqslant\min(\alpha^{1}\wedge\alpha^{2},\alpha^{2}\wedge\alpha^{3}).\end{cases}

Recall that in the construction of the free energy for piecewise-constant paths, we used the random Gaussian field in (
3.6
). Once such a Gaussian field is constructed for
N=1
, it is easy to extend the construction to general
N
by using independent copies. Our next goal is to construct an analogous object in the setting of continuous cascades. We recall that we write
\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}=\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}
. By monotonicity, for every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, the limit

(4.3)

q(1):=\lim_{u\nearrow 1}q(u)\in S^{D}_{+}

is well-defined. We always extend a path
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
by continuity at
1
according to (
4.3
). With this convention, every path
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
is continuous at
1
.

Proposition 4.1

(Construction of Gaussian cascade)
.

We fix
\mathfrak{U}=\mathfrak{U}_{\varpi}
such that (
4.2
) holds, and let
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
. There exists an
\mathbb{R}^{D}
-valued centered Gaussian process
(w^{q}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}
such that for every
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{U}
,

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[w^{q}(\alpha)w^{q}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\intercal}\right]=q(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}).

Moreover, if we display the randomness explicitly by writing
\omega\mapsto w^{q}(\alpha,\omega)
for
\omega
varying in the underlying probability space
\Omega
, then we can construct
w^{q}
in such a way that the mapping
(\alpha,\omega)\mapsto w^{q}(\alpha,\omega)
is jointly measurable.

We understand that the set
\mathfrak{U}
is equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra induced by the norm topology of
\mathfrak{H}
.

Since we aim to prove Proposition
4.1
by appealing to the Kolmogorov extension theorem, we need to construct such a process indexed by any finite subset of
\mathfrak{U}
and verify the consistency condition. When
D=1
, we thus need to show that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha^{1},\ldots,\alpha^{n}\in\mathfrak{U}
, the matrix
(q(\alpha^{i}\wedge\alpha^{j}))_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}
is positive semi-definite. This can be obtained by observing that the matrix
(q(\alpha^{i}\wedge\alpha^{j}))_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}
is ultrametric in the sense of
[
33
, Definition 3.2]
, and by quoting
[
33
, Theorem 3.5]
to conclude.

We can also give a direct proof, which is more in the spirit of the construction of the Gaussian process itself and clearly applies for general
D\in\mathbb{N}
.
As a first step, we show that any finite ultrametric set can be embedded into the leaves of a tree in a natural way. This result is classical but we give a brief proof for the reader’s convenience.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\{\alpha^{1},\dots,\alpha^{n}\}
be an ultrametric subset of the unit sphere in
\mathfrak{H}
. Let
K\geqslant 0
be an integer and let real numbers

\displaystyle s_{0}<s_{1}<\dots<s_{K}=1

be such that
\{s_{k}\}_{k=0}^{K}=\{\alpha^{i}\wedge\alpha^{j}\}_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}
. There exists a rooted tree of depth
K
with leaves
\{\Lambda^{i}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
such that for every
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we have

(4.5)

\displaystyle\alpha^{i}\wedge\alpha^{j}=s_{\Lambda^{i}\wedge\Lambda^{j}},

where
\Lambda^{i}\wedge\Lambda^{j}
denotes the depth of the most recent common ancestor to
\Lambda^{i}
and
\Lambda^{j}
.

Proof.

We recall that for every
i,j,l\in\{1\ldots,n\}
, we have

(4.6)

\alpha^{i}\wedge\alpha^{l}\geqslant\min(\alpha^{i}\wedge\alpha^{j},\alpha^{j}\wedge\alpha^{l}).

For each
k\in\{0,\ldots,K\}
and
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we write

i\sim_{k}j
if and only if
\alpha^{i}\wedge\alpha^{j}\geqslant s_{k}
. By (
4.6
), the relation
\sim_{k}
is an equivalence relation. We define the set of nodes at depth
k
(of the tree we are going to construct) to be the set of equivalence classes of the relation
\sim_{k}
. For
k=K
, the equivalence classes are simply singletons since
s_{K}=1
, and we write
\Lambda^{i}:=\{i\}
. By definition, for each
k\geqslant 1
, an equivalence class for the relation
\sim_{k}
must be a subset of exactly one equivalence class for the relation
\sim_{k-1}
. We draw an edge between the equivalence classes at levels
k
and
k-1
whenever this occurs. This construction results in a graph of depth
K
without loops. At level
0
, there is only one equivalence class since
\alpha^{i}\wedge\alpha^{j}\geqslant s_{0}
for every
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. So our graph is connected, i.e. it is a tree, and we take this unique equivalence class at level
0
as the root of the tree. For every
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
k\in\{0,\ldots,K\}
, if
\alpha^{i}\wedge\alpha^{j}=s_{k}
, then by definition
i
and
j
belong to the same equivalence class for the relation
\sim_{\ell}
if and only if
\ell\leqslant k
. This implies that
\Lambda^{i}\wedge\Lambda^{j}=k
, and thus that the identity (
4.5
) holds.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

We first show the existence of the Gaussian process
(w^{q}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}
. With a view towards applying the Kolmogorov extension theorem, we verify that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha^{1},\ldots,\alpha^{n}\in\mathfrak{U}
, there exists a centered Gaussian vector
(w(\alpha^{i}))_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
with the desired covariance structure. Letting
K\geqslant 0
and
s_{0}<\cdots<s_{K}=1
be such that
\{s_{k}\}_{k=0}^{K}=\{\alpha^{i}\wedge\alpha^{j}\}_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}
, we appeal to Lemma
4.2
to find a tree of depth
K
with leaves
\Lambda^{1},\ldots,\Lambda^{n}
such that (
4.5
) holds. We denote by
\mathscr{A}
the set of vertices of the tree, and let
(g_{\beta})_{\beta\in\mathscr{A}}
consist of independent standard
\mathbb{R}^{D}
-valued Gaussian vectors. For every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
k\in\{0,\ldots,K\}
, we write
\Lambda^{i}_{|k}
to denote the ancestor of the leaf
\Lambda^{i}
at depth
k
. For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we set

\displaystyle w\left(\alpha^{i}\right):=\sum_{k=0}^{K}(q(s_{k})-q(s_{k-1}))^{1/2}g_{\Lambda^{i}_{|k}},

with the understanding that
q(s_{-1})=0
.
Using (
4.5
), we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}w\left(\alpha^{i}\right)w\left(\alpha^{j}\right)^{\intercal}=q\left(s_{\Lambda^{i}\wedge\Lambda^{j}}\right)=q\left(\alpha^{i}\wedge\alpha^{j}\right)\hskip 10.00002pt\forall i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}.

This is the desired form for the covariance. It is straightforward to verify the consistency condition in the Kolmogorov extension theorem. Appealing to this theorem (see e.g.
[
105
, Theorem 2.4.3]
), we thus conclude for the existence of the Gaussian process
(w^{q}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}
.

Next, we verify the measurability. For every
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{U}
, we have

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|w^{q}(\alpha)-w^{q}(\alpha^{\prime})\right|^{2}=\tr\left(q(1)-q(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})\right).

Since moreover,
q
is continuous at
1
due to (
4.3
),
we deduce that
w^{q}
is stochastically continuous; in other words, for every
\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}
and
\varepsilon>0
, we have that
\mathbb{P}\left\{\left|w^{q}(\alpha)-w^{q}(\alpha^{\prime})\right|>\varepsilon\right\}

converges to
0
as
\alpha^{\prime}
approaches
\alpha
.
We can therefore apply
[
46
, Theorem III.3.1]
to find a version of the process that is jointly measurable.
∎

To summarize and introduce more explicit notation, there exists a measurable subset
\bm{\Omega}^{*}
of
\bm{\Omega}
with
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\Omega}^{*})
such that (
4.2
) holds for every
\varpi\in\bm{\Omega}^{*}
. For each such
\varpi
and
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, we can construct a probability space
(\Omega_{\varpi,q},\mathbb{P}_{\varpi,q})
with expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\varpi,q}
, and a centered Gaussian process
(w^{q}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}_{\varpi}}
on
(\Omega_{\varpi,q},\mathbb{P}_{\varpi,q})
, such that for every
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{U}_{\varpi}
, we have

(4.8)

\mathbb{E}_{\varpi,q}\left[w^{q}(\alpha)w^{q}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\intercal}\right]=q(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}),

and the mapping

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{U}_{\varpi}\times\Omega_{\varpi,q}&\to&\mathbb{R}^{D}\\
(\alpha,\omega)&\mapsto&w^{q}(\alpha,\omega)\end{array}\right.

is measurable. The object
w^{q}
will serve as the continuous analogue of the process in (
3.6
).

Notice that we do not assert any measurability property of this Gaussian process with respect to
\varpi
. We do not know of a simple way to improve upon this. Fortunately, as will be explained shortly, this will not be a problem as long as we take
\mathbb{E}_{\varpi,q}
-expectations first, provided that we show that these expectations themselves are measurable with respect to
\varpi
. This is the content of Lemma
4.5
below.

Lest the reader thinks that this is a technical detail that they would rather skip, it is worth noting that the justification of this measurability property will involve ingredients that we need to appeal to later as well. In a nutshell, we will show that the relevant
\mathbb{E}_{\varpi,q}
-expectations can be approximated arbitrarily closely by continuous functions of the overlap arrays. This result will not be of much surprise to specialists, as we will essentially state a general version of
[
77
, Theorem 1.3]
.

Definition 4.3

(Overlap structure)
.

For a measurable subset
S
of a Euclidean space, we say that a pair
(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})
is an
S
-valued overlap structure
if

•

\Gamma
is a probability measure on some Polish space;

•

\mathcal{R}:(\supp\Gamma)\times(\supp\Gamma)\to S
is measurable and satisfies
|\mathcal{R}|\leqslant 1
a.s.

For
d\in\mathbb{N}
and a locally bounded function
C:S\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})
is said to be
C
-admissible
if there exists an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued centered Gaussian process
(z(\rho))_{\rho\in\supp\Gamma}
on some probability space
\Omega_{\Gamma,\mathcal{R}}
such that

•

the covariance is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Omega_{\Gamma,\mathcal{R}}}\left[z(\rho)z(\rho^{\prime})^{\intercal}\right]=C(\mathcal{R}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\supp\Gamma;

•

the process
z
is measurable as a function on
(\supp\Gamma)\times\Omega_{\Gamma,\mathcal{R}}
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
and
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we write

(4.9)

\displaystyle a\approx_{\varepsilon}b\hskip 10.00002pt\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt|a-b|\leqslant\varepsilon.

Proposition 4.4

(Continuity w.r.t. overlap array)
.

We take the setting and notation as in Definition
4.3
. For every Lipschitz
\bm{g}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}
, every bounded measurable
\bm{h}:S^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and every
\varepsilon>0
, there are
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
and bounded continuous functions
F^{i}_{\varepsilon}:(\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})^{n_{i}}\to\mathbb{R}
, with
i\in\{1,2\}
, such that

(4.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Omega_{\Gamma,\mathcal{R}}}\log\int\exp\left(\mathbf{g}\left(z(\rho),C(\mathcal{R}(\rho,\rho))\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\rho)

\displaystyle\approx_{\varepsilon}\left\langle F^{1}_{\varepsilon}\left(C^{\leqslant n_{1}}\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma},

(4.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Omega_{\Gamma,\mathcal{R}}}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n}\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma^{\mathbf{g}}}

\displaystyle\approx_{\varepsilon}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n}\right)F^{2}_{\varepsilon}\left(C^{\leqslant n_{2}}\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma},

uniformly over all
C
-admissible
S
-valued overlap structures
(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})
.
Here,

\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\Gamma}
and
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\Gamma^{\mathbf{g}}}
denote the expectations with respect to
\Gamma^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
and
({\Gamma^{\mathbf{g}}})^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
respectively, with canonical random variable
(\rho^{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
;
\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n}:=\left(\mathcal{R}\left(\rho^{\ell},\rho^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\right)_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}
,
C^{\leqslant n_{i}}=\left(C\left(\mathcal{R}\left(\rho^{\ell},\rho^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\right)\right)_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n_{i}}
; and

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{\mathbf{g}}(\rho):=\frac{\exp\left(\mathbf{g}\left(z(\rho),\mathcal{R}(\rho,\rho)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\rho)}{\int\exp\left(\mathbf{g}\left(z(\rho^{\prime}),\mathcal{R}(\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\Gamma(\rho^{\prime})}.

Proof.

For brevity, we write
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}_{\Omega_{\Gamma,\mathcal{R}}}
. The estimates below are uniform in
(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})
due to
|\mathcal{R}|\leqslant 1
.
For the first approximation (
4.10
), we can use the same argument as in
[
77
, Theorem 1.3]
. We sketch the main steps, omitting some details.
For
a>0
, define
\log_{a}=\max\{-a,\min\{a,\log\}\}
and
\mathbf{g}_{a}
similarly. Since
\mathbf{g}
is Lipschitz, we can use a Gaussian concentration inequality such as
[
37
, Theorem 4.7]
to approximate the left-hand side in (
4.10
) by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\log_{a}\left\langle\exp\left(\mathbf{g}_{a}\left(z(\rho),C(\mathcal{R}(\rho,\rho))\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma}

for large
a
.
Noticing that
\log_{a}
is continuous and that its argument in the expression above remains in a compact set, we can approximate it by polynomials, so that the expression above becomes a linear combination of terms of the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\exp\left(\mathbf{g}_{a}\left(z(\rho),C(\mathcal{R}(\rho,\rho))\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma}^{j}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\prod_{\ell=1}^{j}\exp\left(\mathbf{g}_{a}\left(z(\rho^{\ell}),C(\mathcal{R}(\rho^{\ell},\rho^{\ell}))\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma},

for
j\in\mathbb{N}
. We can move
\mathbb{E}
inside
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\Gamma}
to see that the right-hand side is equal to
\left\langle F^{1}_{\varepsilon,j}\left(C^{\leqslant j}\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma}
for a continuous function
F^{1}_{\varepsilon,j}
satisfying

\displaystyle F^{1}_{\varepsilon,j}\left(C^{\leqslant j}\right)=\mathbb{E}\prod_{\ell=1}^{j}\exp\left(\mathbf{g}_{a}\left(z(\rho^{\ell}),C(\mathcal{R}(\rho^{\ell},\rho^{\ell}))\right)\right).

This is possible since we can write the right-hand side above explicitly as a Gaussian integral.
Collecting
F^{1}_{\varepsilon,j}
back into the linear combination, we obtain
F^{1}_{\varepsilon}
.

The second approximation (
4.11
) is done in a similar way.
Instead of
\log
, we approximate the function
x\mapsto\frac{1}{x}
. The detail can be seen in
[
78
, Section 3.4]
, more precisely, the approximation after
[
78
, (3.25)]
.
One can also see the argument in the second half of
[
77
, Proof (Theorem 4.2) in Section 4.3]
. Again, we only give a sketch. Writing
V(\rho):=\exp\left(\mathbf{g}(z(\rho),C(\mathcal{R}(\rho,\rho)))\right)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n}\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma^{\mathbf{g}}}=\mathbb{E}\frac{\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n}\right)\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}V\left(\rho^{\ell}\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma}}{\left\langle V\left(\rho\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma}^{n}}.

Using the Gaussian concentration, we can approximate
\left\langle V\left(\rho\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma}^{-n}
by a polynomial of
\left\langle V\left(\rho\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma}
. Hence, the right-hand side in the above is approximated by a linear combination of

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n}\right)\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}V\left(\rho^{\ell}\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma}\left\langle V\left(\rho\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma}^{j}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n}\right)\prod_{\ell=1}^{n+j}V\left(\rho^{l}\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma}=\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n}\right)F^{2}_{\varepsilon,j}\left(C^{\leqslant n+j}\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma}

for some
j\in\mathbb{N}
, where the continuous function
F^{2}_{\varepsilon,j}
satisfies

\displaystyle F^{2}_{\varepsilon,j}\left(C^{\leqslant n+j}\right)=\mathbb{E}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n+j}V\left(\rho^{\ell}\right).

Again, collecting
F^{2}_{\varepsilon,j}
back into the linear combination, we obtain
F^{2}_{\varepsilon}
.
∎

We now come back to the setting explained in the paragraph containing (
4.8
), and settle the question of measurability of
\mathbb{E}_{\varpi,q}
-expectations.

Lemma 4.5

(Measurability of expectations)
.

For every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, every Lipschitz
\mathbf{g}:\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
, and every bounded measurable
\mathbf{h}:\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the functions

(4.12)

\displaystyle\varpi

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{{\varpi,q}}\log\int\exp\left(\mathbf{g}\left(w^{q}(\alpha)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}_{\varpi}(\alpha),

(4.13)

\displaystyle\varpi

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{{\varpi,q}}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}_{\varpi}}

are measurable on
\bm{\Omega}^{\star}
, where
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}_{\varpi}}
denotes the expectation with respect to the measure
(\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}_{\varpi})^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
with canonical random variables
(\alpha^{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
, and with

(4.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}_{\varpi}(\alpha):=\frac{\exp\left(\mathbf{g}\left(w^{q}(\alpha)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}_{\varpi}(\alpha)}{\int\exp\left(\mathbf{g}\left(w^{q}(\alpha^{\prime})\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}_{\varpi}(\alpha^{\prime})}.

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

First of all, due to the joint measurability of the Gaussian process, both functions are well-defined. We show that they can be approximated uniformly in
\varpi
by measurable functions.
For the first function (
4.12
), we can apply Proposition
4.4
(with
\Gamma=\mathfrak{R}_{\varpi}
,
R(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})=\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}
, and
C=q
) to get that, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there is an integer
n_{1}
and a continuous function
F_{\varepsilon}^{1}:(\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times{D}})^{n_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
the the function (
4.12
) is approximated by the function

(4.15)

\displaystyle\varpi\mapsto\left\langle F^{1}_{\varepsilon}\left(\left(q\left(\alpha^{l}\wedge\alpha^{l^{\prime}}\right)\right)_{1\leqslant l,l^{\prime}\leqslant n_{1}}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}_{\varpi}}

uniformly in
\varpi
up to an error bounded by
\varepsilon
.
Since (
4.15
) is measurable, we can conclude the measurability of the function (
4.12
).
Similarly, for the second function (
4.13
), we can deduce the measurability using Proposition
4.4
.
∎

Henceforth, we omit
\omega
and
\varpi
for brevity, and simply write
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{E}
to denote the probability and the expectation over both
\bm{\Omega}
and
\Omega_{\varpi,q}
, with the understanding that we always integrate over the space
\Omega_{\varpi,q}
first.

We now show that for piecewise-constant paths, the free energies and Gibbs expectations defined using the discrete construction introduced in the previous section coincide with those defined using the continuous cascade.

Proposition 4.6

(Discrete and continuous constructions are equivalent)
.

Let
K\in\mathbb{N}
, let
(q_{k})_{0\leqslant k\leqslant K}\subseteq S^{D}_{+}
and
(\zeta_{k})_{1\leqslant k\leqslant K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
be such that (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) hold, let
(\nu_{\Lambda})_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
be the associated Poisson–Dirichlet cascade as constructed below (
3.5
), let
q\in\mathcal{Q}
be the piecewise-constant path in (
3.3
), and let
(\mathsf{w}^{q}(\Lambda))_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
and
(w^{q}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}
be as in (
3.6
) (with
N=1
) and (
4.8
) respectively.
For every Lipschitz
\mathbf{g}:\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
and every bounded measurable
\mathbf{h}:\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that

(4.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\log\sum_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\nu_{\Lambda}\exp\left(\mathbf{g}\left(\mathsf{w}^{q}(\Lambda)\right)\right)=\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\left(\mathbf{g}\left(w^{q}(\alpha)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha),

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sum_{\Lambda^{1},\dots,\Lambda^{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\nu^{\mathbf{g}}_{\Lambda^{1}}\cdots\nu^{\mathbf{g}}_{\Lambda^{n}}\mathbf{h}\left(\left(\zeta_{\Lambda^{\ell}\wedge\Lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}}\right)_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(\left(\zeta\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\right)_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}}

where
\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}
is as defined in (
4.14
),
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}}
as in Lemma
4.5
,
and

(4.18)

\displaystyle\nu^{\mathbf{g}}_{\Lambda}:=\frac{\nu_{\Lambda}\exp\left(\mathbf{g}\left(\mathsf{w}^{q}(\Lambda)\right)\right)}{\sum_{\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\nu_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\exp\left(\mathbf{g}\left(\mathsf{w}^{q}(\Lambda^{\prime})\right)\right)},\hskip 20.00003pt\forall\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}.

In (
4.16
) and (
4.17
), the expectations integrate all the randomness. For the expectations on the right side, we first integrate the Gaussian randomness in
(w^{q}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}
and then the randomness of
\mathfrak{R}
, which is justified by Lemma
4.5
.

Proof of Proposition
4.6
.

We start by observing that, for every
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{K}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{w}^{q}(\Lambda)\mathsf{w}^{q}(\Lambda^{\prime})^{\intercal}\right]=q\left(\zeta_{\Lambda\wedge\Lambda^{\prime}}\right),

where we recall that
(q(u))_{u\in[0,1)}
denotes the piecewise-constant path in (
3.3
). In the language of Definition
4.3
, the probability measure with weights
(\nu_{\Lambda})_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
and the field
(\mathsf{w}^{q}(\Lambda))_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
form a
q
-admissible overlap structure, with the mapping
\mathcal{R}
chosen to be
(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})\mapsto\zeta_{\Lambda\wedge\Lambda^{\prime}}
.

By (
4.8
), the measure
\mathfrak{R}
and the Gaussian field
(w^{q}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}
also form a
q
-admissible overlap structure, with
\mathcal{R}
chosen to be
(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\mapsto\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}
. Since
q
is constant on each interval
[\zeta_{k},\zeta_{k+1})
, we can also choose
\mathcal{R}
to be the mapping
(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\mapsto\zeta(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})
, where we write

(4.19)

\zeta:=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\zeta_{k}\mathbf{1}_{\left[\zeta_{k},\zeta_{k+1}\right)}.

Denoting by
(\Lambda^{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\alpha^{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
two families of independent random variables sampled according to
(\nu_{\Lambda})_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
and
\mathfrak{R}
respectively, we see from Proposition
4.4
that in order to prove the result, it suffices to argue that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the overlap arrays
(\zeta_{\Lambda^{\ell}\wedge\Lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}})_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}
and
(\zeta(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}))_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}
have the same law after averaging over all sources of randomness. By
[
77
, Theorem 2.10]
(or
[
37
, Theorem 5.28]
), every discrete Poisson–Dirichlet cascade satisfies the Ghirlanda–Guerra identities, and these identities are preserved whether we look at the overlap as being the mapping
(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})\mapsto\Lambda\wedge\Lambda^{\prime}
or the mapping
(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})\mapsto\zeta_{\Lambda\wedge\Lambda^{\prime}}
. We recall that we built the continuous Poisson–Dirichlet cascade as a limit of discrete Poisson–Dirichlet cascades, using e.g.
[
37
, Corollary 5.32]
. It therefore follows that the continuous Poisson–Dirichlet cascade
\mathfrak{R}
still satisfies the Ghirlanda–Guerra identities, and in particular the overlap array built from the overlap mapping
(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\mapsto\zeta(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})
satisfies the Ghirlanda–Guerra identities. By e.g.
[
37
, Proposition 5.31]
, the laws of the overlap arrays
(\zeta_{\Lambda^{\ell}\wedge\Lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}})_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}
and
(\zeta(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}))_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}
are uniquely determined by the laws of
\zeta_{\Lambda\wedge\Lambda^{\prime}}
and
\zeta(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})
respectively. By
[
77
, Theorem 2.10]
(or
[
37
, Theorem 5.28]
), we have that the probability for
\zeta_{\Lambda\wedge\Lambda^{\prime}}
to be equal to
\zeta_{k}
is
\zeta_{k+1}-\zeta_{k}
, for each
k\in\{0,\ldots,K\}
. By the construction of
\mathfrak{R}
from
[
37
, Corollary 5.32]
, we have that
\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}
is uniformly distributed over
[0,1]
, and in particular, by (
4.19
), the probability for
\zeta(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})
to be equal to
\zeta_{k}
is equal to
\zeta_{k+1}-\zeta_{k}
as well. The proof is therefore complete.
∎

To close this section, we show that the law of a Poisson–Dirichlet cascade under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
remains the same under any probability measure of the form of
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}}
, with
\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}
as in (
4.14
) with
\mathbf{g}
an arbitrary Lipschitz function. In particular, the overlap
\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}}
is uniformly distributed over
[0,1]
, a fact that we will use repeatedly later.

We start by recalling the following invariance property of discrete Poisson–Dirichlet cascades from
[
77
, Theorem 4.4]
.

Lemma 4.7

(Invariance of discrete cascades)
.

Let
K\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{A}
be the rooted tree in (
3.4
), let
(q_{k})_{0\leqslant k\leqslant K}\subseteq S^{D}_{+}
and
(\zeta_{k})_{1\leqslant k\leqslant K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
be such that (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) hold, let
(\nu_{\Lambda})_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
be the associated Poisson–Dirichlet cascade as constructed below (
3.5
), and let
(\mathsf{w}^{q}(\Lambda))_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
be as in (
3.6
).
For every Lipschitz
\mathbf{g}:\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
, there is a random bijection
\pi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
, preserving the parent-child relation, such that

\displaystyle\left(\nu^{\mathbf{g}}_{\pi(\Lambda)}\right)_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}\left(\nu_{\Lambda}\right)_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}

where
\left(\nu^{\mathbf{g}}_{\Lambda}\right)_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
is given in (
4.18
).
In particular, for every bounded measurable function
\mathbf{h}:\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sum_{\Lambda^{1},\dots,\Lambda^{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\nu^{\mathbf{g}}_{\Lambda^{1}}\cdots\nu^{\mathbf{g}}_{\Lambda^{n}}\mathbf{h}\left(\left(\zeta_{\Lambda^{\ell}\wedge\Lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}}\right)_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}\right)=\mathbb{E}\sum_{\Lambda^{1},\dots,\Lambda^{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\nu_{\Lambda^{1}}\cdots\nu_{\Lambda^{n}}\mathbf{h}\left(\left(\zeta_{\Lambda^{\ell}\wedge\Lambda^{\ell^{\prime}}}\right)_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}\right).

We can now prove that this invariance property transfers to continuous cascades.

Proposition 4.8

(Invariance of continuous cascades)
.

For every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, every Lipschitz
\mathbf{g}:\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}
, and every bounded measurable
\mathbf{h}:\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}

where
\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}
is defined in (
4.14
), and we recall that
\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}=\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}^{0}}
.

Proof.

For every
\ell,\ell^{\prime},n\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
Q^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}:=\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}
and
Q^{\leqslant n}:=(Q^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}})_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\mathbf{h}
is bounded and continuous. We fix
\varepsilon>0
. By Proposition
4.4
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
and a bounded continuous function
F_{\varepsilon}
such that

(4.20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(Q^{\leqslant n}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}}\approx_{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(Q^{\leqslant n}\right)F_{\varepsilon}\left(q\left(Q^{\leqslant n}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}},

where
q
acts entrywise. For each
K\in\mathbb{N}
, we write

\zeta^{(K)}:=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{k}{K+1}\mathbf{1}_{\left[\frac{k}{K+1},\frac{k+1}{K+1}\right)},

with the usual understanding that
\zeta^{(K)}(1)=\lim_{u\nearrow 1}\zeta^{(K)}(u)=K/(K+1)
.
In particular, the sequence
(\zeta^{(K)})_{K\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
converges in
L^{1}
to the identity map
x\mapsto x
as
K
tends to infinity. For every
\ell,\ell^{\prime},n\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
Q_{K}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}:=\zeta^{(K)}(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}})
and
Q_{K}^{\leqslant n}:=(Q_{K}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}})_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}
. Since
Q
satisfies the Ghirlanda–Guerra identities by construction of the continuous Poisson–Dirichlet cascade, it is immediate that
Q_{K}
also satisfies the Ghirlanda–Guerra identities. By
[
37
, Corollary 5.32]
, we have that
Q_{K}^{\leqslant n}
converges in law to
Q^{\leqslant n}
. We want to argue that
(Q_{K}^{\leqslant n},q(Q_{K}^{\leqslant n}))
also converges in law to
(Q^{\leqslant n},q(Q^{\leqslant n}))
as
K
tends to infinity. This is not immediate since
q
is not continuous. Notice first that the on-diagonal overlaps are deterministic, and for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

Q_{K}^{\ell,\ell}=\frac{K}{K+1}\xrightarrow{K\to\infty}1=Q^{\ell,\ell}.

Since
q
is continuous at
1
, the question thus only concerns the off-diagonal overlaps. We recall that each of these off-diagonal overlaps is uniformly distributed over
[0,1]
. Since the set of points of discontinuity of
q
is countable, a union bound guarantees that the probability for
Q^{\leqslant n}_{K}
to belong to the set of points of discontinuity of the mapping
q
(as applied entrywise to the array) is zero.
We can therefore apply the continuous mapping theorem (e.g.
[
37
, Exercise A.10 and solution]
) to deduce that indeed
(Q_{K}^{\leqslant n},q(Q_{K}^{\leqslant n}))
converges in law to
(Q^{\leqslant n},q(Q^{\leqslant n}))
as
K
tends to infinity. In particular, for every
K
sufficiently large, we have that

\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(Q^{\leqslant n}\right)F_{\varepsilon}\left(q\left(Q^{\leqslant n}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}\approx_{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(Q_{K}^{\leqslant n}\right)F_{\varepsilon}\left(q\left(Q_{K}^{\leqslant n}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}.

By Proposition
4.6
, the latter quantity can be represented using the discrete Poisson–Dirichlet cascade based on the path
q\circ\zeta^{(K)}
, which is defined on a tree of depth
K
. Denoting the weights of this discrete Poisson–Dirichlet cascade by
(\nu_{\Lambda})_{\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}
, Proposition
4.6
ensures that

\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(Q_{K}^{\leqslant n}\right)F_{\varepsilon}\left(q\left(Q_{K}^{\leqslant n}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{\Lambda^{1},\dots,\Lambda^{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\nu_{\Lambda^{1}}\cdots\nu_{\Lambda^{n}}\mathbf{h}\left(R^{\leqslant n}\right)F_{\varepsilon}\left(q\left(R^{\leqslant n}\right)\right),

where we used the shorthand
R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}:=(\Lambda^{\ell}\wedge\Lambda^{\ell^{\prime}})/(K+1)
and
R^{\leqslant n}:=(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}})_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}
. Another application of Proposition
4.4
, in the same setting of
q
-admissible overlap structures as for (
4.20
), yields that

\mathbb{E}\sum_{\Lambda^{1},\dots,\Lambda^{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\nu_{\Lambda^{1}}\cdots\nu_{\Lambda^{n}}\mathbf{h}\left(R^{\leqslant n}\right)F_{\varepsilon}\left(q\left(R^{\leqslant n}\right)\right)\approx_{\varepsilon}\sum_{\Lambda^{1},\dots,\Lambda^{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\nu^{\mathbf{g}}_{\Lambda^{1}}\cdots\nu^{\mathbf{g}}_{\Lambda^{n}}\mathbf{h}\left(R^{\leqslant n}\right).

By the invariance property from Lemma
4.7
, we have that

\sum_{\Lambda^{1},\dots,\Lambda^{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\nu^{\mathbf{g}}_{\Lambda^{1}}\cdots\nu^{\mathbf{g}}_{\Lambda^{n}}\mathbf{h}\left(R^{\leqslant n}\right)=\sum_{\Lambda^{1},\dots,\Lambda^{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\nu_{\Lambda^{1}}\cdots\nu_{\Lambda^{n}}\mathbf{h}\left(R^{\leqslant n}\right).

To summarize, we have shown that

\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(Q^{\leqslant n}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}}\approx_{3\varepsilon}\sum_{\Lambda^{1},\dots,\Lambda^{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{K}}\nu_{\Lambda^{1}}\cdots\nu_{\Lambda^{n}}\mathbf{h}\left(R^{\leqslant n}\right).

Using this relation also with
\mathbf{g}=0
, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(Q^{\leqslant n}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}^{\mathbf{g}}}\approx_{6\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{h}\left(Q^{\leqslant n}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary, the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 4.9

(Left-continuous paths)
.

All results in this section also hold if, instead of
\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, we take
q
from the set

(4.21)

\displaystyle\Pi:=\left\{q:[0,1]\to S^{D}_{+}\ :\ q\text{ is left-continuous with right limits, and is increasing}\right\}.

The only place where any kind of continuity of a path is needed is in the proof of Proposition
4.1
. There, to show that
w^{q}
is jointly measurable, one requires
q
to be continuous at
1
, as used in (
4.7
). Every path in
\Pi
is also continuous at
1
by definition.

5.
The free energy with continuous cascades

We recall from the beginning of the previous section that we denote by
\mathfrak{R}
a Poisson–Dirichlet cascade such that the law of one overlap is uniformly distributed over
[0,1]
. The object
\mathfrak{R}
is a (random) probability measure on the Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
, and we denote its (random) support by
\mathfrak{U}
. For each realization of
\mathfrak{R}
, we denote by
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
the expectation with respect to the measure
\mathfrak{R}^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
, with canonical random variables
(\alpha^{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
. The expectation
\mathbb{E}
takes the average over the randomness of
\mathfrak{R}
itself. The event in (
4.2
) has
\mathbb{P}
-probability one, and whenever this event is realized and for each
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, there exists an
\mathbb{R}^{D}
-valued Gaussian process
(w^{q}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}
with covariance given by (
4.4
). On this same event, we take
(w^{q}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
to be i.i.d. copies of
w^{q}
and define, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}
,

(5.1)

\displaystyle W^{q}_{N}(\alpha):=(w^{q}_{1}(\alpha),\dots,w^{q}_{N}(\alpha)).

The random variable
W^{q}_{N}(\alpha)
takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
. We can now define, for each
t\geqslant 0
,
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the free energy

(5.2)

\overline{F}_{N}(t,q):=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\iint\exp\left(H_{N}^{t,{q}}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha),

where

(5.3)

H_{N}^{t,{q}}(\sigma,\alpha)=\sqrt{2t}H_{N}(\sigma)-tN\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)+\sqrt{2}W^{q}_{N}(\alpha)\cdot\sigma-{q}(1)\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}.

We also define the associated Gibbs measure

(5.4)

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}\propto\exp\left(H^{t,q}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha),

defined for each fixed realization of
\mathfrak{R}
and of the random Gaussian field
(w^{q}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}
. We use the symbol
\propto
as shorthand for more precise expressions as in (
3.8
). The canonical random variable under
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
is denoted by
(\sigma,\alpha)
. We also write
(\sigma^{\ell},\alpha^{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
to denote independent copies of
(\sigma,\alpha)
under
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
. The expectation
\mathbb{E}
in (
5.2
) integrates all sources of randomness, and we recall that for measurability reasons, we always take the average over the Gaussian process
(W_{N}^{q}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}
first.

In (
5.2
), we allowed ourselves to use the same notation as in (
3.7
). When
q
is piecewise constant, these two quantities coincide, by Proposition
4.6
. This proposition also ensures that Gibbs averages computed under the measures in (
5.4
) and in (
3.8
) coincide.

Recall that we extended the free energy defined in (
3.7
) to all paths
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
using the Lipschitz continuity property from Proposition
3.1
. The next proposition states that the right-hand side of (
5.2
) satisfies the same Lipschitz continuity property, and therefore the two constructions do coincide for all paths and there is no abuse of notation here. This proposition in fact identifies the derivatives of
\overline{F}_{N}
with respect to
t
and
q
precisely.
We set

(5.5)

\displaystyle L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}:=\left\{q\in L^{\infty}:\>|\kappa|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 1\right\}.

Proposition 5.1

(Differentiability of
\overline{F}_{N}
)
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\overline{F}_{N}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}
be the free energy defined in (
5.2
). We have for every
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
that

\left|\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})-\overline{F}_{N}(t^{\prime},{q^{\prime}})\right|\leqslant\left|{q}-{q^{\prime}}\right|_{L^{1}}+|t-t^{\prime}|\,\sup_{|a|\leqslant 1}|\xi(a)|.

In particular, the free energy in (
5.2
) can be extended by continuity to
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, and it coincides everywhere with the free energy defined in (
3.7
) and extended by continuity to
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{1}
in Proposition
3.1
. Moreover, the restriction of the function
\overline{F}_{N}
to
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
is Fréchet (and Gateaux) differentiable everywhere, jointly in its two variables. We denote its Fréchet (and Gateaux) derivative in
q
by
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)=\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q,\cdot)\in L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
. For every
t\geqslant 0
, we have, for every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
,

(5.6)

\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1},\hskip 20.00003pt\left|\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\right|\leqslant\sup_{|a|\leqslant 1}|\xi(a)|,

and, for every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
and
\kappa\in L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
,

(5.7)

\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right\rangle_{N},\hskip 20.00003pt\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right)\right\rangle_{N}.

Finally, for every
r\in[1,+\infty]
,
t\geqslant 0
and
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
with
q^{\prime}-q\in L^{r}
, we have

(5.8)

\left|\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q^{\prime}})-\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\right|_{L^{r}}\leqslant 16N\left|q^{\prime}-{q}\right|_{L^{r}}.

In particular, the mapping
q\mapsto\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
can be extended to
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
by continuity, and the properties in (
5.6
) and (
5.8
) remain valid with
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
.

For the statement of (
5.7
), we impose that
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
so that the Gibbs measure appearing there is well-defined.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

For clarity of presentation we only prove the results concerning
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}
; showing the joint differentiability in
(t,q)
does not pose additional difficulties, the property of
\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
in (
5.7
) is a Gaussian integration by parts as in (
3.11
), and the property of
\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
in (
5.6
) follows from that in (
5.7
) and (
3.30
).

We fix
t\geqslant 0
,
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, and write
\kappa:=q^{\prime}-q\in L^{\infty}([0,1];S^{D})
.
Conditionally on
\mathfrak{R}
, we construct the Gaussian processes
(w^{q}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}
and
(w^{\kappa}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}
from Proposition
4.1
independently of one another, and we observe that, for every
s\in[0,1]
,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\left(w^{{q}+s\kappa}(\alpha)\right)_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}\left(w^{{q}}(\alpha)+\sqrt{s}w^{{\kappa}}(\alpha)\right)_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}.

We may as well take the right-hand side of (
5.9
) as our definition of
w^{q+s\kappa}
, so that these processes are realized on the same probability space and coupled together in a natural way as
s
varies in
[0,1]
. It follows from (
4.4
) that, for every
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{U}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}w^{{q}+s\kappa}(\alpha)\right)\left(w^{{q}+s\kappa}(\alpha^{\prime})\right)^{\intercal}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right).

By this, the Gaussian integration by parts in
[
37
, Theorem 4.6]
, and the fact that
\alpha\wedge\alpha=1
almost surely under
\mathbb{P}\mathfrak{R}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q}+s\kappa)\big|_{s=0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\sqrt{2}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}w^{{q}+s\kappa}(\alpha)\right)\cdot\sigma-\kappa(1)\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\right\rangle_{N}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle R^{1,2}\right\rangle_{N},

where we write

(5.10)

R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}:=\kappa(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}})\cdot\frac{\sigma^{\ell}(\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}})^{\intercal}}{N},

and we observe that
\kappa(1)
is well-defined since
q(1)
and
q^{\prime}(1)
are well-defined. Differentiating once more yields that

(5.11)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q}+s\kappa)\big|_{s=0}=2N\mathbb{E}\left\langle R^{1,2}R^{1,2}-4R^{1,2}R^{1,3}+3R^{1,2}R^{3,4}\right\rangle_{N}.

We recall from (
3.30
) that for every
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
in the support of
P_{N}
, we have
\left|\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right|\leqslant 1
. Using also the invariance of Poisson–Dirichlet cascades in Proposition
4.8
and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we obtain that

(5.12)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q}+s\kappa)\big|_{s=0}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|R^{1,2}\right|\right\rangle_{N}\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left\langle|\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)|\right\rangle_{N}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle|\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)|\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}=|\kappa|_{L^{1}},

as well as

(5.13)

\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}s^{2}}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q}+s\kappa)\big|_{s=0}\right|\leqslant 16N\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|R^{1,2}\right|^{2}\right\rangle_{N}\leqslant 16N\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|^{2}\right\rangle_{N}\\
=16N\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}=16N|\kappa|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

We define the function
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)=\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q,\cdot)\in L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
by duality so that for every
\kappa\in L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
,

(5.14)

\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right\rangle_{N}.

The estimates (
5.12
) and (
5.13
) imply that

(5.15)

\left|\overline{F}_{N}(t,q^{\prime})-\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)-\left\langle q^{\prime}-q,\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|\leqslant 8N|q^{\prime}-q|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

It follows from (
3.30
) and (
5.14
) that for every
\kappa\in L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
,

(5.16)

\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\leqslant|\kappa|_{L^{1}}.

By duality, we deduce that
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\in L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
.
Therefore, we also have that

|\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)-\overline{F}_{N}(t,q^{\prime})|\leqslant|q-q^{\prime}|_{L^{1}}.

In particular, we can extend the free energy
\overline{F}_{N}
in (
5.2
) to all
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{1}
by continuity. We have already observed that the definitions of
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
in (
5.2
) and (
3.7
) coincide when
q
is piecewise constant, and each has been extended continuously to
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, so the two definitions coincide everywhere on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{1}
.

By continuity, the estimate in (
5.15
), which we showed for
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, extends to every
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. This already shows that every point
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
is a point of Fréchet differentiability of
\overline{F}_{N}(t,\cdot):\mathcal{Q}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, and the Fréchet derivative at this point is indeed
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
. We now wish to extend this to every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. We thus fix
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
and take a sequence
(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of elements of
\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
that converges to
q
in
L^{2}
. Since
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q_{n})\in L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can extract a subsequence that converges weakly in
L^{2}
; we denote the limit by
p
. Since
(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
q
strongly in
L^{2}
, substituting
q
by
q_{n}
in (
5.15
) and letting
n
tend to infinity along the subsequence yields that, for every
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
,

\left|\overline{F}_{N}(t,q^{\prime})-\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)-\left\langle q^{\prime}-q,p\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|\leqslant 8N|q^{\prime}-q|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

This characterizes
p
uniquely, so in fact the subsequence extraction was not necessary and
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q_{n})
converges weakly to an object that depends only on
q
itself, not on the approximating sequence. We denote this object by
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
, a notation that is consistent with the previous definition that was restricted to the case
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
. We have thus shown that
\overline{F}_{N}(t,\cdot):\mathcal{Q}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is Fréchet differentiable everywhere.

Since the inequality (
5.16
) is preserved by taking weak
L^{2}
limits on
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
, we also have that
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
belongs to
L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
for every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
.

We recall from Proposition
3.6
that
\overline{F}_{N}(t,\cdot)
is
\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{\star}
-increasing, as defined in (
3.19
). For every smooth
q\in\mathcal{Q}
with derivative bounded away from zero and every smooth
\kappa\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{\star}
, we must therefore have that

\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\geqslant 0.

By density, this is in fact valid for every
\kappa\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{\star}
. This means that
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
belongs to the dual cone

(\mathcal{Q}^{*}_{2})^{*}:=\left\{{q}\in L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})\,\mid\,\forall{q^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{*},\ \left\langle{q},{q^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\geqslant 0\right\}.

Since
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
is a non-empty closed convex cone in a Hilbert space, we have by
[
19
, Corollary 6.33]
that
(\mathcal{Q}^{*}_{2})^{*}=\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. (One can also prove this more directly by proceeding similarly to the proof of Lemma
3.5
.) We have therefore verified that
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
for every smooth
q\in\mathcal{Q}
whose derivative is bounded away from zero. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
is closed and convex, it is also weakly closed, by
[
39
, Corollary 1.4]
(or again by direct arguments). For general
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, since we can obtain
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
as a weak
L^{2}
limit of similar objects computed at smooth paths with derivative bounded away from zero, we deduce that
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
belongs to
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
for every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
.

So far we have only observed the continuity of the mapping
q\mapsto\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
in the sense of weak
L^{2}
convergence. Now that we also know that
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
, we can appeal to Lemma
3.4
and upgrade the continuity property of the mapping
q\mapsto\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
to the strong
L^{r}
topology, for every
r<+\infty
. Precisely, for every
r<+\infty
and every sequence
(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{2}
converging to
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
in
L^{2}
, we have

(5.17)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\left|\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q_{n})-\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\right|_{L^{r}}=0.

It remains to show (
5.8
).
We temporarily display the dependence of
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
on
{q}
explicitly by writing
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}=\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,{q}}
. We fix
{q},{q}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
as in the beginning of this proof, with
\kappa=q^{\prime}-q
, and for each
\eta\in L^{\infty}
, we aim to compute the derivative in
s
of

\mathbb{E}\left\langle\eta\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right\rangle_{N,{q}+s\kappa}.

We realize the Gaussian processes
(w^{q+s\kappa}(\alpha))_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}
as discussed below (
5.9
). As for (
5.11
), recalling the notation
R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}
in (
5.10
) and denoting

Q^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}:=\eta(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}})\cdot\frac{\sigma^{\ell}(\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}})^{\intercal}}{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\eta\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right\rangle_{N,{q}+s\kappa}=2N\mathbb{E}\left\langle Q^{1,2}\left(R^{1,2}-4R^{1,3}+3R^{3,4}\right)\right\rangle_{N,{q}+s\kappa}.

By Hölder’s inequality and the invariance of Poisson–Dirichlet cascades from Proposition
4.8
, we get, for every
r,r^{\prime}\in[1,+\infty]
with
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}=1
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\eta\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right\rangle_{N,{q}+s\kappa}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant 16N\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|Q^{1,2}\right|^{r^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{N,{q}+s\kappa}^{1/r^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|R^{1,2}\right|^{r}\right\rangle_{N,{q}+s\kappa}^{1/r}

\displaystyle\leqslant 16N\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|\eta\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|^{r^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{N,{q}+s\kappa}^{1/r^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|^{r}\right\rangle_{N,{q}+s\kappa}^{1/r}

\displaystyle=16N\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|\eta\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|^{r^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}^{1/r^{\prime}}\left\langle\left|\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|^{r}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}^{1/r}

\displaystyle=16N|\eta|_{L^{r^{\prime}}}|q^{\prime}-q|_{L^{r}}.

Integrating the above in
s
from
0
to
1
and using (
5.14
), we get

(5.18)

\displaystyle\left|\left\langle\eta,\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q}^{\prime})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}-\left\langle\eta,\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|\leqslant 16N|\eta|_{L^{r^{\prime}}}|{q}^{\prime}-{q}|_{L^{r}}.

We established (
5.18
) for every
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
and
\eta\in L^{\infty}
. By duality, this implies that, for every
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
,

\left|\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q^{\prime})-\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\right|_{L^{r}}\leqslant 16N|q^{\prime}-q|_{L^{r}}.

For
r<+\infty
, we can use (
5.17
) to extend this estimate to every
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
with
q^{\prime}-q\in L^{r}
. The case
r=+\infty
can then be obtained by using the result for finite
r
and letting
r
tend to infinity. The proof is therefore complete.
∎

Recall from Proposition
3.2
that the initial condition is given by
\psi=\overline{F}_{1}(0,\cdot)
, which can be written explicitly as

(5.19)

\displaystyle\psi({q})=-\mathbb{E}\log\iint\exp\left(\sqrt{2}w^{q}(\alpha)\cdot\sigma-{q}(1)\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{1}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha).

Proposition
5.1
immediately yields similar statements concerning the function
\psi
. We record them here for future reference. For every
{q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, we write

(5.20)

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{q}\propto\exp\left(w^{{q}}(\alpha)\cdot\sigma-{q}(1)\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{1}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha).

Corollary 5.2
.

The function
\psi
is
L^{1}
-Lipschitz continuous, with Lipschitz constant equal to
1
.
The restriction of the function
\psi
to
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
is Fréchet (and Gateaux) differentiable everywhere; we denote its Fréchet (and Gateaux) derivative by
\partial_{q}\psi(q)=\partial_{q}\psi(q,\cdot)\in L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
. We have, for every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
,

(5.21)

\partial_{q}\psi(q)\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1},

and, for every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
and
\kappa\in L^{2}([0,1];S^{D})
,

(5.22)

\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}\psi({q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}\right\rangle_{q}.

Moreover, for every
r\in[1,+\infty]
and
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
with
q^{\prime}-q\in L^{r}
, we have

(5.23)

\left|\partial_{q}\psi({q})-\partial_{q}\psi({q^{\prime}})\right|_{L^{r}}\leqslant 16\left|{q}-{q^{\prime}}\right|_{L^{r}}.

In particular, the mapping
q\mapsto\partial_{q}\psi(q)
can be extended to
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
by continuity, and the properties in (
5.21
) and (
5.23
) remain valid with
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
.

We recall that Proposition
3.3
asserts that the sequence
(\overline{F}_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is precompact. The regularity estimates on
\overline{F}_{N}
that hold uniformly over
N
naturally transfer to any subsequential limit. Using also the results from Section
2
, we infer that any such limit must be Gateaux differentiable “almost everywhere”. We summarize these points in the next proposition.

Proposition 5.3

(Regularity of the limit)
.

Suppose that
\overline{F}_{N}
converges to some limit
f
pointwise along some subsequence.
Then for every
r\in(1,+\infty]
, the function
\overline{F}_{N}
converges locally uniformly to
f
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{r}
along the subsequence. We have that
f
satisfies the same Lipschitz and local semi-concavity properties as
\overline{F}_{N}
stated in Propositions
3.1
and
3.8
, and that
f
is
\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{\star}
-increasing as
\overline{F}_{N}
is from Proposition
3.6
. Moreover,

•

for each
t\geqslant 0
,
there is a Gaussian null set
\mathcal{N}_{t}
of
{L^{2}}
such that
f(t,\cdot):\mathcal{Q}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is Gateaux differentiable at every point in
\mathcal{Q}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{t}
and
(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})\setminus\mathcal{N}_{t}
is dense in
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
;

•

there is a Gaussian null set
\mathcal{N}
of
\mathbb{R}\times{L^{2}}
such that
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is Gateaux differentiable on
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2})\setminus\mathcal{N}
and
(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}))\setminus\mathcal{N}
is dense in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
,

In the statement of Proposition
5.3
and throughout the paper, we allow ourselves some imprecision when saying that
\overline{F}_{N}
converges pointwise to
f
along a subsequence, since we do not clearly specify over which set this pointwise convergence is valid. As long as there exists a dense set of points
(t,q)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{1}
such that
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
converges to
f(t,q)
along the subsequence, the convergence immediately extends to every
(t,q)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{1}
by the Lipschitz continuity property in Proposition
3.1
.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

The fact that the convergence of
\overline{F}_{N}
to
f
along the subsequence holds locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{r}
, for every
r\in(1,+\infty]
, was already mentioned and follows from Proposition
3.3
. The fact that
f
is Lipschitz, locally semi-concave, and
\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{*}
-increasing is immediate from Propositions
3.1
and
3.8
. The differentiability statements follow from Proposition
2.7
.
∎

The next proposition asserts that at points of differentiability of the limit
f
, the derivatives of
\overline{F}_{N}
converge to those of
f
.

Proposition 5.4

(Convergence of derivatives)
.

Suppose that
\overline{F}_{N}
converges pointwise to some limit
f
along a subsequence
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.

(1) For each
t\geqslant 0
, if
f(t,\cdot)
is Gateaux differentiable at
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}
, then
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N_{k}}(t,q,\cdot)
converges in
L^{r}
to
\partial_{q}f(t,q,\cdot)
for every
r\in[1,+\infty)
.

(2) For each
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, if
f(\cdot,q)
is differentiable at
t>0
, then
\partial_{t}\overline{F}_{N_{k}}(t,q)
converges to
\partial_{t}f(t,q)
.

Proof.

We only show part (1) of the statement, since the second part is only easier. We decompose the proof into three steps.

Step 1.
We start by recalling that for every
a,b\in S^{D}
, the largest eigenvalue of
a+b
is smaller than the sum of the largest eigenvalues of
a
and
b
, and similarly with the smallest eigenvalue. This can be seen from the variational representation of the largest and smallest eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix.

Recall also the definition of
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow,c}
in (
3.32
), and that
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}=\bigcup_{c>0}\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow,c}
. Let
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow,c}
, and let
\kappa\in C^{\infty}([0,1];S^{D})
be a smooth function with
\kappa(0)=0
. In this step, we show that there is
\delta>0
such that for every
s\in[-\delta,\delta]
, we have
q+s\kappa\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow,\frac{c}{2}}
.

Let
\eta\in(0,c)
be a parameter to be determined in the course of the argument, and let
\delta>0
be sufficiently small that for every
u\leqslant v\in[0,1]
, every eigenvalue of
\delta(\kappa(u)-\kappa(v))
lies in the interval
[-\eta(v-u),\eta(v-u)]
. (This amounts to taking
\delta>0
sufficiently small that every eigenvalue of
\delta\dot{\kappa}(u)
belongs to
[-\eta,\eta]
for every
u\in[0,1]
.) For every
s\in[-\delta,\delta]
and
0\leqslant u\leqslant v<1
, we have

(q+s\kappa)(v)-(q+s\kappa)(u)\geqslant(c-\eta)(v-u)\mathrm{Id}.

Let
\lambda_{\max}
and
\lambda_{\min}
be the largest and the smallest eigenvalues of
q(v)-q(u)
respectively. We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ellipt}((q+s\kappa)(v)-(q+s\kappa)(u))\leqslant\frac{\lambda_{\max}+\eta(v-u)}{\lambda_{\min}-\eta(v-u)}\leqslant\frac{1+\tfrac{\eta}{c}}{1-\tfrac{\eta}{c}}\,\frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}},

where we used that
\lambda_{\max}\geqslant\lambda_{\min}\geqslant c(v-u)
in the second inequality. In view of the last two displays, we obtain the announced result by choosing
\eta=\frac{c}{3}
.

Step 2.
In this step, we show part (1) of the statement except that we only prove that
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N_{k}}(t,q)=\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N_{k}}(t_{N_{k}},q_{N_{k}},\cdot)
converges weakly in
L^{2}
to
\partial_{q}f(t,q)=\partial_{q}f(t,q,\cdot)
. The notion of convergence will be upgraded in the next step.

By assumption, there exists
c>0
such that
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow,c}
. Let
\kappa\in C^{\infty}([0,1];S^{D})
be a smooth function with
\kappa(0)=0
. By the result of the previous step, we can find
\delta>0
such that for every
s\in[-\delta,\delta]
, we have
q+s\kappa\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow,\frac{c}{2}}
.
By Proposition
3.8
, there is a constant
C<+\infty
(depending on
c
) such that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\in[-\delta,\delta]
, and
\lambda\in[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)\overline{F}_{N_{k}}(t,{q})+\lambda\overline{F}_{N_{k}}(t,{q}+s\kappa)-\overline{F}_{N_{k}}(t,{q}+\lambda s\kappa)\leqslant C\lambda(1-\lambda)s^{2}|\dot{\kappa}|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Dividing both sides by
\lambda
and sending
\lambda
to zero, we get

\displaystyle\overline{F}_{N_{k}}(t,{q}+s\kappa)-\overline{F}_{N_{k}}(t,{q})-s\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N_{k}}(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\leqslant Cs^{2}|\dot{\kappa}|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Since
|\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N_{k}}(t,{q})|_{L^{2}}
is bounded uniformly in
k\in\mathbb{N}
due to Proposition
5.1
, for any subsequence
(N^{\prime}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, we can extract a further subsequence
(N^{\prime\prime}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
along which
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})
converges weakly to some
{p}\in{L^{2}}
. Passing to the limit along
(N^{\prime\prime}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, we get

\displaystyle f(t,{q}+s\kappa)-f(t,{q})-s\left\langle\kappa,{p}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\leqslant Cs^{2}|\dot{\kappa}|^{2}_{L^{2}}

for every
s\in[-\delta,\delta]
.
Sending
s
to zero and using the Gateaux differentiability of
f(t,\cdot)
at
{q}
, we obtain that

\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}f(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\left\langle\kappa,{p}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Since the set of
\kappa
’s that we have considered is dense in
{L^{2}}
, we conclude that
\partial_{q}f(t,{q})={p}
.

We have thus shown that any subsequence
(N^{\prime}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
has a further subsequence
(N^{\prime\prime}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
along which
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})
converges weakly to
\partial_{q}f(t,{q})
. This therefore completes the argument for the weak convergence in
L^{2}
of
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N_{k}}(t,q)
to
\partial_{q}f(t,q)
.

Step 3.
In this step, we improve the convergence obtained in the previous step from weakly in
L^{2}
to strongly in
L^{r}
for every
r\in[1,+\infty)
. We recall from (
5.6
) that
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
. The strong convergence in
L^{r}
therefore follows from Lemma
3.4
. The proof is therefore complete.
∎

6.
Cavity calculations and ultrametricity

The goal of this section is to present a number of cavity calculations and exploit the asymptotic ultrametricity of the Gibbs measure. Our arguments contain and extend the method introduced in
[
3
]
and now often called the Aizenman–Sims–Starr scheme. Roughly speaking, the cavity calculation of
[
3
]
consists in comparing
\overline{F}_{N+1}
with
\overline{F}_{N}
by separating out the integration of one of the spin variables. Next, if one knows that the Gibbs measure is approximately ultrametric, then one can observe that the integration of the spun-off spin variable can be expressed in the form of

\psi({q}+t\nabla\xi({p}))-t\int_{0}^{1}\big(p\cdot\nabla\xi(p)-\xi(p)\big),

for some
p\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
which may depend on
N
