. Ultrametricity can indeed be enforced by means of small perturbations of the Hamiltonian, as was shown in
[
76
]
and reviewed in detail in
[
77
]
. Intuitively, for each fixed
N
, the natural choice of
p
is such that the law of the overlap under the “cavity Gibbs measure” with
N
spins is the law of
p(U)
, where
U
is uniformly distributed over
[0,1]
.

We will perform a family of other cavity calculations that will allow us to keep track of arbitrary continuous observables of the overlap. This will have two main benefits that will be detailed in the next section. The first one is that we will indeed be able to track the identity of
p
in the calculation sketched above. Under a differentiability assumption on the limit free energy
f
, we will justify that we can choose
p=\partial_{q}f(t,q)
. The second crucial benefit is that we will discover another property that this
p
must satisfy, namely, that

p=\partial_{q}\psi(q+t\nabla\xi(p)).

Collecting all this information then essentially yields a proof of our main results.

This section is organized as follows. We first define the various terms that will enter into the cavity calculations. We next perform the Aizenman–Simms–Starr calculation for the free energy. We then do a cavity calculation for a general observable of the overlap of the spun-off spin variables. We continue by establishing that the quantities we obtain in these calculations can be approximated arbitrarily closely by continuous functions of the law of the overlap array. We next discuss Ghirlanda–Guerra identities, which are enforced by small perturbations of the Hamiltonian and ensure ultrametricity. The ultrametricity property implies that the law of the overlap array is a continuous function of the law of the overlap between only two replicas; and Poisson–Dirichlet cascades provide us with a canonical representation of these objects. So all the “cavity averages” we obtained in the cavity calculations, which involve the “cavity Gibbs measure”, can in fact be re-expressed as cavity averages with respect to some fixed Poisson–Dirichlet cascade.

Throughout this section, we fix
(t,{q})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, and the integer
M\in\mathbb{N}
denotes the number of cavity spins. For the purpose of this work, we only need to consider the case when
M=1
, but we prefer to carry the cavity calculations with a general
M
, as these might be useful for future applications.

6.1.
Definitions and notation

6.1.1.
Hamiltonians and perturbation

We start by introducing the various Hamiltonians that will enter into the cavity calculations. The basic idea of a cavity calculation is to decompose an element
\rho\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times(N+M)}
into
\rho=(\sigma,\tau)
, with
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times M}
, and to express the free energy of Gibbs averages involving
N+M
variables as averages of the
\tau
variables under some Gibbs measure over the
\sigma
variables. We are in particular interested in separating out the dependence in
\sigma
and
\tau
of the Hamiltonian
H_{N+M}(\rho)=H_{N+M}((\sigma,\tau))
. Temporarily focusing on the case
M=1
, we would like for instance to argue that

(6.1)

H_{N+1}(\sigma,\tau)\simeq H_{N+1}(\sigma,0)+\tau\cdot\nabla_{\tau}H_{N+1}(\sigma,0),

where we use the somewhat informal notation
\nabla_{\tau}H_{N+1}
to denote the gradient in
\tau
of the mapping
(\sigma,\tau)\mapsto H_{N+1}(\sigma,\tau)
. Since
\rho\rho^{\intercal}=\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}+\tau\tau^{\intercal}
, we have from (
1.1
) that for every
(\sigma,\tau),(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times(N+1)}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[H_{N+1}(\sigma,\tau)H_{N+1}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\right]=(N+1)\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}+\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal}}{N+1}\right).

Differentiating this expression, we get that for every
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
,

(6.2)

\mathbb{E}\left[H_{N+1}(\sigma,\tau)\big(b\cdot\nabla_{\tau}H_{N+1}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\big)\right]=\tau b^{\intercal}\cdot\nabla\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}+\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal}}{N+1}\right)

and

(6.3)

\mathbb{E}\left[\big(a\cdot\nabla_{\tau}H_{N+1}(\sigma,0)\big)\big(b\cdot\nabla_{\tau}H_{N+1}\big)(\sigma^{\prime},0)\right]=ba^{\intercal}\cdot\nabla\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N+1}\right).

We see from (
6.2
) that
(H_{N+1}(\sigma,0))_{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}}
and
(\nabla_{\tau}H_{N+1}(\sigma,0))_{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}}
are independent Gaussian fields. The Hamiltonian
H_{N+1}(\sigma,0)
will serve as our reference Hamiltonian, the common part between
H_{N+1}
and
H_{N}
, and we will similarly expand
H_{N}(\sigma)
into
H_{N+1}(\sigma,0)
plus an independent Gaussian field. From now on, we revert to the setting with general
M\in\mathbb{N}
, and introduce convenient notation to denote the various Hamiltonians entering our calculations. While we may not always repeat it, each time we introduce a new Hamiltonian or other Gaussian field, we impose that it be independent of all the previously-defined random fields; and the expectation
\mathbb{E}
is understood to average all these processes.

In order to enforce the asymptotic validity of the Ghirlanda–Guerra identities, and thereby the ultrametricity of the Gibbs measure, we need to introduce a rich family of perturbations of the Hamiltonian.
Let
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be an enumeration of
[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
, and
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be an enumeration of
\{a\in S^{D}_{+}:|a|\leqslant 1\}\cap\mathbb{Q}^{D\times D}
. For every
h\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, let
(H^{h}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha))_{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times N},\,\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}}

be an independent centered Gaussian process with covariance

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[H^{h}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)H^{h}_{N}(\sigma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\right]=N\left(a_{h_{1}}\cdot\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right)^{\odot h_{2}}+\lambda_{h_{3}}\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)^{h_{4}}

where
\odot
is the Schur product of matrices, i.e.
a\odot b=(a_{i,j}b_{i,j})_{i,j}
.
Let us explain why this process exists. By
[
71
, (6.1)-(6.2)]
, there exists a Gaussian process with covariance
a\cdot\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right)^{\odot n}
for every
a\in S^{D}_{+}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Proposition
4.1
ensures the existence of a Gaussian process with covariance
\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}
. Linear combinations with non-negative coefficients of covariance functions are still covariance functions, since we can simply take superpositions of independent processes to realize them, and
[
71
, Proposition 6.5]
also allows for the multiplication of two covariance functions. Finally, the measurability can be handled as in the proof of Proposition
4.1
.

For each
h\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, let
c_{h}>0
be a constant such that

\displaystyle c_{h}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}H^{h}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)H^{h}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)}\leqslant 2^{-|h|_{1}},

uniformly over
\sigma\in\supp P_{N}
,
\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}
, and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and where we write
|h|_{1}:=\sum_{i=1}^{4}h_{i}
.
Let
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{D(D+1)/2}
be an orthonormal basis of
S^{D}
.
For every

(6.4)

\displaystyle x=\left((x_{h})_{h\in\mathbb{N}^{4}},(x_{i})_{i=1}^{D(D+1)/2}\right)\in[0,3]^{\mathbb{N}^{4}\times\frac{D(D+1)}{2}},

we set

(6.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}H_{N}^{x}(\sigma,\alpha)&:=\check{H}_{N}^{x}(\sigma,\alpha)+\sum_{i=1}^{D(D+1)/2}x_{i}e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal},\qquad\text{where}\\
\check{H}_{N}^{x}(\sigma,\alpha)&:=\sum_{h\in\mathbb{N}^{4}}x_{h}c_{h}H^{h}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha).\end{split}

The second term in the definition of
H_{N}^{x}
will ensure the concentration of the “self-overlap”
\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}
. Notice that we impose each coordinate of
x
to take values in the interval
[0,3]
.
We define the free energy with perturbation

(6.6)

\displaystyle\overline{F}^{x}_{N}(t,{q})

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\iint\exp\left(H^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha),

and its associated Gibbs measure

(6.7)

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{\circ}_{N,x}

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(H^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha).

We keep denoting by
(\sigma,\alpha)
the canonical random variable under
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{\circ}_{N,x}
, and we write
(\sigma^{\ell},\alpha^{\ell})_{\ell\geqslant 1}
to denote independent copies of
(\sigma,\alpha)
.
The expectation
\mathbb{E}
in (
6.6
) integrates all the sources of Gaussian randomness and the randomness of
\mathfrak{R}
.

Our “reference” Hamiltonian for the cavity calculation is denoted by
(\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma))_{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}}
, and it is the centered Gaussian process such that, for every
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma)\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma^{\prime})\right]=(N+M)\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N+M}\right).

This is simply an independent copy of what we were denoting
H_{N+M}(\sigma,0)
at the opening of this section. Notice that the parameter
M
appears in the definition of the covariance above. Abusing notation, we prefer to keep the dependence of
\widetilde{H}_{N}
on
M
implicit, as it only takes
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
as input.
We let
\widetilde{W}^{q}_{N}
be an independent copy of
W^{q}_{N}
defined in (
5.1
), and, for every
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
and
\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}
, we set

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha):=\sqrt{2t}\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma)-t(N+M)\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N+M}\right)+\widetilde{W}^{q}_{N}(\alpha)\cdot\sigma-{q}(1)\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}.

We denote the free energy and the Gibbs measure arising in the cavity calculation by

(6.8)

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{N}^{x}(t,{q}):=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\iint\exp\left(\widetilde{H}^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha)

and

(6.9)

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}\propto\exp\left(\widetilde{H}^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha).

6.1.2.
Notation for the overlaps

For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
,
h\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we write

(6.10)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{N,\sigma}:=\frac{\sigma^{\ell}\left(\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)^{\intercal}}{N},\qquad R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\alpha}:=\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}},\qquad R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{N}:=\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{N,\sigma},R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\right);\\
R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{N,h}:=\left(a_{h_{1}}\cdot\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{N,\sigma}\right)^{\odot h_{2}}+\lambda_{h_{3}}R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\right)^{h_{4}};\\
R_{N}:=\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{N}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}},\qquad R^{\leqslant n}_{N}:=\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{N}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}.\end{cases}

For every
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, we set

(6.11)

\displaystyle Q^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{p}:=\left({p}\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\right),R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\alpha}\right),\hskip 20.00003ptQ_{p}:=\left(Q^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{p}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}},\hskip 20.00003ptQ^{\leqslant n}_{p}:=\left(Q^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{p}\right)_{1\leqslant\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n}.

Under some circumstances, we will be able to argue that the
R_{N,\sigma}
-overlaps synchronize with the
R_{\alpha}
-overlaps; this means that the
R_{N}
-overlaps are close to the
Q_{p}
-overlaps for a suitable choice of
p
.

6.1.3.
Definitions for the free-energy cavity calculation

We now define the Gibbs average that will show up in the free-energy cavity calculation, or in other words in the Aizenman–Sims–Starr scheme
[
3
]
. We define
\theta:\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times{D}}\to\mathbb{R}
such that, for every
a\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
,

(6.12)

\displaystyle\theta(a):=a\cdot\nabla\xi(a)-\xi(a).

We define the following independent centered Gaussian processes indexed by
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
:

•

let
\mathsf{Z}(\sigma)=(\mathsf{Z}_{1}(\sigma),\dots,\mathsf{Z}_{M}(\sigma))
be an
M
-tuple of independent
\mathbb{R}^{D}
-valued centered Gaussian vectors
\mathsf{Z}_{i}(\sigma)
with covariance
\mathbb{E}\mathsf{Z}_{i}(\sigma)\mathsf{Z}_{i}(\sigma^{\prime})^{\intercal}=\nabla\xi(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N})
;

•

let
\mathsf{Y}(\sigma)
be real-valued with covariance
\mathbb{E}\mathsf{Y}(\sigma)\mathsf{Y}(\sigma^{\prime})=M\theta(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N})
.

The fact that there indeed exists such a Gaussian process
\mathsf{Z}
follows from (
6.3
) and a simple rescaling. Concerning the existence of the process
\mathsf{Y}
, we recall from Subsection
1.5
that
\xi
takes the form given in (
1.24
), where the matrices
\mathsf{C}^{(p)}
belong to
S^{D^{p}}_{+}
and the series is absolutely convergent. A direct calculation yields that

\theta(a)=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}(p-1)\mathsf{C}^{(p)}\cdot a^{\otimes p}.

This is of the same form as (
1.24
), so the existence of the process
\mathsf{Y}
follows from the second part of Subsection
1.5
.

For every
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}
,
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times M}
, and
\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}
, we set

(6.13)

\displaystyle U(\sigma,\alpha,\tau):=\sqrt{2t}\mathsf{Z}(\sigma)\cdot\tau-t\nabla\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal}+\sqrt{2}W^{{q}}_{M}(\alpha)\cdot\tau-{q}(1)\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal},

and for every
x\in[0,3]^{\mathbb{N}^{4}\times\frac{D(D+1)}{2}}
,

(6.14)

A_{N}(x):=\mathbb{E}\log\left\langle\int\exp\left(U(\sigma,\alpha,\tau)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{M}(\tau)\right\rangle_{N,x}\\
-\mathbb{E}\log\left\langle\exp\left(\sqrt{2t}\mathsf{Y}(\sigma)-tM\theta\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{N,x}.

With
\psi
and
\theta
defined in (
5.19
) and (
6.12
), we set, for every
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
,

(6.15)

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p}):=\psi({q}+t\nabla\xi({p}))-t\int_{0}^{1}\theta({p}(s))\mathrm{d}s.

We will show that the limit of
A_{N}(x)
is related to
\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})
for some
{p}
.

6.1.4.
Definitions for the Gibbs-average cavity calculation

We write
\rho\in\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times(N+M)}
as
\rho=(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times N}\times\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times M}
.
For a bounded continuous function
g:\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times{D}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, we want to find the limit of
\mathbb{E}\left\langle g(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\circ}
as
N
tends to infinity. To study this, we need a few more definitions.
We define the Gibbs measure
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\star}
by

(6.16)

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\star}\propto\exp\left(\widetilde{H}_{N}^{t,{q}}(\sigma,\alpha)+U(\sigma,\alpha,\tau)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N+M}(\sigma,\tau)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha).

The canonical random variable under
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\star}
is denoted by
(\rho,\alpha)=(\sigma,\tau,\alpha)
.
For every
\pi\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, we define the Gibbs measure
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},\pi}
by

(6.17)

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},\pi}\propto\exp\left(\sqrt{2}W^{\pi}_{M}(\alpha)\cdot\tau-\pi(1)\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{M}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha),

and we denote by
(\tau,\alpha)
the canonical random variable under
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},\pi}
.
We will show that the limit of
\mathbb{E}\left\langle g(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\circ}
is related to
\mathbb{E}\left\langle g(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},\pi}
for some
\pi
.

6.2.
Cavity calculations per se

6.2.1.
Free-energy cavity calculation

Proposition 6.1

(Free-energy cavity calculation)
.

We have, uniformly over
M,N\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in[0,3]^{\mathbb{N}^{4}\times\frac{D(D+1)}{2}}
,

\displaystyle-(N+M)\overline{F}_{N+M}^{x}(t,{q})+N\overline{F}_{N}^{x}(t,{q})=A_{N}(x)+O\left(M^{2}N^{-\frac{1}{16}}\right).

Proof.

The argument consists in a rigorous justification of the expansion (
6.1
) and of a similar expansion for
H_{N}
.
We keep writing
\rho=(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times(N+M)}
, so that
\rho\rho^{\intercal}=\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}+\tau\tau^{\intercal}
and

\displaystyle W^{q}_{N+M}(\alpha)\cdot\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}\widetilde{W}^{q}_{N}(\alpha)\cdot\sigma+W^{q}_{M}(\alpha)\cdot\tau.

Using the Taylor expansion of
\xi
, the local Lipschitzness of
\nabla\xi
, and
|\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}-\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N+M}|=O(\frac{M}{N})
, we get, for every
\rho
in the support of
P_{N+M}
,

(6.18)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(N+M)\xi\left(\frac{\rho\rho^{\intercal}}{N+M}\right)&=(N+M)\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N+M}\right)+\nabla\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal}+O\left(\frac{M^{2}}{N}\right),\\
N\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)&=(N+M)\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N+M}\right)+M\theta\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)+O\left(\frac{M^{2}}{N}\right).\end{split}

These two relations yield, respectively,

(6.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}H_{N+M}(\rho)H_{N+M}(\rho^{\prime})&=\mathbb{E}\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma)\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma^{\prime})+\mathbb{E}(\mathsf{Z}(\sigma)\cdot\tau)(\mathsf{Z}(\sigma^{\prime})\cdot\tau^{\prime})+O\left(\frac{M^{2}}{N}\right),\\
\mathbb{E}H_{N}(\sigma)H_{N}(\sigma^{\prime})&=\mathbb{E}\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma)\widetilde{H}_{N}(\sigma^{\prime})+\mathbb{E}\mathsf{Y}(\sigma)\mathsf{Y}(\sigma^{\prime})+O\left(\frac{M^{2}}{N}\right)\end{split}

uniformly over
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
in the support of
P_{N+M}
.
Recalling
H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)
and
\check{H}^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)
in (
6.5
), we verify that

(6.20)

(N+M)^{-\frac{1}{8}}\mathbb{E}\check{H}^{x}_{N+M}(\rho,\alpha)\check{H}^{x}_{N+M}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\\
=N^{-\frac{1}{8}}\mathbb{E}\check{H}^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)\check{H}^{x}_{N}(\sigma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})+O\left(MN^{-\frac{1}{8}}\right).

Using again that
\rho\rho^{\prime\intercal}=\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}+\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal}
, we see that the second term on the right-hand side in (
6.5
) satisfies

(6.21)

\displaystyle(N+M)^{-\frac{1}{16}}\sum_{i=1}^{D(D+1)/2}x_{i}e_{i}\cdot\rho\rho^{\intercal}=N^{-\frac{1}{16}}\sum_{i=1}^{D(D+1)/2}x_{i}e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}+O\left(MN^{-\frac{1}{16}}\right).

We want to insert the above relations into

\displaystyle-(N+M)\overline{F}_{N+M}^{x}(t,{q})+N\overline{F}_{N}^{x}(t,{q})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\log\iint\exp\left(H^{t,{q}}_{N+M}(\rho,\alpha)+(N+M)^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N+M}(\rho,\alpha)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N+M}(\rho)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\log\iint\exp\left(H^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha)=:\mathrm{I}_{0}-\mathrm{II}_{0}.

Recalling
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
in (
6.9
),
we rewrite
A_{N}(x)
in (
6.14
) as

\displaystyle A_{N}(x)=\mathbb{E}\log\iint\exp\left(U(\sigma,\alpha,\tau)+\widetilde{H}^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N+M}(\sigma,\tau)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha)

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}\log\iint\exp\left(\sqrt{2t}\mathsf{Y}(\sigma)-tM\theta\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)+\widetilde{H}^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=:\mathrm{I}_{1}-\mathrm{II}_{1}.

Note that all the Hamiltonians are of the form
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{s})+\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{s})
where the first term
\mathbf{G}(\mathbf{s})
is centered Gaussian, the second term
\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{s})
is deterministic, and
\mathbf{s}
is
(\rho,\alpha)
or
(\sigma,\alpha)
.
To compare
\mathrm{I}_{0}
with
\mathrm{I}_{1}
and
\mathrm{II}_{0}
with
\mathrm{II}_{1}
, we consider two interpolations of the form

\displaystyle\varphi(r):=\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\left(\sqrt{1-r}\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s})+(1-r)\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s})+\sqrt{r}\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s})+r\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s})\right)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{P}(\mathbf{s}),

where
\mathbf{G}^{0}
and
\mathbf{G}^{1}
are independent and
\mathfrak{P}
is
P_{N+M}\otimes\mathfrak{R}
or
P_{N}\otimes\mathfrak{R}
.
For the first interpolation between
\mathrm{I}_{0}
and
\mathrm{I}_{1}
, we take

(6.22)

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s})+\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=H^{t,{q}}_{N+M}(\rho,\alpha)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N+M}(\rho,\alpha),

(6.23)

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s})+\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=U(\sigma,\alpha,\tau)+\widetilde{H}^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha).

We understand that the Gaussian fields
H_{N+M}^{x}
and
H_{N}^{x}
appearing in (
6.22
) and (
6.23
) respectively are independent.
For the second interpolation, between
\mathrm{II}_{0}
and
\mathrm{II}_{1}
, we take

(6.24)

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s})+\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=H^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha),

(6.25)

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s})+\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2t}\mathsf{Y}(\sigma)-tM\theta\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)+\widetilde{H}^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha).

To remain faithful with what we announced, we should take the Gaussian fields
H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)
appearing in (
6.24
) and (
6.25
) to be independent. This is of course not necessary here, and taking them to be equal is just as convenient; this can be phrased in our setting by absorbing this term into the definition of
\mathfrak{P}
.

We control
|\varphi(1)-\varphi(0)|
by computing the derivative

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\varphi(r)=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{r}}\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s})-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{1-r}}\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s})+\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s})-\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s})\right\rangle_{r},

where
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{r}
is the Gibbs measure naturally associated with the free energy expression defining
\varphi(r)
.
We use Gaussian integration by parts (e.g.
[
37
, Theorem 4.6]
) to get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\varphi(r)=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s})-\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s}^{\prime})+\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s})-\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s})\right\rangle_{r},

where
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
is an independent copy of
\mathbf{s}
under
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{r}
, and

(6.26)

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s}^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbb{E}\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s})\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})-\mathbb{E}\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s})\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})\right).

We can then bound
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{D}^{1}-\mathbf{D}^{0}
using the estimates in (
6.18
), (
6.19
), (
6.20
), and (
6.21
).
For both interpolations, we have
|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\varphi(r)|=O(M^{2}N^{-\frac{1}{16}})
where
M^{2}
comes from (
6.18
) and (
6.19
), and
N^{-\frac{1}{16}}
comes from (
6.20
) and (
6.21
).
∎

Lemma 6.2

(Cascade representation of
\mathscr{P}
)
.

For each
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, conditionally on
\mathfrak{R}
and on the event where
\mathfrak{U}=\supp\mathfrak{R}
satisfies (
4.2
), we define the following independent centered Gaussian processes indexed by
\alpha\in\mathfrak{U}
:

•

let
Z(\alpha)=(Z_{1}(\alpha),\dots,Z_{M}(\alpha))
be an
M
-tuple of independent
\mathbb{R}^{D}
-valued centered Gaussian vectors
Z_{i}(\alpha)
with covariance
\mathbb{E}Z_{i}(\alpha)Z_{i}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\intercal}=\nabla\xi({p}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right))
;

•

let
Y(\alpha)
be real-valued with covariance
\mathbb{E}Y(\alpha)Y(\alpha^{\prime})=M\theta({p}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right))
.

Letting also

V(\alpha,\tau):=\sqrt{2t}Z(\alpha)\cdot\tau-t\nabla\xi\left({p}(1)\right)\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal}+\sqrt{2}W^{q}_{M}(\alpha)\cdot\tau-{q}(1)\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal},

we have

(6.27)

-M\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})=\mathbb{E}\log\iint\exp\left(V(\alpha,\tau)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{M}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha)\\
-\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\left(\sqrt{2t}Y(\alpha)-tM\theta\left({p}(1)\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha).

Proof.

We denote the right-hand side of (
6.27
) by
\mathrm{I}-\mathrm{II}
.
We have

\sqrt{2t}Z(\alpha)+\sqrt{2}W^{q}_{M}(\alpha)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}\sqrt{2}W^{{q}+t\nabla\xi({p})}_{M}(\alpha),

and thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}=-M\overline{F}_{M}(0,{q}+t\nabla\xi({p}))=-M\psi(0,{q}+t\nabla\xi({p})),

where we used Proposition
3.2
in the last equality.

To compute
\mathrm{II}
, we assume that
{p}
is a step function; the general case can be recovered by approximation (one can verify that
\mathrm{II}
is continuous in
{p}
by a similar argument to that for the continuity in
q
of
\overline{F}_{N}
from Proposition
5.1
). Hence, we assume that
{p}=\sum_{l=1}^{k}p_{l}\mathbf{1}_{\left[s_{l-1},s_{l}\right)}
for
0=s_{0}<s_{1}<\dots<s_{k}=1
and
0\leqslant p_{0}\leqslant p_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant p_{k}
. For brevity, we also write
\widetilde{\theta}:=2tM\theta
. Following the computation of the second term in
[
77
, Lemma 3.1]
(comparing the second term in
[
77
, (3.11)]
with that in
[
77
, (3.15)]
; substituting
s_{l},p_{l},\widetilde{\theta}
for
\zeta_{p},q_{p},\theta
therein), we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\sqrt{2t}Y(\alpha)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}s_{l}\left(\widetilde{\theta}(p_{l+1})-\widetilde{\theta}(p_{l})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(-\sum_{l=1}^{k}(s_{l}-s_{l-1})\widetilde{\theta}(p_{l})+s_{k}\widetilde{\theta}(p_{k})-s_{0}\widetilde{\theta}(p_{0})\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(-\int_{0}^{1}\widetilde{\theta}({p}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\widetilde{\theta}({p}(1))\right),

which yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}=\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\sqrt{2t}Y(\alpha)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha)-tM\theta({p}(1))=-tM\int_{0}^{1}\theta({p}(s))\mathrm{d}s.

Comparing
\mathrm{I}
and
\mathrm{II}
with the definition of
\mathscr{P}_{t,q}(p)
in (
6.15
), we get the desired result.
∎

6.2.2.
Gibbs-average cavity calculation

Recall
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{\circ}_{N,x}
in (
6.7
) and
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\star}
in (
6.16
).

Proposition 6.3

(Gibbs-average cavity calculation)
.

For every continuous function
g:\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times{D}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have uniformly over
M,N\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in[0,3]^{\mathbb{N}^{4}\times\frac{D(D+1)}{2}}
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\circ}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\star}+O\left(M^{2}N^{-\frac{1}{16}}\right).

Proof.

We use the estimates in the proof of Proposition
6.1
and a similar interpolation computation.
We write
\Theta:=g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)
,
\mathbf{s}:=(\rho,\alpha)
, and
\mathfrak{P}:=P_{N+M}\otimes\mathfrak{R}
.
We rewrite
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\circ}
and
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\star}
as follows,

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\circ}\propto\exp\left(\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s})+\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s})\right)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{P}(\mathbf{s}),

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\star}\propto\exp\left(\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s})+\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s})\right)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{P}(\mathbf{s}),

where
\mathbf{G}^{i}(\mathbf{s})
collects centered Gaussian terms,
\mathbf{D}^{i}(\mathbf{s})
collects deterministic terms in the respective Hamiltonians, and they satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s})+\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=H^{t,{q}}_{N+M}(\rho,\alpha)+(N+M)^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N+M}(\rho,\alpha),

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s})+\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{H}_{N}^{t,{q}}(\sigma,\alpha)+U(\sigma,\alpha,\tau)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha).

Again we understand that
H^{x}_{N+M}
and
H^{x}_{N}
are independent here.
For every
r\in[0,1]
, we define the interpolating Gibbs measure

\displaystyle\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{r}\propto\exp\left(\sqrt{1-r}\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s})+\sqrt{r}\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s})+(1-r)\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s})+r\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s})\right)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{P}(\mathbf{s}),

and we set
\varphi(r):=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\Theta\right\rangle_{r}
. We aim to show that
|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\varphi(r)|=o(1)
uniformly in
r
. Notice that
\Theta
depends on two independent copies of
\mathbf{s}
, which we denote by
\mathbf{s}^{1}
and
\mathbf{s}^{2}
. We also give ourselves
\mathbf{s}^{3}
and
\mathbf{s}^{4}
two additional independent copies of
\mathbf{s}
. We write the Gibbs measure in the definition of
\varphi(r)
explicitly as a ratio and differentiate to obtain that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\varphi(r)
is the
\mathbb{E}
-expectation of the
\langle\cdot\rangle_{r}
-covariance between
\Theta
and

\frac{1}{2\sqrt{r}}\big(\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s}^{1})+\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s}^{2})\big)-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{1-r}}\big(\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{1})+\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{2})\big)+\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s}^{1})+\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s}^{2})-\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{1})-\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s}^{2}).

We can rewrite this covariance as a Gibbs average by utilizing the additional variable
\mathbf{s}^{3}
. We next perform a Gaussian integration by parts as in e.g.
[
37
, Theorem 4.6]
to obtain a Gibbs average of the form

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\varphi(r)=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\Theta\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{4}c_{i,j}\mathbf{V}^{i,j}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}c_{i}\mathbf{U}^{i}\right)\right\rangle_{r},

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}^{i,j}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{G}^{1}\left(\mathbf{s}^{i}\right)\mathbf{G}^{1}\left(\mathbf{s}^{j}\right)-\mathbf{G}^{0}\left(\mathbf{s}^{i}\right)\mathbf{G}^{0}\left(\mathbf{s}^{j}\right)\right],\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbf{U}^{i}=\mathbf{D}^{1}\left(\mathbf{s}^{i}\right)-\mathbf{D}^{0}\left(\mathbf{s}^{i}\right),

and
(c_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{4}
and
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{3}
are absolute constants.
Then, we can use the estimates (
6.18
), (
6.19
), (
6.20
), and (
6.21
) to see that
|\mathbf{V}^{i,j}|
and
|\mathbf{U}^{i}|
are
O\left(M^{2}N^{-\frac{1}{16}}\right)
. Since
\Theta
is also bounded a.s., we deduce
|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\varphi(r)|=O\left(M^{2}N^{-\frac{1}{16}}\right)
uniformly in
r
. The desired result thus follows.
∎

6.2.3.
Other useful interpolation estimates

We now collect two more useful estimates that are obtained using similar arguments. Recall
\overline{F}^{x}_{N}(t,{q})
in (
6.6
) and
\widetilde{F}^{x}_{N}(t,{q})
in (
6.8
).

Lemma 6.4

(Control of perturbative part)
.

For each fixed
M\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{x}\left|\overline{F}^{x}_{N}(t,{q})-\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\right|=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{x}\left|\widetilde{F}^{x}_{N}(t,{q})-\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\right|=0,

where the suprema are over
x\in[0,3]^{\mathbb{N}^{4}\times\frac{D(D+1)}{2}}
.

Proof.

We use a similar interpolation as in the proof of Proposition
6.1
. We recall the normalizing factor
\frac{1}{N}
in the definition of the free energy.
The first convergence follows from the interpolation and the presence of
N^{-\frac{1}{16}}
in front of
H^{x}_{N}
. The second convergence follows from these facts and, additionally, the second line in (
6.19
) together with the boundedness of
\mathbb{E}\mathsf{Y}(\sigma)\mathsf{Y}(\sigma^{\prime})
.
∎

Lemma 6.5

(Regularity in
N
of the free energy)
.

There exists
C<+\infty
such that for every
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\geqslant 0
and
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}
,

\displaystyle\left|N\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})-(N+M)\overline{F}_{N+M}(t,{q})\right|\leqslant CM\left(t+|{q}|_{L^{1}}\right).

Proof.

Let us first assume that
{q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
.
As before, we write
\rho=(\sigma,\tau)
, and carry out an interpolation computation.
Let
\mathbf{s}:=(\rho,\alpha)
, and
\mathfrak{P}:=P_{N+M}\otimes\mathfrak{R}
.
We set

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s})+\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=H^{t,{q}}_{N+M}(\rho,\alpha),\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s})+\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s})=H^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)

where
\mathbf{G}^{i}(\mathbf{s})
collects centered Gaussian terms and
\mathbf{D}^{i}(\mathbf{s})
collects deterministic terms.
For every
r\in[0,1]
, we set

\displaystyle\varphi(r):=\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\left(\sqrt{1-r}\mathbf{G}^{0}(\mathbf{s})+(1-r)\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s})+\sqrt{r}\mathbf{G}^{1}(\mathbf{s})+r\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s})\right)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{P}(\mathbf{s}).

We have
\varphi(0)=(N+M)\overline{F}_{N+M}(t,{q})
and
\varphi(1)=N\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})
.
Again, using Gaussian integration by parts, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\varphi(r)=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s})-\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s}^{\prime})+\mathbf{D}^{1}(\mathbf{s})-\mathbf{D}^{0}(\mathbf{s})\right\rangle_{r}

for
\mathbf{V}
given in (
6.26
), and where
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{r}
is the corresponding Gibbs measure.
This time, we bound
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{D}^{1}-\mathbf{D}^{0}
using

\displaystyle Nt\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right)

\displaystyle=(N+M)t\xi\left(\frac{\rho\rho^{\prime\intercal}}{N+M}\right)+O(M)t,

\displaystyle{q}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}

\displaystyle={q}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\rho\rho^{\prime\intercal}+O\left(M\right)\left|{q}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|

uniformly in
N
. We get

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\varphi(r)\right|\leqslant O(M)\left(1+\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|{q}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|\right\rangle_{r}\right)=O(M)\left(t+|q|_{L^{1}}\right)

and the announced result follows for
{q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
. The full result follows by approximation using Proposition
3.1
.
∎

6.3.
Overlap approximations

Recall the notation
\approx_{\varepsilon}
in (
4.9
).

6.3.1.
Overlap approximations for the free-energy cavity calculation

We show that
A_{N}(x)
in (
6.14
) and
-M\mathscr{P}_{t,q}({p})
in (
6.15
) can be approximated by continuous functions of finitely many entries in the overlap arrays. Recall
R^{\leqslant n}_{N}
in (
6.10
),
Q^{\leqslant n}_{p}
in (
6.11
),
L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
in (
5.5
), and that
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
denotes the expectation with respect to the measure
\mathfrak{R}^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
and a bounded continuous function
F_{\varepsilon}:(\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}\times\mathbb{R}^{D\times D})^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
such that the following two properties hold:

•

A_{N}(x)\approx_{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{\varepsilon}\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{N},q\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{\alpha}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{N,x}
uniformly over
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in[0,3]^{\mathbb{N}^{4}\times\frac{D(D+1)}{2}}
;

•

-M\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})\approx_{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{\varepsilon}\left(Q^{\leqslant n}_{p},q\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{\alpha}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
uniformly over
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
.

In the above
q
acts entrywise (as
p
does in the definition of
Q_{p}
).

Proof.

We show that
A_{N}(x)
and
-M\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})
have similar overlap structures as defined in Definition
4.3
, which will allow us to apply Proposition
4.4
. Let
\Gamma
be a probability measure on some separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{X}
, and let
\mathcal{R}=(\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}):\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}\times\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function satisfying
|\mathcal{R}_{1}|,|\mathcal{R}_{2}|\leqslant 1
.

Assume that there are independent centered Gaussian processes indexed by
\rho\in\supp\Gamma
:

•

Z(\rho)=(Z_{1}(\rho),\dots,Z_{M}(\rho))
is an
M
-tuple of independent
\mathbb{R}^{D}
-valued centered Gaussian vectors
z_{i}(\rho)
with covariance
\mathbb{E}Z_{i}(\rho)Z_{i}(\rho^{\prime})^{\intercal}=\nabla\xi\left(\mathcal{R}_{1}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\right)
;

•

W(\rho)=(w_{1}(\rho),\dots,w_{M}(\rho))
is an
M
-tuple of independent
\mathbb{R}^{D}
-valued centered Gaussian vectors
w_{i}(\rho)
with covariance
\mathbb{E}w_{i}(\rho)w_{i}(\rho^{\prime})^{\intercal}={q}\left(\mathcal{R}_{2}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})\right)
;

•

Y(\rho)
is real-valued with covariance
\mathbb{E}Y(\rho)Y(\rho^{\prime})=M\theta(\mathcal{R}_{1}(\rho,\rho^{\prime}))
.

We also assume a stochastic continuity property of these processes so that we can construct jointly measurable versions of these as in Proposition
4.1
.
Writing
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\Gamma}
to denote the expectation with respect to
\Gamma^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
, we consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\log\bigg\langle\int\exp\Big(\sqrt{2t}Z(\rho)\cdot\tau-t\nabla\xi\left(\mathcal{R}_{1}(\rho,\rho)\right)\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt+\sqrt{2}W(\rho)\cdot\tau-{q}(\mathcal{R}_{2}(\rho,\rho))\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal}\Big)\mathrm{d}P_{M}(\tau)\bigg\rangle_{\Gamma},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\log\left\langle\exp\left(\sqrt{2t}Y(\rho)-tM\theta\left(\mathcal{R}_{1}(\rho,\rho)\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma},

where
\mathbb{E}
integrates the Gaussian randomness in
Z(\rho)
,
W(\rho)
and
Y(\rho)
.
Comparing with the expression for
A_{N}(x)
in (
6.14
), we see that
A_{N}(x)
is the average of
\mathcal{F}_{1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})-\mathcal{F}_{2}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})
with
(\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{N}},\alpha)
,
R_{N}
, and
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
substituted for
\rho
,
\mathcal{R}
, and
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\Gamma}
(this further averaging is with respect to the randomness intrinsic to
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
itself).
Using Lemma
6.2
, we see that
-M\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})
is the average of
\mathcal{F}_{1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})-\mathcal{F}_{2}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})
with
\alpha
,
Q_{p}
, and
\mathfrak{R}
substituted for
\rho
,
\mathcal{R}
, and
\Gamma
.

We will apply Proposition
4.4
to
\mathcal{F}_{1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})
. For
\mathcal{F}_{1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})
, the corresponding function
C
is
C_{1}:\mathcal{R}\mapsto 2t\nabla\xi(\mathcal{R}_{1})+2q(\mathcal{R}_{2})
. One can also identify the corresponding Lipschitz function
\mathbf{g}
as an integral in
\tau
. Note that there are
M
independent copies of the
\mathbb{R}^{D}
-valued processes here. Proposition
4.4
is still applicable and we can approximate

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})\approx_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{1,\varepsilon}(C_{1}(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n_{1}}))\right\rangle_{\Gamma}

for some
n_{1}
and some continuous
F_{1,\varepsilon}
, where
C_{1}
acts entrywise.

For
\mathcal{F}_{2}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})
, the corresponding function
C
is
C_{2}:\mathcal{R}\mapsto M\theta(\mathcal{R}_{1})
and
\mathbf{g}
is linear. Proposition
4.4
is directly applicable and we obtain the approximation

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})\approx_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{2,\varepsilon}(C_{2}(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n_{2}}))\right\rangle_{\Gamma}

for some
n_{2}
and some continuous
F_{2,\varepsilon}
, where
C_{2}
acts entrywise.

Since both
\nabla\xi
and
\theta
are continuous, we can absorb them into continuous functions to get

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})-\mathcal{F}_{1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{R})\approx_{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n}_{1},q(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n}_{2}))\right\rangle_{\Gamma}.

We emphasize that
q
cannot be absorbed because it is in general not continuous.
For simplicity, we also add the variable
\mathcal{R}_{2}
(which forms
\mathcal{R}
together with
\mathcal{R}_{1}
) and express the approximating function as
F_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n},q(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n}_{2}))
.
In view of (
6.10
) and (
6.11
), for
\mathcal{R}
being
R_{N}
or
Q_{p}
, we have
\mathcal{R}_{2}=R_{\alpha}
in both cases. Hence, we arrive at the approximations of the announced form.
∎

6.3.2.
Overlap approximations for the Gibbs-average cavity calculation

In the same fashion, we want to approximate
\mathbb{E}\left\langle g(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\star}
and
\mathbb{E}\left\langle g(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},{q}+t\nabla\xi({p})}
, where the two Gibbs measures are defined in (
6.16
) and (
6.17
), respectively. Also, recall the definition of
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
in (
6.9
), and that
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
denotes the expectation with respect to
\mathfrak{R}^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
.

Lemma 6.7
.

For every bounded continuous function
g:\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and every
\varepsilon>0
, there is
n\in\mathbb{N}
and a bounded continuous function
F_{\varepsilon}:(\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}\times\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}\times\mathbb{R})^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
such that the following two properties hold:

•

\mathbb{E}\left\langle g(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\star}\approx_{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{\varepsilon}\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{N},q\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{\alpha}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{N,x}
uniformly over
N,x
;

•

\mathbb{E}\left\langle g(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},{q}+t\nabla\xi({p})}\approx_{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{\varepsilon}\left(Q^{\leqslant n}_{p},q\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{\alpha}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
uniformly over
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
.

Again,
q
acts entrywise.

Proof.

Comparing the definition of
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{\star}_{N+M,x}
and that of
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right\rangle_{N+M,x}^{\star}

(6.28)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\frac{\left\langle\iint g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)e^{U(\sigma,\alpha,\tau)+U(\sigma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}P_{1}(\tau)\mathrm{d}P_{1}(\tau^{\prime})\right\rangle_{N,x}}{\left\langle\iint e^{U(\sigma,\alpha,\tau)+U(\sigma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}P_{1}(\tau)\mathrm{d}P_{1}(\tau^{\prime})\right\rangle_{N,x}}

for
U
given in (
6.13
).
Similarly, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},{q}+t\nabla\xi({p})}

(6.29)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\frac{\left\langle\iint g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)e^{V(\alpha,\tau)+V(\alpha^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}P_{1}(\tau)\mathrm{d}P_{1}(\tau^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}}{\left\langle\iint e^{V(\alpha,\tau)+V(\alpha^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}P_{1}(\tau)\mathrm{d}P_{2}(\tau^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}}

where

V(\alpha,\tau)
is in Lemma
6.2
and satisfies
V(\alpha,\tau){=}\sqrt{2t}Z(\alpha)\cdot\tau-t\nabla\xi\left({p}(1)\right)\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal}+\sqrt{2}W^{q}_{M}(\alpha)\cdot\tau-{q}(1)\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal}
.

In the formalism of Definition
4.3
, we can see that (
6.28
) and (
6.29
) are expressed in the same overlap and covariance structure. Here,
\Gamma
corresponds to
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
and
\mathfrak{R}
;
\mathcal{R}=(\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2})
(which is
\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times{D}}\times\mathbb{R}
-valued) corresponds to
R_{N}
and
Q_{p}
; and
C
is
\mathcal{R}\mapsto 2t\nabla\xi(\mathcal{R}_{1})+2q(\mathcal{R}_{2})
.

To apply Proposition
4.4
, let us first assume that
g
does not depend on
\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal}
. Then, we can identify the function
\mathbf{g}
as an integral in
\tau
and set
\mathbf{h}=g
so that both (
6.28
) and (
6.29
) can be rewritten as the left-hand side of (
4.11
). Applying Proposition
4.4
, we can approximate (
6.28
) and (
6.28
) by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(\mathcal{R}^{1,2}_{2}\right)F_{\varepsilon}\left(C(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n})\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma}

for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and continuous
F_{\varepsilon}
, where we used the observation that
\mathcal{R}_{2}=R_{\alpha}
for
\mathcal{R}
being either
R_{N}
or
Q_{p}
(see (
6.10
) and (
6.11
)). Since the functions
g
and
\nabla\xi
in the definition of
C
are continuous, we can absorb them into
F_{\varepsilon}
so that the above approximating term becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathcal{R}^{\leqslant n},q\left(\mathcal{R}_{2}^{\leqslant n}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\Gamma},

as announced. Since
q
is not continuous in general, it cannot be absorbed.

If
g
depends on
\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal}
, then Proposition
4.4
is not directly applicable. Nevertheless, we can easily modify the approximation argument in its proof to get the same result.
∎

6.4.
Ghirlanda–Guerra identities

Recall the notation of overlaps in (
6.10
).
We denote by
\mathbb{E}_{x}
the expectation under which
x
is an i.i.d. sequence of uniform random variables over
[1,2]
.
For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, integer
n\geqslant 2
,
h\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, and a bounded measurable function
\mathbf{f}:\left(\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times{D}}\times\mathbb{R}\right)^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
, we define, for
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle=\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
in (
6.9
),

(6.30)

\displaystyle\Delta^{x}_{N}(\mathbf{f},n,h)=\left|\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{f}\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{N}\right)R^{1,n+1}_{N,h}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{f}\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{N}\right)\right\rangle\mathbb{E}\left\langle R^{1,2}_{N,h}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{l=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{f}\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{N}\right)R^{1,l}_{N,h}\right\rangle\right|.

In (
6.30
) and throughout,
\mathbb{E}
integrates the Gaussian randomness in the Hamiltonian and the randomness in
\mathfrak{R}
. In particular, it does not integrate
x
.

Proposition 6.8
.

The following holds:

(1)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|R^{1,1}_{N,\sigma}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle R^{1,1}_{N,\sigma}\right\rangle_{N,x}\right|\right\rangle_{N,x}=0
;

(2)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\Delta^{x}_{N}(\mathbf{f},n,h)=0
for every
n
,
h
, and
\mathbf{f}
.

We start with a lemma which leads to part (
1
) of the proposition. We recall the notation for the coordinates of
x
in (
6.4
).

Lemma 6.9
.

There exists a constant
C<+\infty
not depending on
x
such that, for every
i\in\{1,\dots,D(D+1)/2\}
,

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\right\rangle\right|\right\rangle_{N,x}\mathrm{d}x_{i}\leqslant CN^{\frac{25}{32}}.

Proof.

We write
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle=\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
.
Fixing any
x
except for the entry
x_{i}
,
we set

\displaystyle\varphi(x_{i}):=\log\iint\exp\left(\widetilde{H}^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)+N^{-\frac{1}{16}}H^{x}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha)

and
\phi(x_{i}):=\mathbb{E}\varphi(x_{i})
.
We compute the derivatives:

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(x_{i})=N^{-\frac{1}{16}}\left\langle e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\right\rangle,\hskip 20.00003pt\varphi^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})=N^{-\frac{1}{8}}\left\langle(e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal})^{2}-\left\langle e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\right\rangle^{2}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(x_{i})=N^{-\frac{1}{16}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\right\rangle,\hskip 20.00003pt\phi^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})=N^{-\frac{1}{8}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle(e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal})^{2}-\left\langle e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\right\rangle^{2}\right\rangle.

Since
|e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}|\leqslant N
, we have, for all
x_{i}\in[0,3]
,

(6.31)

\displaystyle|\phi^{\prime}(x_{i})|\leqslant N^{\frac{15}{16}}.

Integrating
\phi^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})
over
x_{i}\in[1,2]
yields

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left\langle(e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal})^{2}-\left\langle e_{i}\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\right\rangle^{2}\right\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x_{i}=N^{\frac{1}{8}}(\phi^{\prime}(2)-\phi^{\prime}(1))\leqslant 2N^{\frac{17}{16}}.

By a concentration argument such as
[
77
, Theorem 1.2]
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{x}\mathbb{E}|\varphi-\mathbb{E}\varphi|\leqslant CN^{\frac{1}{2}}

for some constant
C<+\infty
. Defining

\displaystyle\delta:=|\varphi(x_{i}+y_{i})-\phi(x_{i}+y_{i})|+|\varphi(x_{i}-y_{i})-\phi(x_{i}-y_{i})|+|\varphi(x_{i})-\phi(x_{i})|,

we thus have
\int_{1}^{2}\delta\mathrm{d}x_{i}\leqslant CN^{\frac{1}{2}}
for all
y_{i}\in[0,1]
. By Hölder’s inequality (as in e.g.
[
37
, Exercise 2.3]
), the functions
\varphi
and
\phi
are convex. By
[
77
, Lemma 3.2]
, we can obtain from basic properties of convex functions that

\displaystyle|\varphi^{\prime}(x_{i})-\phi^{\prime}(x_{i})|\leqslant\phi^{\prime}(x_{i}+y_{i})-\phi^{\prime}(x_{i}-y_{i})+\frac{\delta}{y_{i}}.

Using (
6.31
) and the mean value theorem, we get

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{2}\left(\phi^{\prime}(x_{i}+y_{i})-\phi^{\prime}(x_{i}-y_{i})\right)\mathrm{d}x_{i}=\phi(2+y_{i})-\phi(2-y_{i})-\phi(1+y_{i})+\phi(1-y_{i})\leqslant 4N^{\frac{15}{16}}y_{i}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{2}\mathbb{E}|\varphi^{\prime}(x_{i})-\phi^{\prime}(x_{i})|\mathrm{d}x_{i}\leqslant 4N^{\frac{15}{16}}y_{i}+\frac{CN^{\frac{1}{2}}}{y_{i}}.

Inserting the expressions of
\varphi^{\prime}
and
\phi^{\prime}
and setting
y_{i}=N^{-\frac{7}{32}}
, we get the desired result.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.8
.

We write
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle=\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
.
The previous lemma implies that

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|e_{i}\cdot\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle e_{i}\cdot\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right\rangle\right|\right\rangle\mathrm{d}x_{i}\leqslant CN^{-\frac{7}{32}}

for every
i\in\{1,\ldots D(D+1)/2\}
.
Since
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{D(D+1)/2}
is an orthonormal basis, we deduce that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{\left[1,2\right]^{D(D+1)/2}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right\rangle\right|\right\rangle\mathrm{d}x=0,

which proves Proposition
6.8
(
1
).

To prove the second part, we proceed as in
[
77
, Theorem 3.3]
(with
h
,
N^{\frac{7}{16}}
,
N^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}x_{h}c_{h}H^{h}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)
substituted for
p
,
s
,
\nu_{N}(s)
,
g_{p}(\sigma)
therein) to get that for every
h
, there is a constant
C_{h}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|H^{h}_{N}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle H^{h}_{N}\right\rangle\right|\right\rangle\mathrm{d}x_{h}\leqslant C_{h}N^{\frac{3}{4}}.

Let
\mathbf{f}
be bounded and measurable and write
\mathbf{f}=\mathbf{f}(R^{\leqslant n})
. Without loss of generality, we assume
\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1
. Then

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{f}H^{h}_{N}\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{f}\right\rangle\mathbb{E}\left\langle H^{h}_{N}\right\rangle\right|\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|H^{h}_{N}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle H^{h}_{N}\right\rangle\right|\right\rangle.

For brevity, we omit the subscript
N
from overlaps.
By the Gaussian integration by parts in e.g.
[
37
, Theorem 4.6]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{f}H^{h}_{N}\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{f}\right\rangle\mathbb{E}\left\langle H^{h}_{N}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=x_{h}c_{h}N^{\frac{15}{16}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{f}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}R^{1,\ell}_{h}-nR^{1,n+1}_{h}\right)\right\rangle+\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{f}\right\rangle\mathbb{E}\left\langle R^{1,2}_{h}-R^{1,1}_{h}\right\rangle\right).

The above three displays yield

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left|n\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{f}R^{1,n+1}_{h}\right\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{f}\right\rangle\mathbb{E}\left\langle R^{1,2}\right\rangle-\sum_{\ell=2}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\mathbf{f}R^{1,\ell}_{h}\right\rangle\right|\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{x}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|R^{1,1}_{h}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle R^{1,1}_{h}\right\rangle\right|\right\rangle+C_{h}N^{-\frac{3}{16}}.

Since
R^{1,1}_{\alpha}=1
and
R^{1,1}_{\sigma}
is bounded, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}_{h}<+\infty
such that
\left|R^{1,1}_{h}-R^{2,2}_{h}\right|\leqslant C^{\prime}_{h}\left|R^{1,1}_{\sigma}-R^{2,2}_{\sigma}\right|
and thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|R^{1,1}_{h}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle R^{1,1}_{h}\right\rangle\right|\right\rangle\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|R^{1,1}_{h}-R^{2,2}_{h}\right|\right\rangle\leqslant C^{\prime}_{h}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|R^{1,1}_{\sigma}-R^{2,2}_{\sigma}\right|\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant 2C^{\prime}_{h}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|R^{1,1}_{\sigma}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle R^{1,1}_{\sigma}\right\rangle\right|\right\rangle.

Taking
\mathbb{E}_{x}
in this display, by Proposition
6.8
(
1
), we see that the right-hand side vanishes as
N\to\infty
.
Inserting this into the previous display, we get Proposition
6.8
(
2
).
∎

6.5.
Identifying the limits

Let
\Delta^{x}_{N}(\mathbf{f},n,h)
be as given in (
6.30
). We enumerate as
((\mathbf{f}_{j},n_{j},h_{j}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
all the triples
(\mathbf{f},n,h)
where
\mathbf{f}:\left(\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times{D}}\times\mathbb{R}\right)^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{R}
is a monomial (with coefficient
1
),
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
h\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
.
Then, we modify each
\mathbf{f}_{j}
in two steps:
first, since
R^{\leqslant n}
is bounded, we can adjust
\mathbf{f}_{j}
outside a bounded set to make it bounded;
secondly, we rescale
\mathbf{f}_{j}
to make sure that
\Delta^{x}_{N}(\mathbf{f}_{j},n_{j},h_{j})\leqslant 1
. For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in[0,3]^{\mathbb{N}^{4}\times\frac{D(D+1)}{2}}
, we set

(6.32)

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}(x):=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|R^{1,1}_{N,\sigma}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle R^{1,1}_{N,\sigma}\right\rangle_{N,x}\right|\right\rangle_{N,x}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j}\Delta^{x}_{N}(\mathbf{f}_{j},n_{j},h_{j}).

(6.33)

\displaystyle\mbox{We denote by $\mathcal{K}$ the convex hull of the set}\left\{\tau\tau^{\intercal}:\>\tau\in\supp P_{1}\right\}.

The set
\mathcal{K}
is closed, is such that
\mathcal{K}\subseteq S^{D}_{+}
, and we recall from (
3.12
) that we have
|a|\leqslant 1
for every
a\in\mathcal{K}
.
Recall the definitions of
R_{N}
in (
6.10
),
A_{N}(x)
in (
6.14
),
\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}
in (
6.15
),
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,k}
in (
6.9
),
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}^{\circ}
in (
6.7
), and
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},\pi}
in (
6.17
). Whenever we speak of the convergence in law of an overlap array, we always mean this in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions.

Proposition 6.10
.

We fix
M\in\mathbb{N}
and
(t,q)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, and suppose that there is a sequence
(N_{k},x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lim_{k\to\infty}N_{k}=+\infty
and
\lim_{k\to\infty}\Delta_{N_{k}}(x_{k})=0
.
Then, there are a subsequence
(N^{\prime}_{k},x^{\prime}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
, and
a\in\mathcal{K}
satisfying
a\geqslant{p}
such that

(1)

R_{N^{\prime}_{k}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N^{\prime}_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}}
converges in law to

\displaystyle\left(Q^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{p}\mathbf{1}_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}+(a,1)\mathbf{1}_{\ell=\ell^{\prime}}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}

under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
as
k
tends to infinity;

(2)

we have
\lim_{k\to\infty}A_{N^{\prime}_{k}}\left(x^{\prime}_{k}\right)=-M\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})
;

(3)

for every bounded continuous
g:\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times{D}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right\rangle_{N^{\prime}_{k}+M,x^{\prime}_{k}}^{\circ}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},{q}+t\nabla\xi({p})}.

In the statement of Proposition
6.10
, when we say that
(N_{k}^{\prime},x_{k}^{\prime})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a subsequence, we mean that
\lim_{k\to\infty}N_{k}^{\prime}=+\infty
and that
\{(N_{k}^{\prime},x_{k}^{\prime})\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}\subseteq\{(N_{k},x_{k})\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

We define the
quantile function

\kappa:[0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
of a real-valued random variable
X
as the right-continuous increasing function satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}g(X)=\int_{0}^{1}g(\kappa(s))\mathrm{d}s

for every bounded measurable function
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. We can obtain
\kappa
by taking the right-continuous inverse of the probability distribution function of
X
.
Consistently with (
4.3
), we also set
\kappa(1):=\lim_{s\nearrow 1}\kappa(s)
whenever
\kappa
is bounded.

Proof of Proposition
6.10
.

We denote by
R^{\mathrm{diag}}_{N}:=\left(R^{\ell,\ell}_{N}\right)_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
the diagonal part consisting of self-overlaps, and by
R^{\mathrm{off}}_{N}:=\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{N}\right)_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
the off-diagonal part. We will use similar notation for the diagonal and off-diagonal parts of other overlap arrays.

Part (
1
)
.
Since
R^{1,1}_{N,\sigma}\in S^{D}_{+}
and
\left|R^{1,1}_{N,\sigma}\right|\leqslant 1
, we can extract a subsequence
\left(N^{\prime}_{k},\,x^{\prime}_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
from
\left(N_{k},\,x_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

(6.34)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\langle R^{1,1}_{N^{\prime}_{k},\sigma}\right\rangle_{x^{\prime}_{k}}=z

for some
z\in S^{D}_{+}
.
Since each
R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{N}
is bounded, by passing to a further subsequence, we can assume that

(6.35)

R_{N^{\prime}_{k}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N^{\prime}_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}}
converges in law to
R_{\infty}
as
k
tends to infinity,

for some random array
R_{\infty}
(and in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions).
We will show that the law of
R_{\infty}
has a natural representation under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
.
We write
R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}:=\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty,\sigma},R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty,\alpha}\right)
where
R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty,\bullet}
is the limit of
R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{N,\bullet}
with
\bullet
being
\sigma
or
\alpha
. For
R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}
, the subscripts
\sigma
and
\alpha
are purely symbolic.

First, we determine
R^{\mathrm{diag}}_{\infty}
.
Due to the first term on the right of (
6.32
), the assumption
\lim_{k\to\infty}\Delta_{N_{k}}\left(x^{N_{k}}\right)=0
ensures the concentration of
R^{\ell,\ell}_{N,\sigma}
.
Also, it is clear from
R^{\ell,\ell}_{N,\alpha}=1
that
R^{\ell,\ell}_{\infty,\alpha}=1
, which together with (
6.34
) yields that
R_{\infty}^{\mathrm{diag}}
is deterministic and

(6.36)

\displaystyle R_{\infty}^{\ell,\ell}=(z,1)\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\ell\in\mathbb{N}.

Next, we determine
R^{\mathrm{off}}_{\infty}
.
The same assumption on
\Delta_{N_{k}}(x^{N_{k}})
also ensures that
R_{\infty}
satisfies the Ghirlanda–Guerra identities. Panchenko’s synchronization result
[
82
, Theorem 4]
guarantees the existence of a Lipschitz function
\Psi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to S^{D}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying
\Psi(s)\geqslant\Psi(s^{\prime})
for all
s\geqslant s^{\prime}
such that

(6.37)

\displaystyle R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}=\Psi\left(\tr\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}\right)\right)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{a.s.}\ \forall\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N},

where we used the shorthand
\tr\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}\right):=\tr\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty,\sigma}\right)+R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty,\alpha}
.

To proceed, we determine the distribution of
\tr\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}\right)
.
We denote the quantile function of
\tr\left(R^{1,2}_{\infty}\right)
by
\kappa
. The assumption
\lim_{k\to\infty}\Delta_{N_{k}}\left(x^{N_{k}}\right)=0
implies that the Ghirlanda–Guerra identities hold for
\left(\tr\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}\right)\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}
. Using the Dovbysh–Sudakov representation in
[
77
, Theorem 1.7]
together with the characterization of Poisson–Dirichlet cascades by the overlap distribution in
[
77
, Theorems 2.13 and 2.17]
, we deduce that
\left(\tr\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}\right)\right)_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
has the same law as the overlap array associated with a Poisson–Dirichlet cascade, and this cascade must be such that the quantile function of one overlap is
\kappa
.
Using this and (
6.36
), we set
r:=\tr(z)+1
so that

(6.38)

\displaystyle\tr\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}\right)=\kappa\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}+r\mathbf{1}_{\ell=\ell^{\prime}},

where
\left(\alpha^{\ell}\right)_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
is sampled from
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
. To be precise, we have argued that the two sides of (
6.38
) have the same law, jointly over
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, where the law of the right-hand side is under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
. Since so far we had only specified the law of
R_{\infty}
but not its realization itself, we may as well choose the probability space to be that for
\mathbb{E}\langle\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
and realize
\tr\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}\right)
according to (
6.38
); the full array
R_{\infty}
is then specified according to (
6.37
), and we get the representation

(6.39)

\displaystyle R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}=\Psi\left(\kappa\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}+r\mathbf{1}_{\ell=\ell^{\prime}}\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}.

In order to simplify (
6.39
), we write
\Psi=:(\Psi_{\sigma},\Psi_{\alpha})
so that
\Psi_{\sigma}
is
S^{D}_{+}
-valued and
\Psi_{\alpha}
is
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-valued.
By the invariance of Poisson–Dirichlet cascades from Proposition
4.8
, we have that
R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{N,\alpha}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
has the same law as
\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
. Therefore,
R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty,\alpha}
must also have the same law, which means that
\Psi_{\alpha}(\kappa(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}))
is uniformly distributed over
[0,1]
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
for
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}
. Since
\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}
is itself uniform over
[0,1]
and
\Psi\circ\kappa
is increasing and right-continuous, we must have

\displaystyle\Psi_{\alpha}(\kappa(s))=s,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall s\in[0,1].

Thus, we can simplify (
6.39
) into

(6.40)

\displaystyle R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}=\bigg(\Psi_{\sigma}\left(\kappa\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}+r\mathbf{1}_{\ell=\ell^{\prime}}\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\bigg),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}.

Before concluding, we show that

(6.41)

\displaystyle\kappa(s)\leqslant r,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall s\in[0,1].

Note that (
6.37
) implies that
R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty,\sigma}\in S^{D}_{+}
for
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}
. The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality implies that
v\cdot R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{N,\sigma}v\leqslant\frac{1}{2}v\cdot\left(R^{\ell,\ell}_{N,\sigma}+R^{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}_{N,\sigma}\right)v
for every
v\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
, which yields

(6.42)

\displaystyle R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty,\sigma}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left(R^{\ell,\ell}_{\infty,\sigma}+R^{\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty,\sigma}\right).

Taking the trace on both sides of (
6.42
) and using (
6.38
), we get

\displaystyle\kappa\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\leqslant r.

Since
\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}
is uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
for
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}
and
\kappa
is right-continuous, we arrive at (
6.41
).

We set
{p}:=\Psi_{\sigma}\circ\kappa
and
a:=\Psi_{\sigma}(r)
.
Since
a
is the subsequential limit of
\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}
, we have that
a
belongs to the set
\mathcal{K}
defined in (
6.33
), and in particular
a\in S^{D}_{+}
and
|a|\leqslant 1
. Since the function
\Psi_{\sigma}
is increasing, the inequality (
6.41
) implies that
{p}\leqslant a
. Since
\kappa
, as a quantile function, is also increasing, we have
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}
.
Notice that, for
y,y^{\prime}\in S^{D}_{+}
satisfying
y\geqslant y^{\prime}
, we have
(y+y^{\prime})\cdot(y-y^{\prime})\geqslant 0
and thus
|y|^{2}\geqslant|y^{\prime}|^{2}
. This along with
{p}\leqslant a
gives
|{p}|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant|a|\leqslant 1
.
We have thus verified the conditions on
{p}
and
a
stated in the proposition.
Part (
1
) follows from (
6.35
) and the representation in (
6.40
).

Part (
2
)
.
Due to the possibility that
r>\kappa(1)
, in general, a representation of
R_{\infty}
as a Poisson–Dirichlet cascade might be off on the diagonal.
To circumvent this, we build a sequence of Poisson–Dirichlet cascades whose overlap arrays approximate
R_{\infty}
arbitrarily closely.
Allowed by (
6.41
), we can
choose a sequence
(\kappa_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
of right-continuous increasing
step functions from
[0,1)
to
[0,r]
that converges to
\kappa
in
L^{1}([0,1);\mathbb{R})
as
m
tends to infinity. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we can also impose that
\lim_{s\nearrow 1}\kappa_{m}(s)=r
while preserving the properties mentioned in the previous sentence. As usual, we set
\kappa_{m}(1):=\lim_{s\nearrow 1}\kappa_{m}(s)
, and thus have
\kappa_{m}(1)=r
.
Setting
{p}_{m}:=\Psi_{\sigma}\circ\kappa_{m}
, we have that
{p}_{m}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
and

(6.43)

\displaystyle{p}_{m}(1)=\Psi_{\sigma}(r),\hskip 20.00003pt\lim_{m\to\infty}\left|{p}_{m}-{p}\right|_{L^{1}}=0.

For each
m
, we consider
Q_{{p}_{m}}
defined in (
6.11
). Notice that, from our choice of
p
and (
6.40
), we have
\left(Q^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{p}\right)_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}=\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}\right)_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
.

By the second property in (
6.43
) and the continuity of Poisson–Dirichlet cascades in the overlap distribution (
[
37
, Corollary 5.32]
or
[
77
, Theorem 2.17]
), we deduce that
\left(Q^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{{p}_{m}}\right)_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
converges in law to
\left(Q^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{p}\right)_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}=\left(R^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{\infty}\right)_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
. Due to the first property in (
6.43
), we have
Q^{\ell,\ell}_{{p}_{m}}=R^{\ell,\ell}_{\infty}
, which is deterministic. We thus obtain that, under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
,

(6.44)

Q_{{p}_{m}}
converges in law to
R_{\infty}
as
m
tends to infinity.

In order to proceed, we would like to improve upon the convergences in (
6.35
) and (
6.44
) into

(6.45)

\mbox{$\big(R_{N^{\prime}_{k}},q(R_{\alpha})\big)$ under $\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N^{\prime}_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}}$ converges in law }\\
\mbox{to $\big(R_{\infty},q(R_{\alpha})\big)$ under $\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}$ as $k$ tends to infinity,}

and, under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
,

(6.46)

\big(Q_{p_{m}},q(R_{\alpha})\big)
converges in law to
\big(R_{\infty},q(R_{\alpha})\big)
as
m
tends to infinity.

These convergences can be obtained by appealing to the continuous mapping theorem as in the proof of Proposition
4.8
: in short, the off-diagonal elements of
R_{\alpha}
are uniformly distributed over
[0,1]
, so they have probability zero to belong to the set of points of discontinuity of
q
.

We now fix any
\varepsilon>0
and recall the notation
\approx_{\varepsilon}
from (
4.9
).
For the function
F_{\varepsilon}
given by Lemma
6.6
and using the convergences in law in (
6.45
) and (
6.46
), we have for sufficiently large
k
and
m
that

\displaystyle A_{N^{\prime}_{k}}\left(x^{\prime}_{k}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{l.approx_finite_overlap}}}{{\approx_{\varepsilon}}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{\varepsilon}\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{N^{\prime}_{k}},q\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{\alpha}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{N^{\prime}_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e.joint_cvg_in_law_1}}}{{\approx_{\varepsilon}}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{\varepsilon}\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{\infty},q\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{\sigma}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e.joint_cvg_in_law_2}}}{{\approx_{\varepsilon}}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{\varepsilon}\left(Q^{\leqslant n}_{{p}_{m}},q\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{\alpha}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{l.approx_finite_overlap}}}{{\approx_{\varepsilon}}}-M\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p}_{m}).

The symbol
\approx_{\varepsilon}
does not define a transitive relation, and cumulatively we obtain an error of
4\varepsilon
between the first and the last term in the string of approximate equalities.
Recall the expression of
\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}
in (
6.15
).
These along with (
6.43
) and the Lipschitzness of
\psi
from Corollary
5.2
imply
\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p}_{m})=\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})
.
Hence, sending
k
and
m
to infinity, we get

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\to\infty}A_{N^{\prime}_{k}}\left(x^{\prime}_{k}\right)\approx_{4\varepsilon}-M\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})\approx_{4\varepsilon}\limsup_{k\to\infty}A_{N^{\prime}_{k}}\left(x^{\prime}_{k}\right).

Sending
\varepsilon
to zero, we obtain part (
2
).

Part (
3
)
.
Recall
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{\star}_{N+M,x}
in (
6.16
).
For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
{p}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, we write

\displaystyle r(k):=\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right\rangle_{N^{\prime}_{k}+M,x^{\prime}_{k}}^{\star},\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{r}\left({p}^{\prime}\right):=\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},{q}+t\nabla\xi({p}^{\prime})}.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, we denote by
F_{\varepsilon}
the function given by Lemma
6.7
and we use (
6.45
) and (
6.46
) to get that, for every sufficiently large
k
and
m
,

\displaystyle r(k)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{l.approx_finite_overlap_2}}}{{\approx_{\varepsilon}}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{\varepsilon}\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{N^{\prime}_{k}},q\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{\alpha}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{N^{\prime}_{k},x^{\prime}_{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e.joint_cvg_in_law_1}}}{{\approx_{\varepsilon}}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{\varepsilon}\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{\infty},q\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{\alpha}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{e.joint_cvg_in_law_2}}}{{\approx_{\varepsilon}}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle F_{\varepsilon}\left(Q^{\leqslant n}_{{p}_{m}},q\left(R^{\leqslant n}_{\alpha}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{l.approx_finite_overlap_2}}}{{\approx_{\varepsilon}}}\mathfrak{r}\left({p}_{m}\right).

Using the second property in (
6.43
) and a computation similar to the one in (
5.11
), we have
\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathfrak{r}\left({p}_{m}\right)=\mathfrak{r}({p})
. Therefore, sending
m
and
k
to infinity and then
\varepsilon
to zero, we obtain that
\lim_{k\to\infty}r(k)=\mathfrak{r}({p})
. Lemma
6.3
allows us to replace
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{\star}_{N^{\prime}_{k}+M,x^{\prime}_{k}}
in
r(k)
by
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{\circ}_{N^{\prime}_{k}+M,x^{\prime}_{k}}
in the limit. Hence, part (
3
) follows.
∎

6.6.
Corollaries

We now combine Propositions
6.8
and
6.10
. Recall the definition of
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
in (
6.7
), and that
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
denotes the expectation with respect to
\mathfrak{R}^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
. Recall also
\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}
from (
6.15
) and the definitions of the overlaps in (
6.10
) and (
6.11
).

Corollary 6.11
.

For every
(t,{q})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, there are sequences
(N^{\pm}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of increasing integers,
(x_{k}^{\pm})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
{p}_{\pm}\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
, and
a_{\pm}\in\mathcal{K}
satisfying
a_{\pm}\geqslant p_{\pm}
such that

(1)

R_{N^{\pm}_{k}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N^{\pm}_{k},x^{\pm}_{k}}
converges in law to

\displaystyle\left(Q^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{{p}_{\pm}}\mathbf{1}_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}+(a_{\pm},1)\mathbf{1}_{\ell=\ell^{\prime}}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}

under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
as
k
tends to infinity;

(2)

we have

\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p}_{-})\leqslant\liminf_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\leqslant\limsup_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\leqslant\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p}_{+}).

Proof.

Using Lemma
6.4
and Proposition
6.1
with
M=1
, we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})=\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\overline{F}_{N}^{x}(t,{q})\geqslant-\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}A_{N}(x).

Lemmas
6.4
and
6.5
imply that
\sup_{N,x}|A_{N}(x)|\leqslant c
for some constant
c
.
Proposition
6.8
and (
6.32
) yield that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\Delta_{N}(x)=0
. Using these two facts and following the argument in
[
77
, Lemma 3.3]
, we can find a sequence
(x_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying
\lim_{N\to\infty}\Delta_{N}(x_{N})=0
and
A_{N}(x_{N})\geqslant\mathbb{E}_{x}A_{N}(x)+o(1)
as
N
tends to infinity.

We remark that
[
77
, Lemma 3.3]
is originally stated to get an upper bound for
A_{N}(x_{N})
. Examining the proof, one can see that as long as
A_{N}(x)
is bounded uniformly, the argument still works for the other side needed here. Hence,

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\geqslant-\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{N}(x_{N}).

We extract
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
along which the
\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{N}(x_{N})
is achieved.
By Proposition
6.10
at
M=1
, we can find a subsequence
(N^{-}_{k},\,x^{-}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of
(N_{k},\,x_{N_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
{p}_{-}\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}
such that part (
1
) holds and the first inequality in part (
2
) holds.
The other half follows from a similar argument.
∎

Recall
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
in (
6.7
) and
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},\pi}
in (
6.17
).

Corollary 6.12
.

For any
M\in\mathbb{N}
, any
(t,{q})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty})
, and any sequence of increasing integers, there are a subsequence
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
, and
a\in\mathcal{K}
satisfying
a\geqslant p
such that

(1)

R_{N_{k}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N_{k},x_{k}}
converges in law to

\displaystyle\left(Q^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}_{p}\mathbf{1}_{\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}+(a,1)\mathbf{1}_{\ell=\ell^{\prime}}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}

under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
as
k
tends to infinity;

(2)

for every bounded continuous
g:\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times{D}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right\rangle_{N_{k}+M,x_{k}}^{\circ}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal},\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},{q}+t\nabla\xi({p})}.

Proof.

Recall
\Delta_{N}(x)
in (
6.32
).
By Proposition
6.8
, we have
\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\Delta_{N}(x)=0
. As discussed in the previous proof, we can find
(x_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\Delta_{N}(x_{N})=0
. The desired result then follows from Proposition
6.10
.
∎

7.
Proofs of the main results

We now leverage the results of the previous section to complete the proofs of our main results. We first show Theorem
1.3
as well as the fact that any subsequential limit of the free energy
\overline{F}_{N}
must solve the equation “almost everywhere”, as was announced in (
1.23
). We then deduce Theorem
1.2
by a continuity argument. We next identify, for “almost every” choice of
q
, the limit law of the array of the conditional expectations of the
\sigma
-overlaps given the
\alpha
-overlaps. Up to the additional perturbation provided by the parameter
\widehat{t}
introduced around (
1.20
), we can then slightly upgrade this result and thereby obtain a proof of Theorem
1.4
.

7.1.
Critical point identification

Recall the definition of
\mathscr{P}_{t,q}
in (
6.15
). In the next proposition, we assume the convergence of the entire sequence
\left(\overline{F}_{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Suppose that
\left(\overline{F}_{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges pointwise to some limit
f
, and let
t\geqslant 0
and
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
. If
f(t,\cdot)
is Gateaux differentiable at
{q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
, then
f(t,{q})=\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}(\partial_{q}f(t,{q}))
.

Proof.

Let
(N^{\pm}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(x^{\pm}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, and
{p}_{\pm}
be given by Corollary
6.11
, whose part (
2
) implies

(7.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p}_{-})\leqslant f(t,{q})\leqslant\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p}_{+}).

Recall the definitions of
\overline{F}^{x}_{N}
in (
6.6
) and of
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
in (
6.9
). We set
\overline{F}_{k}^{\pm}:=\overline{F}^{x^{\pm}_{k}}_{N^{\pm}_{k}}
.
For every continuous
\kappa\in L^{\infty}
, by a computation similar to that for (
5.7
), we have, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{k}^{\pm}(t,{q})\right\rangle_{{L^{2}}}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N^{\pm}_{k}}\right\rangle_{N^{\pm}_{k},x^{\pm}_{k}}.

By Lemma
6.4
, the function
\left(\overline{F}_{k}^{\pm}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
also converges pointwise to
f
as
k
tends to infinity. Following the proof of Proposition
5.4
verbatim, we obtain that
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{k}^{\pm}(t,{q})
converges to
\partial_{q}f(t,{q})
in
L^{2}
.
Using this and Corollary
6.11
(
1
) yields that

\displaystyle\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}f(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(R^{1,2}_{\alpha}\right)\cdot{p}_{\pm}(R^{1,2}_{\alpha})\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}=\int_{0}^{1}\kappa(s)\cdot{p}_{\pm}(s)\mathrm{d}s.

Since the set of continuous
\kappa\in L^{\infty}
is dense in
{L^{2}}
, we have
{p}_{\pm}=\partial_{q}f(t,q)
, which along with (
7.1
) gives the desired result.
∎
