In the following result, the limit is subsequential.

Proposition 7.2
.

Suppose that
\left(\overline{F}_{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges pointwise to some limit
f
along a subsequence
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, and let
t\geqslant 0
.
If
f(t,\cdot)
is Gateaux differentiable at some
{q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
, then

(7.2)

\partial_{q}f(t,{q})=\partial_{q}\psi({q}+t\nabla\xi(\partial_{q}f(t,{q}))).

If, in addition,
t>0
and
f(\cdot,q)
is differentiable at
t
, then

(7.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f(t,{q})=\int_{0}^{1}\xi(\partial_{q}f(t,{q})).

Proof.

By passing to a further subsequence, we may assume that
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is the subsequence given in Corollary
6.12
(for
M=1
) along with
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
.

Recall
\widetilde{F}_{N}^{x}
in (
6.8
) and
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,x}
in (
6.9
).
As in the proof of (
5.7
), we have for any bounded continuous
\kappa:[0,1]\to S^{D}
that

(7.4)

\displaystyle\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}\widetilde{F}_{N_{k}}^{x_{k}}(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N_{k}}\right\rangle_{N_{k},x_{k}},\hskip 10.00002pt\partial_{t}\widetilde{F}_{N_{k}}^{x_{k}}(t,{q})=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N_{k}}\right)\right\rangle_{N_{k},x_{k}}.

By Lemma
6.4
, we have that
\widetilde{F}_{N_{k}}^{x_{k}}
converges pointwise to
f
.
Following the proof of Proposition
5.4
, we deduce that
\partial_{q}\widetilde{F}_{N_{k}}^{x_{k}}(t,{q})
converges to
\partial_{q}f(t,q)
in
L^{2}
. Using also the convergence of the overlaps from Corollary
6.12
(
1
), we let
k
tend to infinity in the first relation in (
7.4
) to get that

\displaystyle\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}f(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot{p}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}=\left\langle\kappa,{p}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Since the set of bounded continuous functions spans
L^{2}
, we deduce that

(7.5)

\displaystyle{p}=\partial_{q}f(t,{q}).

Under the additional assumption on the differentiability in
t
(and an adaptation to
\widetilde{F}_{N_{k}}^{x_{k}}
of the part of Proposition
5.4
concerning the derivative in
t
), we can pass to the limit in the second relation in (
7.4
) and get that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f(t,{q})=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\xi\left({p}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}=\int_{0}^{1}\xi({p}(u))\mathrm{d}u.

This and (
7.5
) yield (
7.3
).

To obtain (
7.2
), we relate
{p}
to the derivative of
\psi
. Recall
\overline{F}_{N}^{x}
in (
6.6
) and
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle^{\circ}_{N,x}
in (
6.7
).
By Lemmas
6.4
and
6.5
, we have that
\overline{F}_{N_{k}+1}^{x_{k}}
converges pointwise to
f
. Following the proof of (
5.7
), we have for any bounded continuous
\kappa
that

\displaystyle\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N_{k}+1}^{x_{k}}(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\frac{\rho\rho^{\prime\intercal}}{N_{k}+1}\right\rangle_{N_{k}+1,x_{k}}^{\circ}.

We write
\rho
as an
(N_{k}+1)
-tuple of
D
-dimensional coordinates
\rho=\left(\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{N_{k}},\rho_{N_{k}+1}\right)
, which are i.i.d. under
P_{N_{k}+1}=P_{1}^{\otimes(N_{k}+1)}
. Since the Hamiltonian in
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N_{k}+1,x_{k}}^{\circ}
only depends on
\rho\rho^{\intercal}
and
\rho\rho^{\prime\intercal}
, we deduce that
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N_{k}+1,x_{k}}^{\circ}
is invariant under permutations of the coordinates of
\rho
. Therefore, writing
\sigma:=(\rho_{1},\dots\rho_{N_{k}})
and
\tau:=\rho_{N_{k}+1}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N_{k}+1}^{x_{k}}(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal}\right\rangle_{N_{k}+1,x_{k}}^{\circ}.

By an adaptation of Proposition
5.4
, we have that
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N_{k}+1}^{x_{k}}(t,{q})
converges in
L^{2}
to
\partial_{q}f(t,q)
. Using also Corollary
6.12
(
2
) (with
M=1
), we can pass to the limit in the previous display and get

\displaystyle\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}f(t,{q})\right\rangle_{L^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\tau\tau^{\prime\intercal}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},q+t\nabla\xi({p})},

where
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},q+t\nabla\xi({p})}
is given in (
6.17
) with
M=1
. Notice that since
M=1
, the Gibbs measure
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R},{q}+t\nabla\xi({p})}
is the same as the Gibbs measure
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{{q}+t\nabla\xi({p})}
defined in (
5.20
).
By (
5.22
), the right-hand side in the previous display is equal to
\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}\psi({q}+t\nabla\xi({p}))\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
. Combining this with (
7.5
) yields that

(7.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{q}\psi({q}+t\nabla\xi({p}))={p}.

In conclusion, (
7.2
) follows from (
7.5
) and (
7.6
).
∎

Recall
\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}
in (
6.15
),
\theta
in (
6.12
), and
\mathcal{J}_{t,{q}}
in (
1.7
). We have

(7.7)

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})=\mathcal{J}_{t,{q}}\left({q}+t\nabla\xi({p}),{p}\right).

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Let
{p}
and
{q^{\prime}}
be as given in the statement. The relation (
7.2
) in Proposition
7.2
implies that
({q^{\prime}},p)
is a critical point.
The convergence in (
1.17
) follows from Proposition
7.1
and (
7.7
).
∎

Proposition
7.2
also proves that any subsequential limit of the free energy must satisfy the Hamilton–Jacobi equation (
1.23
) at every point of differentiability of the limit. We recall that this limit function must be Gateaux differentiable jointly in its two variables “almost everywhere”, by Proposition
5.3
.

7.2.
Critical point representation

Proposition 7.3
.

Suppose that
\left(\overline{F}_{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges pointwise to some limit
f
.
For every
(t,{q})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, there is
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
such that

(7.8)

\displaystyle f(t,{q})=\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p}),\hskip 20.00003pt{p}=\partial_{q}\psi({q}+t\nabla\xi({p})).

Proof.

Proposition
5.3
yields a sequence
({q}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
convergent to
{q}
in
{L^{2}}
such that
f(t,\cdot)
is Gateaux differentiable at every
{q}_{n}
. By the continuity of
f
given there, we have

(7.9)

\displaystyle f(t,{q})=\lim_{n\to\infty}f(t,{q}_{n}).

We write
{p}_{n}:=\partial_{q}f(t,{q}_{n})
. Proposition
5.3
also implies that
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}|{p}_{n}|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 1
.
By passing to a subsequence, we can assume that
{p}_{n}
converges weakly in
{L^{2}}
to some limit
{p}
. Arguing as in Step 3 of the proof of Proposition
5.4
, we can upgrade this to the strong convergence in
L^{2}
, and we also see that
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
.

Recall
\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}
in (
6.15
). By Corollary
5.2
and the local Lipschitzness of
\nabla\xi
and
\theta
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}_{n}}({p}_{n})-\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|{q}_{n}-{q}\right|_{L^{1}}+t\left|\nabla\xi({p}_{n})-\nabla\xi({p})\right|_{L^{1}}+t\left|\theta({p}_{n})-\theta({p})\right|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leqslant Ct\left|{p}_{n}-{p}\right|_{L^{1}}+\left|{q}_{n}-{q}\right|_{L^{1}},

for some constant
C
depending only on
\xi
. By Jensen’s inequality, we have that
q_{n}
and
p_{n}
converge in
L^{1}
to
q
and
p
respectively as
n
tends to infinity, and therefore

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}_{n}}({p}_{n})=\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p}).

By construction, the function
f(t,\cdot)
is Gateaux differentiable at
{q}_{n}
. Proposition
7.1
therefore ensures that

f(t,{q}_{n})=\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}_{n}}({p}_{n}).

These along with (
7.9
) yields
f(t,{q})=\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})
, which gives the first relation in (
7.8
).

Proposition
7.2
ensures that
{p}_{n}=\partial_{q}\psi({q}_{n}+t\nabla\xi({p}_{n}))
.
Using (
5.23
) and the local Lipschitzness of
\nabla\xi
, we get

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{q}\psi({q}_{n}+t\nabla\xi({p}_{n}))-\partial_{q}\psi({q}+t\nabla\xi({p}))\right|_{L^{2}}\leqslant C|{q}_{n}-{q}|_{L^{2}}+C|{p}_{n}-{p}|_{L^{2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall n\in\mathbb{N},

for some
C
only depending on
\xi
. Sending
n
to infinity, we get
{p}=\partial_{q}\psi({q}+t\nabla\xi({p}))
, which gives the second relation in (
7.8
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Let
{p}
be given in Proposition
7.3
and set
{q^{\prime}}:={q}+t\nabla\xi({p})
. The second relation in (
7.8
) ensures that
({q^{\prime}},p)
is a critical point. The first relation in (
7.8
) along with (
7.7
) yields (
1.17
).
∎

7.3.
Convergence of the overlap

7.3.1.
Convergence of the conditional overlap

Recall the Gibbs measure
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
associated with the original free energy given in (
5.4
).
For
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider the conditional expectation of the spin overlap

\displaystyle R_{N,\sigma|\alpha}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}:=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\frac{\sigma^{\ell}\left(\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)^{\intercal}}{N}\,\Big|\,\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{N},

where the conditioning is with respect to
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
(not
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
). We also recall that
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
is the expectation with respect to the measure
\mathfrak{R}^{\otimes\mathbb{N}}
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Suppose that
\left(\overline{F}_{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges pointwise to some limit
f
along a subsequence
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, and let
t\geqslant 0
.
If
f(t,\cdot)
is Gateaux differentiable at some
{q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
, then
\left(R_{N_{k},\sigma|\alpha}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}:\>\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N_{k}}
converges in law to
\left({p}\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}:\>\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
as
k
tends to infinity, where
{p}=\partial_{q}f(t,q)
.

Proof.

We write
{p}_{N}:=\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}\left(t,{q}\right)
.
Using the invariance of the Poisson–Dirichlet cascade in Proposition
4.8
, the computation of
\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}\left(t,{q}\right)
in (
5.7
), and the definition of conditional expectation, we have the following identities, for any bounded continuous
\kappa:[0,1]\to S^{D}
:

\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot{p}_{N}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right\rangle_{N}=\int_{0}^{1}\kappa(s)\cdot{p}_{N}(s)\mathrm{d}s=\left\langle\kappa,\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}\left(t,{q}\right)\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\\
=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right\rangle_{N}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\langle\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\,\Big|\,\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right\rangle_{N}\right\rangle_{N}.

Hence, we deduce that

(7.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\,\Big|\,\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right\rangle_{N}={p}_{N}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)

a.s. under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
. For every
{p}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, we write
O_{{p}^{\prime}}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}:={p}^{\prime}\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)
,
so that under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
,

(7.11)

\displaystyle R_{N,\sigma|\alpha}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}=O_{{p}_{N}}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}:\>\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}.

Proposition
5.4
ensures that
{p}_{N}
converges to
{p}
in
L^{1}
along the subsequence, and thus

(7.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|{p}_{N}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)-{p}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}=\left|{p}_{N}-{p}\right|_{L^{1}}

tends to zero as
N
tends to infinity along the subsequence.
We fix any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any bounded Lipschitz
g:\left(\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times{D}}\times\mathbb{R}\right)^{n^{2}-n}\to\mathbb{R}
. We write
R^{\square}_{N,\sigma|\alpha}:=\left(R_{N,\sigma|\alpha}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n;\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
and similarly for
O^{\square}_{{p}_{N}}
and
O^{\square}_{p}
.
The invariance of the Poisson–Dirichlet cascade from Proposition
4.8
implies that
O_{{p}_{N}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
has the same distribution as
O_{{p}_{N}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
.
Combining this with (
7.11
) yields that

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(R^{\square}_{N,\sigma|\alpha}\right)\right\rangle_{N}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(O^{\square}_{p}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}\right|=\left|\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(O^{\square}_{{p}_{N}}\right)\right\rangle_{N}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(O^{\square}_{p}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(O^{\square}_{{p}_{N}}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(O^{\square}_{p}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}\right|\leqslant\|g\|_{\mathrm{Lip}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|O^{\square}_{{p}_{N}}-O^{\square}_{p}\right|\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}.

Using also that (
7.12
) tends to zero along the subsequence therefore yields the announced result.
∎

7.3.2.
Convergence of the overlap under perturbation

Let
\left(\widehat{H}_{N}(\sigma)\right)_{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times N}}
be the Gaussian process in (
1.19
).
For every
\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, we consider

(7.13)

\widehat{F}_{N}\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)\\
:=-\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\left(H_{N}^{t,{q}}(\sigma,\alpha)+\sqrt{2\widehat{t}}\widehat{H}_{N}(\sigma)-\widehat{t}N\left|\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha),

where
H^{t,{q}}_{N}(\sigma,\alpha)
is given in (
5.3
).
We denote the associated Gibbs measure by
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,\widehat{t}}\,
.
Recall the functional
\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{t,\widehat{t},q}
defined in (
1.21
), and the overlaps
R_{N}
and
Q_{p}
defined in (
6.10
) and (
6.11
).

Proposition 7.5
.

Suppose that
\left(\widehat{F}_{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges pointwise to some limit
f
along a subsequence
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Then, for each
t\geqslant 0
, the function
f\left(t,\cdot,\cdot\right):\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is Gateaux differentiable (jointly in its two variables) on a subset of
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})
that is dense in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. Moreover, for every
\widehat{t}\geqslant 0
and every point
{q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
of Gateaux differentiability of
f(t,\widehat{t},\cdot)
, the following holds for
{p}=\partial_{q}f\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)
and
{q^{\prime}}={q}+t\nabla\xi\left({p}\right)+2\widehat{t}{p}
:

(1)

{p}=\partial_{q}\psi\left({q^{\prime}}\right)
;

(2)

if
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is the full sequence
(N)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, then
\lim_{N\to\infty}\widehat{F}_{N}\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)=\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{t,\widehat{t},{q}}({q^{\prime}},{p})
;

(3)

if
\widehat{t}>0
and
f(t,\cdot,{q})
is differentiable at
\widehat{t}
, then
\left(R_{N_{k}}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}:\>\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N_{k},\widehat{t}}
converges in law to
\left({p}\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}:\>\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
as
k
tends to infinity.

Proof.

By straightforward modifications, the differentiability follows from Proposition
5.3
, part (
1
) follows from Proposition
7.2
, and part (
2
) follows from Proposition
7.1
.

Hence, we focus on part (
3
).
For convenience, we drop the subsequence
(N_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in the notation, but we understand that we only take limits along the subsequence throughout the proof.
By an adaptation of Proposition
5.4
, we have (along the subsequence)

(7.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left|\partial_{q}\widehat{F}_{N}\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)-\partial_{q}f\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)\right|_{L^{2}}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\lim_{N\to\infty}\partial_{\widehat{t}}\widehat{F}_{N}\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)=\partial_{\widehat{t}}f\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right).

For brevity, we write
{p}_{N}:=\partial_{q}\widehat{F}_{N}\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)
,
{p}:=\partial_{q}f\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)
, and
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}:=\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N,\widehat{t}}\,
.
We have

\displaystyle\partial_{\widehat{t}}\widehat{F}_{N}\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right|^{2}\right\rangle_{N}.

Adapting the proof of Proposition
7.4
, we have that (
7.10
) holds, which implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}-{p}_{N}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|^{2}\right\rangle_{N}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\right|^{2}\right\rangle_{N}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|{p}_{N}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|^{2}\right\rangle_{N}

(7.15)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\widehat{t}}\widehat{F}_{N}\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)-\left|\partial_{q}\widehat{F}_{N}\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)\right|^{2}_{L^{2}}

where in the second equality we used the invariance of the Poisson–Dirichlet cascade.

We can now argue as for (
7.3
) in Proposition
7.2
to conclude. Indeed, since
t
is fixed here, we view
\widehat{F}_{N}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
and
f(t,\cdot,\cdot)
as functions of
(\widehat{t},{q})
. We can substitute
\widehat{t}
and
|\cdot|^{2}
for
t
and
\xi
in Proposition
7.2
to get

(7.16)

\displaystyle\partial_{\widehat{t}}f\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)=\left|\partial_{q}f\left(t,\widehat{t},{q}\right)\right|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Combining (
7.14
), (
7.15
), and (
7.16
), we obtain that (along the subsequence)

(7.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}-{p}_{N}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|^{2}\right\rangle_{N}=0.

The first convergence in (
7.14
) ensures that (along the subsequence)

(7.18)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|{p}_{N}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)-{p}\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\right|^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}=0.

We fix any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any bounded Lipschitz
g:\left(\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times{D}}\times\mathbb{R}\right)^{n^{2}-n}\to\mathbb{R}
.
We write
R^{\square}_{N}:=\left(R_{N}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\leqslant n;\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
and similarly for
Q^{\square}_{{p}_{N}}
and
Q^{\square}_{p}
.
The invariance of the Poisson–Dirichlet cascade implies that
Q_{{p}_{N}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
has the same distribution as
Q_{{p}_{N}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
.
We therefore have that

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(R^{\square}_{N}\right)\right\rangle_{N}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(Q^{\square}_{p}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(R^{\square}_{N}\right)\right\rangle_{N}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(Q^{\square}_{{p}_{N}}\right)\right\rangle_{N}+\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(Q^{\square}_{{p}_{N}}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}-\mathbb{E}\left\langle g\left(Q^{\square}_{p}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|g\|_{\mathrm{Lip}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|R^{\square}_{N}-Q^{\square}_{{p}_{N}}\right|\right\rangle_{N}+\mathbb{E}\left\langle\left|Q^{\square}_{{p}_{N}}-Q^{\square}_{p}\right|\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}\right).

Combining this with (
7.17
) and (
7.18
) therefore completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

The theorem follows from Proposition
7.5
.
∎

7.4.
Stability and high-temperature uniqueness of the critical point

We now turn to a proof of Proposition
1.5
on the uniqueness of the critical point at high temperature.

Proof of Proposition
1.5
.

The fact that
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
has at least one critical point is a consequence of Theorem
1.2
(and could also be proved directly).
For each
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
(q^{\prime}_{i},{p}_{i})\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{2}
be a critical point of
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
.
Inserting (
1.9
) into (
1.10
), we get
{p}_{i}=\partial_{q}\psi(q+t\nabla\xi({p}_{i}))
.
By (
5.23
), we have

\displaystyle\left|{p}_{1}-{p}_{2}\right|_{L^{2}}\leqslant 16t\left|\nabla\xi({p}_{1})-\nabla\xi({p}_{2})\right|_{L^{2}}.

By the local Lipschitzness of
\nabla\xi
, we have

\displaystyle C:=\sup_{a,a^{\prime}\in S^{D}_{+}:\>|a|,|a^{\prime}|\leqslant 1;\;a\neq a^{\prime}}\frac{\left|\nabla\xi(a)-\nabla\xi(a^{\prime})\right|}{|a-a^{\prime}|}<+\infty.

The property in (
5.21
) implies that
|{p}_{i}(s)|\leqslant 1
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
and
s\in[0,1)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\left|{p}_{1}-{p}_{2}\right|_{L^{2}}\leqslant 16Ct\left|{p}_{1}-{p}_{2}\right|_{L^{2}}.

Hence, if
t<t_{c}:=(16C)^{-1}
, we must have
{p}_{1}={p}_{2}
. Since
q^{\prime}_{i}
is determined by (
1.9
), the uniqueness follows.
∎

We close this section with a proof of Proposition
1.6
on the stability of relevant critical points.

Proof of Proposition
1.6
.

We take
(t_{n},q_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
and
(q^{\prime}_{n},p_{n})\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{2}
as in the statement. The fact that
(q^{\prime}_{n},p_{n})
is a critical point of
\mathcal{J}_{t_{n},q_{n}}
(see (
1.9
) and (
1.10
)) means that

(7.19)

p_{n}=\partial_{q}\psi(q^{\prime}_{n})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptq^{\prime}_{n}=q_{n}+t_{n}\nabla\xi(p_{n}).

By (
5.21
) from Corollary
5.2
, we have that
p_{n}\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
. By Lemma
3.4
, the sequence
(p_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is therefore precompact in
L^{2}
. By the second relation in (
7.19
) and the convergence of
(t_{n},q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times L^{2}
, we deduce that the sequence
(q^{\prime}_{n},p_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is precompact. Moreover, if
(p_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in
L^{2}
along a subsequence, then
(q_{n}^{\prime},p_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in
(L^{2})^{2}
along the same subsequence. Recall also from (
1.7
) that

(7.20)

\mathcal{J}_{t_{n},q_{n}}(q^{\prime}_{n},p_{n})=\psi(q^{\prime}_{n})+\left\langle p_{n},q_{n}-q^{\prime}_{n}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}+t_{n}\int_{0}^{1}\xi(p_{n}).

Letting
(q^{\prime},p)\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}^{2}
denote a subsequential limit of
(q^{\prime}_{n},p_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we have that
\psi(q^{\prime}_{n})
converges to
\psi(q^{\prime})
along the subsequence, by the continuity of
\psi
from Corollary
5.2
. The scalar product in (
7.20
) converges to
\left\langle p,q-q^{\prime}\right\rangle_{{L^{2}}}
along the subsequence, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. Finally, the integral
\int_{0}^{1}\xi(p_{n})
converges to
\int_{0}^{1}\xi(p)
along the subsequence, because
\xi
is continuous and
p_{n}\in L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
. So we conclude that as
n
tends to infinity along the subsequence, we have that
\mathcal{J}_{t_{n},q_{n}}(q^{\prime}_{n},p_{n})
converges to
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}(q^{\prime},p)
. Using also the uniform Lipschitz continuity of
\overline{F}_{N}
from Proposition
3.1
, we obtain (
1.22
). Using the continuity of
\partial_{q}\psi
from Corollary
5.2
and (
7.19
), we also verify that
(q^{\prime},p)
is a critical point of
\mathcal{J}_{t,q}
, and thus the proof is complete.
∎

8.
Further results in the convex case

In this section, we give a proof of Theorem
1.1
. We also derive a number of alternative representations of the variational problem describing the limit free energy, and explain how to remove the compensating term
-Nt\xi(\sigma\sigma^{\intercal})/N
appearing in (
1.5
). We also show that the limit free energy is Gateaux differentiable everywhere in
(0,\infty)\times(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})
, and use this to show that it satisfies the Hamilton–Jacobi equation (
1.23
) at every point in this set. We collect all results into a final statement that also describes the convergence of the conditional
\sigma
-overlaps for every
(t,q)\in(0,\infty)\times(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})
.

8.1.
Parisi formula when
\xi
is convex on
S^{D}_{+}

When
\xi
is convex over the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
, an interpolation argument allows one to establish a lower bound on any subsequential limit of
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)

[
49
,
13
]
. When
{D}=1
, one can generalize this bound to the case when
\xi
is only assumed to be convex over
S^{1}_{+}=\mathbb{R}_{+}
using the Talagrand positivity principle (see
[
77
, Theorem 3.4]
). If
{D}>1
and
\xi
only depends on the
D
diagonal entries of its argument, then one may as well think of
\xi
as being defined on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, and one can simplify the setup and only consider paths that take values in
\mathbb{R}^{D}_{+}
instead of
S^{D}_{+}
. In this context, one can still apply the scalar positivity principle to get the Parisi formula under the assumption that
\xi
is convex over
\mathbb{R}^{D}_{+}
, see
[
17
, Section 3.2]
.

For general vector spin glass models, to the best of our knowledge, existing results all require
\xi
to be convex on
\mathbb{R}^{{D}\times{D}}
. Here we obtain the lower bound on the limit free energy by combining the results of
[
71
]
and
[
29
]
. We derive the converse bound from the results of this paper, but as already mentioned, these results are much more precise than necessary in this case.

Recall the definitions of
\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}
in (
6.15
) and of
\mathcal{K}
in (
6.33
).

Proposition 8.1

(Parisi formula for the enriched model)
.

If
\xi
is convex on
S^{D}_{+}
, then for every
t\geqslant 0
and
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, we have

(8.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})=\sup_{{p}\in\mathcal{Q}:\>\exists a\in\mathcal{K},\,p\leqslant a}\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})=\sup_{{p}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p}).

Proof.

We fix any
(t,q)
.
Under the stated convexity assumption, the lower bound in
[
71
, Theorem 1.1]
expressed in terms of the solution to (
1.8
) is proved in
[
29
, Corollary 4.14]
to admit a representation by the Hopf–Lax formula:

(8.2)

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\geqslant\sup_{{q^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\inf_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\left\{\psi({q}+{q^{\prime}})-\left\langle q^{\prime},p^{\prime}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}+t\int_{0}^{1}\xi({p^{\prime}}(s))\mathrm{d}s\right\}.

On the other hand, by Corollary
6.11
, we can find
{p}\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
and
a\in\mathcal{K}
satisfying
a\geqslant p
such that

(8.3)

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,{q})\leqslant\mathscr{P}_{t,{q}}({p})=\psi({q}+t\nabla\xi({p}))-t\int_{0}^{1}\theta({p}(s))\mathrm{d}s,

where we recall that
\theta
is defined in (
6.12
).
To compare these two formulas, we need to show that

(8.4)

\displaystyle\theta(b)=\xi^{*}(\nabla\xi(b)),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall b\in S^{D}_{+},

with
\xi^{*}
as in (
1.13
).
Fix any
b\in S^{D}_{+}
. It is clear from their definitions that
\theta(b)\leqslant\xi^{*}(\nabla\xi(b))
. For any
b^{\prime}\in S^{D}_{+}
, the convexity of
\xi
on
S^{D}_{+}
implies that for every
\lambda\in[0,1]
,

\lambda\xi(b^{\prime})+(1-\lambda)\xi(b)\geqslant\xi(\lambda b^{\prime}+(1-\lambda)b).

Rearranging terms and sending
\lambda
to zero, we obtain that

\xi(b^{\prime})-\xi(b)\geqslant(b^{\prime}-b)\cdot\nabla\xi(b),

and thus

b\cdot\nabla\xi(b)-\xi(b)\geqslant b^{\prime}\cdot\nabla\xi(b)-\xi(b^{\prime}).

Taking the supremum over
b^{\prime}\in S^{D}_{+}
, we get
\theta(b)\geqslant\xi^{*}(\nabla\xi(b))
, and thus complete the argument for (
8.4
).

Using (
8.4
), we have

\int_{0}^{1}\theta({p}(s))\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{1}\xi^{*}(\nabla\xi({p}(s)))\mathrm{d}s\geqslant\sup_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\left\{\left\langle\nabla\xi(p),p^{\prime}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}-\int_{0}^{1}\xi(p^{\prime}(s))\mathrm{d}s\right\}\\
\geqslant\left\langle\nabla\xi(p),p\right\rangle_{L^{2}}-\int_{0}^{1}\xi(p(s))\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{1}\theta({p}(s))\mathrm{d}s,

which implies

(8.5)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\theta({p}(s))\mathrm{d}s=\sup_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\left\{\left\langle\nabla\xi(p),p^{\prime}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}-\int_{0}^{1}\xi(p^{\prime}(s))\mathrm{d}s\right\}.

Applying this to (
8.2
), we get

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)

\displaystyle\geqslant\inf_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\left\{\psi({q}+t\nabla\xi(p))-t\left\langle\nabla\xi(p),p^{\prime}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}+t\int_{0}^{1}\xi({p^{\prime}}(s))\mathrm{d}s\right\}

\displaystyle\geqslant\psi({q}+t\nabla\xi(p))-t\int_{0}^{1}\theta({p}(s))\mathrm{d}s,

which along with (
8.3
) yields the first equality in (
8.1
). The second equality can be deduced similarly.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Fix any
(t,q)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
.
Applying (
8.5
) to (
8.3
) and comparing it with (
8.2
), we get

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)

\displaystyle=\sup_{{q^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\inf_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\left\{\psi({q}+{q^{\prime}})-\left\langle q^{\prime},p^{\prime}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}+t\int_{0}^{1}\xi({p^{\prime}}(s))\mathrm{d}s\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{q^{\prime}\in q+\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\inf_{p\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\mathcal{J}_{t,q}(q^{\prime},p),

where the last equality follows from the definition of
\mathcal{J}_{t,{q}}(q^{\prime},{p})
in (
1.7
).
Hence, we have verified the first identity in (
1.12
).
It is proved in
[
29
, Theorem 4.6]
that the unique viscosity solution
f
to (
1.8
), defined in
[
29
, Definition 4.2]
, admits the Hopf–Lax representation under the assumption that
\xi
is convex on
S^{D}_{+}
. The Hopf–Lax formula for
f
evaluated at
(t,q)
has the same expression as the one on the first line of the above display. Since this identity holds at every
(t,q)
, we conclude that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}=f
pointwise everywhere on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
.
∎

In the above proof, to show that
\left(\overline{F}_{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to the solution
f
, we directly match the variational formula for
\lim_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}
with the Hopf–Lax representation of
f
. We mention another approach via the comparison principle for viscosity solutions. Let
p
be as given in (
8.3
) and consider the function
g:(t^{\prime},q^{\prime})\mapsto\mathscr{P}_{t^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(p)
. Using the convexity of
\xi
, one can verify that
g
is a subsolution of (
1.8
). The comparison principle (e.g.
[
29
, Proposition 3.8 and Remark 4.7]
) implies that
g\leqslant f
everywhere. This along with (
8.3
) yields
\limsup\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\leqslant g(t,q)\leqslant f(t,q)
. Since
(t,q)
was arbitrary, we have
\limsup\overline{F}_{N}\leqslant f
everywhere on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
. The matching lower bound is provided by
[
71
, Theorem 1.1]
. This argument can be seen in
[
25
]
for
D=1
.

In the corollary below, we verify the version of the Hopf–Lax formula appearing in (
1.14
).

Corollary 8.2

(Alternative form of Hopf–Lax formula)
.

If
\xi
is convex on
S^{D}_{+}
, then for every
t\geqslant 0
and
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
, we have

(8.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)=\sup_{q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\left\{\psi(q+q^{\prime})-t\int_{0}^{1}\xi^{*}\left(\frac{q^{\prime}}{t}\right)\right\}.

Proof.

We fix any
(t,q)
and denote the variational formula on the right-hand side of (
8.6
) by
\mathrm{RHS}
.
Using (
8.4
) in (
8.3
), we get

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\leqslant\mathrm{RHS}.

To show the converse bound, we recall the definition of
\xi^{*}
in (
1.13
) to see that, for every
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\left\{\frac{1}{t}\left\langle q^{\prime},p^{\prime}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}-\int_{0}^{1}\xi(p^{\prime}(s))\mathrm{d}s\right\}\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\xi^{*}\left(\frac{q^{\prime}(s)}{t}\right)\mathrm{d}s.

Using this in (
8.2
), we get
\liminf_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)\geqslant\mathrm{RHS}
. This gives the announced identity at
(t,q)
.
∎

We can extract from Proposition
8.1
a result that takes a form that is more common in the literature. For every
\pi\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
and
x\in S^{D}
, we set

(8.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathscr{P}(\pi,x):=\mathbb{E}\log\iint\exp\left(w^{\nabla\xi\circ\pi}(\alpha)\cdot\tau-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\xi\circ\pi(1)\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal}+x\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{1}(\sigma)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}(\alpha)\\
+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\theta\left(\nabla\xi\circ\pi(s)\right)\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

This resembles the classic Parisi functional for the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model.

Corollary 8.3

(Parisi formula for free energy with correction)
.

If
\xi
is convex on
S^{D}_{+}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\left(H_{N}(\sigma)-\frac{N}{2}\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)=\inf_{\pi\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\mathscr{P}(\pi,0).

Proof.

Recall the expression of
\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
from (
5.2
) and the random Hamiltonian
H_{N}(\sigma)
with covariance (
1.1
).
Setting
t=\frac{1}{2}
and
q=0
, we get

\displaystyle-\overline{F}_{N}\left(\frac{1}{2},0\right)=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\left(H_{N}(\sigma)-\frac{N}{2}\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma).

Recalling the definition of
\mathscr{P}_{t,q}
in (
6.15
), we have
-\mathscr{P}_{\frac{1}{2},0}(p)=\mathscr{P}(p,0)
for every
p\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, where we also used
\sqrt{2}w^{\frac{1}{2}q^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}w^{q^{\prime}}
for
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
, which is clear from (
4.4
).
Now, the announced identity follows from (
8.1
) in Proposition
8.1
evaluated at
t=\frac{1}{2}
and
q=0
.
∎

We can further remove the correction term
-\frac{N}{2}\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)
and get the following.

Proposition 8.4

(Parisi formula)
.

If
\xi
is convex on
S^{D}_{+}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\left(H_{N}(\sigma)\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in S^{D}_{+}}\inf_{\pi\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\left\{\mathscr{P}(\pi,y)-\frac{1}{2}\xi^{*}(2y)\right\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{z\in S^{D}_{+}}\inf_{y\in S^{D}_{+},\ \pi\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}\left\{\mathscr{P}(\pi,y)-y\cdot z+\frac{1}{2}\xi(z)\right\}.

Proof.

The method is the same as in
[
72
, Section 5]
and
[
27
, Section 5]
. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times S^{D}
, we consider

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{N}(t,x):=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp\left(H_{N}(\sigma)-\frac{N}{2}\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)+tN\xi\left(\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}}{N}\right)+x\cdot\sigma\sigma^{\intercal}\right)\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma).

It is shown in
[
27
, Proposition 5.2]
that the convexity of
\xi
over
S^{D}_{+}
implies that the limit of
(\mathcal{F}_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
in the local uniform topology is a viscosity solution
f
of
\partial_{t}f-\xi(\nabla_{x}f)=0
on
(0,\infty)\times S^{D}
. The notion of viscosity solutions is defined in
[
27
, Section 5]
.
Recall that
S^{D}_{++}
is the set of
{D}
-by-
{D}
positive definite matrices, which is the interior of
S^{D}_{+}
.
Since
S^{D}_{++}
is an open subset of
S^{D}
, we immediately have that
f
is a viscosity solution of

(8.8)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f-\xi(\nabla_{x}f)=0\hskip 20.00003pt\text{on}\hskip 10.00002pt(0,\infty)\times S^{D}_{++}.

We want to use variational formulas for the solution of (
8.8
) to conclude. For this, we need to first identify the initial condition of
f
and verify more properties.

Applying Corollary
8.3
with
e^{x\cdot\tau\tau^{\intercal}}\mathrm{d}P_{1}(\tau)
substituted for
P_{1}
therein, we have, for every
x\in S^{D}
,

(8.9)

\displaystyle f(0,x)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{N}(0,x)=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi}\mathscr{P}(\pi,x).

It is verified in
[
27
, Lemma 5.1]
that
\mathcal{F}_{N}
is Lipschitz uniformly in
N
and convex, and satisfies that
\mathcal{F}_{N}(t,x)\leqslant\mathcal{F}_{N}(t,x^{\prime})
whenever
x\geqslant x^{\prime}
. Passing to the limit, we have that
f
is Lipschitz and convex and that
f(t,\cdot)
is increasing for every
t\geqslant 0
. These properties along with the well-posedness
[
27
, Proposition 5.3]
of (
8.8
) implies that
f
is the viscosity solution of (
8.8
) with the above initial condition, which is unique in the class of Lipschitz functions that are increasing for each fixed
t
.

Since
\xi
is convex on
S^{D}_{+}
and
f(0,\cdot)
is also convex, we can represent
f
by the Hopf–Lax formula and by the Hopf formula described in
[
27
, Proposition 5.3]
: for every
t>0
and
x\in S^{D}_{+}
,

(8.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(t,x)&=\sup_{y\in S^{D}_{+}}\left\{f(0,x+y)-t\xi^{*}\left(\frac{y}{t}\right)\right\}\\
&=\sup_{z\in S^{D}_{+}}\inf_{y\in S^{D}_{+}}\left\{f(0,y)+(x-y)\cdot z+t\xi(z)\right\}.\end{split}

Notice that, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{N}\left(\frac{1}{2},0\right)=\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}\log\int\exp(H_{N}(\sigma))\mathrm{d}P_{N}(\sigma).

Hence, setting
t=\frac{1}{2}
and
x=0
in (
8.10
), inserting (
8.9
) to (
8.10
), and using the convergence of
\mathcal{F}_{N}
to
f
, we can obtain the announced identities.
∎

Remark 8.5
.

In the spin glass literature, sometimes it is preferred to consider left-continuous paths in
\Pi
defined in (
4.21
) instead of the right-continuous ones in
\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}
.
We explained in Remark
4.9
that, for
\pi\in\Pi
, the Gaussian process
w^{\pi}
exists and satisfies all the properties in Section
4
. Hence,
\mathscr{P}(\pi,x)
is defined in the same way as in (
8.7
) for
\pi\in\Pi
and
x\in S^{D}
. By the
L^{1}
-Lipschitzness of
\psi
in Corollary
5.2
, the value of
\mathscr{P}(\pi,x)
does not change if we replace
\pi
by its left-continuous version.
Therefore,
\inf_{\pi\in\mathcal{Q}_{\infty}}
in Corollary
8.3
and Proposition
8.4
can be replaced by
\inf_{\pi\in\Pi}
.
Due to the first equality in Proposition
8.1
, we can further refine
\inf_{\pi\in\Pi}
therein to
\inf_{\pi\in\Pi:\>\exists a\in\mathcal{K},\,\pi\leqslant a}
for
\mathcal{K}
defined in (
6.33
).

8.2.
Differentiability of the Parisi formula

We show that the limit free energy is differentiable. We use a similar argument as that for the so-called generic models, which is presented, for instance, in
[
77
, Section 3.7]
.

Proposition 8.6
.

Suppose that
\xi
is convex on
S^{D}_{+}
, and let
f
be the limit of
\left(\overline{F}_{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
as identified in Proposition
8.1
.

•

For each
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the function
f(t,\cdot)
is Gateaux differentiable everywhere on
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
.

•

The function
f
is Gateaux differentiable everywhere on
(0,\infty)\times(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})
.

Proof.

We show the first property; the second one can be verified in an analogous way.
Fix any
(t,{q})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})
.
We write
f({q^{\prime}}):=f(t,{q^{\prime}})
and
\mathscr{P}_{{q^{\prime}}}(\pi):=\mathscr{P}_{t,{q^{\prime}}}(\pi)
.
By Proposition
8.1
, we can write, for every
q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
,

(8.11)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q^{\prime})=f(q^{\prime})=\sup_{\pi\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}}\mathscr{P}_{q^{\prime}}(\pi).

This identity at
q
together with (
8.3
) implies that there is
\pi\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
such that

(8.12)

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{q}(\pi)=f(q).

Step 1.

We show that
f
is differentiable at
q
along nice directions.
Let
\kappa\in C^{\infty}([0,1];S^{D})
be such that
\kappa(0)=0
. Due to
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}
, by the same argument in Step 1 of the proof of Proposition
5.4
, there are
c,\delta>0
such that
{q}+r\kappa\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow,c}\cap L^{\infty}
for all
r\in[-\delta,\delta]
(we recall that
\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow,c}
is defined in (
3.32
)).
By Proposition
5.3
, we have that the function
r\mapsto f(q+r\kappa)
is semi-concave on
[-\delta,\delta]
. We want to show that this function is differentiable at
r=0
. For this, we need to introduce the notion of superdifferential (e.g.
[
24
, Definition 3.1.1]
). For a function
g:I\to\mathbb{R}
defined on an open interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, the
superdifferential
of
g
at
r\in I
is the set of real numbers
a
satisfying

\displaystyle\limsup_{s\to r}\frac{g(s)-g(r)-a(s-r)}{|s-r|}\leqslant 0.

It is well-known (e.g.
[
24
, Proposition 3.3.4 (c-d)]
, or
[
37
, Theorem 2.13]
for a variant with convex functions) that for a semi-concave function
g:I\to\mathbb{R}
and
r\in I
, the superdifferential at
r
is not empty, and if it contains exactly one point
a\in\mathbb{R}
, then
g
is differentiable at
r
with
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}g(r)=a
.
So we let
a\in\mathbb{R}
be any element of the superdifferential of the mapping
r\mapsto f(q+r\kappa)
at
r=0
, and proceed to identify it uniquely.
In the following, we denote by
C
a constant that depends only on
(t,{q})
,
c
, and
\kappa
, and may vary from instance to instance.
By Proposition
5.3
, we have

(8.13)

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)f(q)+\lambda f(q+r\kappa)-f({q}+\lambda r\kappa)\leqslant C\lambda(1-\lambda)r^{2}

uniformly in
\lambda\in[0,1]
and
r
. Dividing both sides by
\lambda
, sending
\lambda
to zero, and using the definition of the superdifferential, we get

\displaystyle f(q+r\kappa)-f(q)-ar\leqslant Cr^{2}.

Dividing both sides by
r>0
and using (
8.11
) and (
8.12
), we get

\displaystyle a\geqslant\frac{f(q+r\kappa)-f(q)}{r}-Cr\geqslant\frac{\mathscr{P}_{q+r\kappa}(\pi)-\mathscr{P}_{q}(\pi)}{r}-Cr.

Repeating it for
-r<0
, we have

\displaystyle a\leqslant\frac{f(q)-f(q-r\kappa)}{r}+Cr\leqslant\frac{\mathscr{P}_{q}(\pi)-\mathscr{P}_{q-r\kappa}(\pi)}{r}+Cr.

Recall the definition of
\mathscr{P}_{t,q}
in (
6.15
).
By the smoothness of
\psi
in
Corollary
5.2
, and the
L^{\infty}
bound on
\pi
, there is
C
such that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\mathscr{P}_{q+r\kappa}(\pi)-\mathscr{P}_{q}(\pi)}{r}-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathscr{P}_{q+r\kappa}(\pi)\Big|_{r=0}\right|\leqslant Cr,

uniformly in
r\in[-\delta,\delta]
.
Combining the above three displays, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathscr{P}_{q+r\kappa}(\pi)\Big|_{r=0}-Cr\leqslant a\leqslant\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathscr{P}_{q+r\kappa}(\pi)\Big|_{r=0}+Cr

Now, sending
r
to zero, this identifies
a
uniquely, and thus
r\mapsto f(q+r\kappa)
is differentiable at
r=0
.

Step 2.

We want to identify the derivative of
f
at
q
.
We use an argument similar to that in the proof of Proposition
7.1
.
Let
(N^{\pm}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(x^{\pm}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, and
{p}_{\pm}
be given by Corollary
6.11
.
We write
\overline{F}_{k}^{\pm}=\overline{F}^{x^{\pm}_{k}}_{N^{\pm}_{k}}
.
As in the proof of Proposition
7.1
, we can compute, for every
\kappa\in C([0,1];S^{D})
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\overline{F}^{\pm}_{k}(t,{q}+r\kappa)\Big|_{r=0}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right)\cdot\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N^{\pm}_{k}}\right\rangle_{N^{\pm}_{k},x^{\pm}_{k}}.

By Lemma
6.4
, we also have that
\overline{F}_{k}^{\pm}
converges pointwise to
f
. Similarly to Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
5.4
, the semi-concavity (
8.13
) implies the convergence of
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\overline{F}^{\pm}_{k}(t,{q}+r\kappa)\big|_{r=0}
to
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}f(q+r\kappa)\big|_{r=0}
.
This convergence along with the above display and Corollary
6.11
(
1
) implies, for every
\kappa\in C([0,1];S^{D})
,

(8.14)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}f(q+r\kappa)\Big|_{r=0}=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\kappa\left(R^{1,2}_{\alpha}\right)\cdot{p}_{\pm}\left(R^{1,2}_{\alpha}\right)\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}=\left\langle\kappa,{p}_{\pm}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Since
C([0,1];S^{D})
is dense in
{L^{2}}
, we must have
{p}_{\pm}={p}
for some
{p}\in{L^{2}}
.

Step 3.
We are now ready to show that
f
is Gateaux differentiable at
q
.
By Proposition
5.3
and (
8.11
), we have that
f:\mathcal{Q}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous. Therefore, by the first step of the proof of Proposition
2.7
, the function
f
admits a Lipschitz extension
\overline{f}:{L^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}
. We show that
\overline{f}
is Gateaux differentiable at
{q}
.
Fix any
{q^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
and let
(\kappa_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
C^{\infty}([0,1];S^{D})
with
\kappa_{n}(0)=0
that converges to
{q^{\prime}}
in
{L^{2}}
.
We have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\overline{f}({q}+r{q^{\prime}})-\overline{f}(q)}{r}-\left\langle{p},{q^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|\leqslant\left|\frac{\overline{f}({q}+r\kappa_{n})-\overline{f}(q)}{r}-\left\langle{p},\kappa_{n}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}\right|+C_{0}\left|\kappa_{n}-{q^{\prime}}\right|_{L^{2}},

where
C_{0}:=\left\|\overline{f}\right\|_{\mathrm{Lip}}+|{p}|_{L^{2}}
.
By (
8.14
), sending
r
to zero and then
n
to infinity in the above display, we get
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\overline{f}({q}+r{q^{\prime}})\Big|_{r=0}=\left\langle{p},{q^{\prime}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}}
. Hence, we can conclude that
\overline{f}
is Gateaux differentiable at
{q}
and its derivative is equal to
{p}
.

Since admissible directions at
{q}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2}
for
f
includes
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
spans
{L^{2}}
, we can deduce from Definition
2.2
that
f
is Gateaux differentiable at
{q}
with derivative
{p}
.
This verifies the first announced property.
∎

8.3.
Summary

In the convex case, we summarize our results below.

Corollary 8.7
.

Suppose that
\xi
is convex on
S^{D}_{+}
.
Then, the sequence
\left(\overline{F}_{N}\right)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges pointwise to some limit
f
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{Q}_{2}
.
At every
(t,q)\in(0,\infty)\times(\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty})
, the function
f
is Gateaux differentiable (jointly in its two variables) and satisfies

(8.15)

\displaystyle\partial f_{t}(t,q)-\int_{0}^{1}\xi\left(\partial_{q}f(t,q)\right)=0.

For every
t\in[0,\infty)
,
f(t,\cdot)
is Gateaux differentiable at every
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{\uparrow}\cap L^{\infty}
and the following holds for
p=\partial_{q}f(t,q)
and
p_{N}=\partial_{q}\overline{F}_{N}(t,q)
:

(1)

p,\,p_{N}\in\mathcal{Q}\cap L^{\infty}_{\leqslant 1}
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
(p_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
p
in
L^{r}
for every
r\in[1,\infty)
as
N
tends to infinity;

(2)

f(t,q)=\mathscr{P}_{t,q}(p)
and
p=\partial_{q}\psi(q+t\nabla\xi(p))
;

(3)

p_{N}(\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}\left\langle\frac{\sigma\sigma^{\prime\intercal}}{N}\,|\,\alpha\wedge\alpha^{\prime}\right\rangle_{N}
almost surely under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
for every
N
, and the overlap array
\left(p_{N}\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}:\>\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
converges in law to
\left(p\left(\alpha^{\ell}\wedge\alpha^{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\right)_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}:\>\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}
under
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{R}}
as
N
tends to infinity.

In part (
2
),
\mathscr{P}_{t,q}
is given in (
6.15
).
In part (
3
), the conditional expectation is taken with respect to the measure
\mathbb{E}\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
for
\left\langle\cdot\right\rangle_{N}
given in (
5.4
).

Proof of Corollary
8.7
.

The existence of
f
is given by Proposition
8.1
. The differentiability of
f
follows from Proposition
8.6
. Proposition
7.2
yields (
8.15
). Part (
1
) is a consequence of (
5.6
) in Proposition
5.1
and Proposition
5.4
.
Part (
2
) follows from Propositions
7.1
and
7.2
. Lastly, part (
3
) is a consequence of Proposition
7.4
and (
7.10
) in its proof.
∎
